Similarly, we will say that
\widetilde{y}
is in a certain alcove if its projection
y
is, and so on.

2.3.3.
Valuations of the root datum and parahoric subgroups

In
[
KP23
, Chapter 6]
, the points of the (reduced) apartment are defined as valuations of the root datum.
4
4

4

More precisely, the points of the apartment are defined as
equipollence classes
of valuations of the root datum.
Since the role of equipollence classes is to have a definition which doesn’t depend on a choice of pinning, we simply ignore this, and note that
the valuations of root datum we discuss are all with respect to the chosen Chevalley valuation
o
.

We will now give a brief explanation of how a point
\widetilde{y}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
gives rise to a valuation of the root datum,
and how this is used to describe parahoric subgroups of
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\widehat{G}_{j}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))
.
For simplicity, we fix
j\in\mathcal{J}
and focus on a single factor.

Let
\widetilde{y}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T}_{j},\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)
, and let
y\in\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{T}_{j},\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)

denote the projection of
\widetilde{y}
via (
7
).
The “valuation of the root datum” (with respect to
o_{j}
) described by
\widetilde{y}
only depends on
y
, and it is given as follows.
For
a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})
a root and
U_{a}\hookrightarrow\widehat{G}
the corresponding root group,
the pinning of
\widehat{G}
provides an isomorphism
u_{a}:\mathbb{G}_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{a}
, and we can
define

\displaystyle\varphi_{o,a}:U_{a}\left(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{a}^{-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{val}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{1}{e}\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle\text{where }\operatorname{val}\text{ is the discrete valuation,}

\displaystyle\varphi_{y,a}(u):=\varphi_{o,a}(u)+\langle a,y-o\rangle.

The collection
\varphi_{y}=\{\varphi_{y,a}\}_{a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})}
is called a
valuation of the root datum
.

Remark 2.14
.

Note that the notation “
\varphi
” here has nothing to do with Frobenius, but we use it to be consistent with
[
KP23
, Chapter 6]
.
We only use
\varphi
to denote valuations of the root datum in this subsection; elsewhere in this work
\varphi
refers to a Frobenius morphism.

We can use
\varphi_{y}
to describe subgroups of the root groups. Namely, for
a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})
let

(8)

U_{a,y}:=\varphi_{y,a}^{-1}\left([0,\infty]\right)=u_{a}\left(u^{\lceil-e\langle a,y-o\rangle\rceil}\right)\subset U_{a}\left(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right).

Here, the second equality depends on the fact that
u
has valuation
1/e
.
The parahoric subgroup

P_{y}:=\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))_{y}^{0}\subset\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))

attached to
y
, as defined in
[
KP23
, Definition 7.3.3]
, is the subgroup generated by the subgroups
\widehat{T}(\mathbb{F}_{j}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
and
U_{a,x}
,
a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})
.

More generally, we define for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
(and even more generally,
r\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
with
\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
as in
[
KP23
, Section 1.6]
)

U_{a,y,r}:=\varphi_{y,a}^{-1}\left([r,\infty]\right)=u_{a}\left(u^{\lceil-e\langle a,y-o\rangle+re\rceil}\right)\subset U_{a}\left(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right).

If
r\geq 0
we can also define
\mathbb{G}_{m,r}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))=\left(u^{\lceil er\rceil}\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{\times}
, and by taking products, we obtain for the split torus
\widehat{T}
a subgroup
\widehat{T}_{r}\subset\widehat{T}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))
.
We note that
\widehat{T}_{r}
also admits a more “canonical” description in terms of a valuation morphism
\operatorname{val}:\widehat{T}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))\to\frac{1}{e}\mathbb{Z}

[
KP23
, Section 7.2]
.
5
5

5

In the first edition of
[
KP23
]
, there is a sign error in the definition of this valuation morphism in
[
KP23
, Section 2.5.b]
, as pointed out in the errata available on Kaletha’s homepage.

Given
r\geq 0
, the subgroup
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))_{y,r}^{0}\subset\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))
is by definition generated by
U_{a,y,r}
and
\widehat{T}_{r}
.

Even more generally, let
f:\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})\to\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
denote a concave function in the sense of
[
KP23
, Definition 7.3.1]
, where
\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})=\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})\cup\{0\}
.
Then the subgroup
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))_{y,f}^{0}
of
[
KP23
, Definition 7.3.3]
is by definition generated by
\widehat{T}_{f(0)}
and
U_{a,y,f(a)}
,
a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})
.
When
f=r
is constant we recover the groups
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))_{y,r}^{0}
and when
f=0
we recover the parahoric subgroups
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))_{y,0}^{0}
.

As shown in
[
KP23
, Theorem 8.5.2]
, there exists for
y
and
f
a concave function as above a smooth group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{y,f}
over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}_{j}\llbracket u\rrbracket
with connected fibers, whose generic fiber is
\widehat{G}
and where the subgroup
\mathcal{G}_{y,f}(\mathbb{F}_{j}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\subset\mathcal{G}_{y,f}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))=\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))
equals
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))_{y,f}^{0}
.

Example 2.15
.

Suppose
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{3}
,
\widehat{T}\subset\widehat{G}
is the diagonal torus,
and we identify
X_{*}(\widehat{T})=\mathbb{Z}^{3}
in the usual way.
Let
\lambda=-\frac{1}{3}(1,0,-1)
and
y=u^{\lambda}\cdot o=o+\frac{1}{3e}(1,0,-1)
.
Then the parahoric subgroup
P_{y}=\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))_{y}^{0}\subset\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!))
is generated by
\widehat{T}(\mathbb{F}_{j}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
and
U_{a,y}
,
a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})
.
The positive roots are
\alpha_{12}=(1,-1,0)
,
\alpha_{23}=(0,1,-1)
, and
\alpha_{13}=(1,0,-1)
.
Note that
\langle\alpha_{12},y-o\rangle=\langle\alpha_{23},y-o\rangle=1/3e
and
\langle\alpha_{13},y-o\rangle=2/3e
, so by (
8
) we can see that

\displaystyle U_{\alpha_{12}}=u_{\alpha_{12}}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)),

\displaystyle U_{-\alpha_{12}}=u_{-\alpha_{12}}(u\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)),

\displaystyle U_{\alpha_{23}}=u_{\alpha_{23}}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)),

\displaystyle U_{-\alpha_{23}}=u_{-\alpha_{23}}(u\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)),

\displaystyle U_{\alpha_{13}}=u_{\alpha_{13}}(\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)),

\displaystyle U_{-\alpha_{13}}=u_{-\alpha_{13}}(u\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)).

To summarize, we have

P_{y}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)^{\times}&\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)&\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\\
u\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)&\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)^{\times}&\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\\
u\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)&u\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)&\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)^{\times}\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

2.3.4.
Tamely ramified descent

Assume that the group
\Gamma
acts on
\widehat{G}
via pinned automorphisms, which in particular restrict to an action on
\widehat{T}
.
Then
\Gamma
acts by “conjugation” on
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathbb{R}}=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}X_{*}(\widehat{T}_{j})_{\mathbb{R}}
, meaning that if
x=(x_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
is an element, then

(9)

\theta(x)_{j}=\theta(x_{\theta^{-1}j}),

where
\theta(y)
for
y\in X_{*}(\widehat{T}_{j})_{\mathbb{R}}
is the action via pinned automorphisms.
We let
\Gamma
act on
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
via (
6
).

For any
n\in\widehat{N}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
and
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
, we have

\theta(n\cdot x)={{}^{\theta}n}\cdot\theta(x)\,\,\,\,\,\text{ for }\theta\in\Gamma.

Indeed, this shows why the above
\Gamma
-action on the apartment is the “correct” one.

Recall that the torus
\widehat{T}
descends to a torus
T^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)}
of
G^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)}
over
\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)
by étale descent.
It follows from tamely ramified descent for Bruhat–Tits buildings
[
KP23
, Chapter 12]
that we have an identification

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{\Gamma}.

Let us briefly explain why this is true.
By tamely ramified descent as in loc. cit., we can identify
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{I}
,
using the fact that
I
acts diagonally.
Then we can perform unramified descent in two steps, first perform “diagonal” unramified descent
[
KP23
, Chapter 9]
with respect to the stabilizer group
\Gamma_{0,\mathcal{J}}
,
and finally take fixed points with respect to the quotient
\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}
which permutes the factors (in a possibly twisted way).

Remark 2.16
.

Even when
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, the identification
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{I}=\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)

typically does not identify the simplicial structures (as one typically
defines
the simplicial structure on the
I
-fixed points so that the second equality identifies the simplicial structures).
If we consider the points of the apartment as being valuations of root data, the walls are determined by where there are jumps in the filtration of a root subgroup.
But since the valuation of
u
is
1/e
and the valuation of
v
is
1
, these jumps occur at different stages.
See
Fig.

1
on page
1
for an illustration of what this might look like.

o
o+(1,0,0)
o+(1,1,0)

Figure 1.

A snapshot of the apartment
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{I}
, when
\widehat{T}\subset\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{3}
is the diagonal torus and
e=6
.
The small triangles bounded by think gray lines are the alcoves of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!)\right)
, whereas the large triangles bounded by thick black lines are the alcoves of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
.

2.3.5.
The building

The enlarged building
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
is the quotient of
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))\times\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)

by the equivalence relation
(g,x)\sim(g^{\prime},x^{\prime})
if there exists
n\in\widehat{N}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
such that
x^{\prime}=n\cdot x
and
g^{\prime}=ngn^{-1}
.
This will satisfy tamely ramified descent for the same reason as above.

One can also define the (reduced) building
\mathcal{B}\left(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
, and as in the case of the apartment we have a canonical splitting

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)=\mathcal{B}\left(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)\oplus\prod_{j\in J}X_{*}(\widehat{A}_{j})_{\mathbb{R}},

similarly to (
7
).
Abusively we will use the simplicial structure of the building to talk about the enlarged building, so for example we will call a point
\widetilde{y}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)

a vertex if its projection
y\in\mathcal{B}\left(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
is a vertex.

2.4.
Various Frobenius endomorphisms

We warn the reader that the notation
\varphi
will be severely overloaded.
First of all we will use
\varphi
to denote the usual (arithmetic,
x\mapsto x^{p}
modulo
p
) Frobenius automorphism of
\mathbb{F}_{p^{k}}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{k}}
, and
\mathbb{Q}_{p^{k}}
.
For any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
we have the Frobenius endomorphism
\varphi:\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)[u]\to\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)[u]
,
which acts as the usual on
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and
u\mapsto u^{p}
.
We similarly define endomorphisms of
\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)[v]
,
R[v]
,
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
,
\mathfrak{S}_{R}
,
\mathcal{E}_{L,R}
,
\mathcal{E}_{R}
,
E^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)
,
\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)
, and we denote all of them by
\varphi
.
There are induced endomorphisms of the schemes
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}
,
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}
, and
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}
, which we also denote by
\varphi
.

Let
H
be a group scheme defined over
\mathcal{O}
(we will apply this to
H=\widehat{G}
).
Let
\widetilde{H}:=H\times_{\mathcal{O}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
Then we have an endomorphism
\varphi=1\otimes\varphi:H\times_{\mathcal{O}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}\to H\times_{\mathcal{O}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
which corresponds to an identification
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{H}\cong\widetilde{H}
.

Given an affine
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
scheme
\operatorname{Spec}A
, we also let
\varphi
denote the effect of pulling back along
\varphi
, i. e.

\varphi:\widetilde{H}(A)\to\varphi^{*}\widetilde{H}\left(\varphi^{*}A\right).

Typically,
A
is equipped with its own Frobenius endomorphism, giving a natural map
A\to\varphi^{*}A
.
(For example, we could take
A=\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
.)
In this case, we also identify
\varphi
with the composite

\varphi:\widetilde{H}(A)\to\varphi^{*}\widetilde{H}\left(\varphi^{*}A\right)\cong\widetilde{H}\left(\varphi^{*}A\right)\to\widetilde{H}(A).

Example 2.17
.

If
H=\mathbb{G}_{a}
and
A
has a Frobenius endomorphism
\varphi:A\to A
, then
\varphi:\widetilde{H}(A)\to\widetilde{H}(A)
is identified with the usual Frobenius
\varphi:A\to A
.

Remark 2.18
.

If
f:H_{1}\to H_{2}
is a homomorphism of group schemes over
\mathcal{O}
, then the induced homomorphism
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{H}_{1}\to\widetilde{H}_{2}

commutes with
\varphi
. For example, we will apply this to the case when
\mathbb{G}_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{a}\hookrightarrow\widehat{G}
is the inclusion of a root group.

2.4.1.
Equivariance

The
\Gamma
-action on
\widetilde{H}
commutes with
\varphi
, because the
\Gamma
-action on
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
commutes with
\varphi
.
This is also true when
H
has an action of
\Gamma
defined over
\mathcal{O}
, and
\widetilde{H}
is given the “diagonal” action.

The resulting map on points will then also be
\Gamma
-equivariant.
For example, the map on global sections
\varphi:\widetilde{H}(\mathfrak{S}_{L,R})\to\widetilde{H}(\mathfrak{S}_{L,R})
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.
In particular, there is an induced map
\varphi:H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widetilde{H}(\mathfrak{S}_{L,R})\right)\to H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widetilde{H}(\mathfrak{S}_{L,R})\right)
.

2.4.2.
Endomorphism of the building

We follow
[
PR09
, Section 6.b.2]
in giving
\varphi
as an endomorphism of the apartments and buildings we considered in
Section

2.3
.
For simplicity we assume that
\mathbb{F}\supseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
, so that we are in the situation of
Example

2.1
, and we identify
\mathcal{J}
with embeddings
\mathbb{F}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}
.

We first define an endomorphism
\varphi
of the apartment
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
by

\varphi(x)_{j}=o_{j\varphi}+p(x_{j\varphi}-o_{j\varphi}).

That is,
\varphi
acts by cyclically permutating the factors and scaling by
p
.
Note that if
\lambda\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}
, then

\varphi(u^{\lambda}\cdot x)_{j}=\varphi\left(x-\frac{1}{e}\lambda\right)_{j}=o_{j\varphi}+p(x_{j\varphi}-o_{j\varphi})-\frac{1}{e}p\lambda_{j\varphi}=\varphi(u^{\lambda})_{j}\cdot\varphi(x)_{j}.

In fact,
\varphi(n\cdot x)=\varphi(n)\cdot\varphi(x)
for all
n\in\widehat{N}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
and
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
.

Note that
\varphi
commutes with the
\Gamma
-action, and in particular yields an endomorphism of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{\Gamma}
.
This is precisely the endomorphism described in
[
PR09
, Section 6.b.2]
.

Since the diagonal
\varphi
respects the equivalence relation used to define the enlarged building, there is an induced endomorphism of the enlarged building.

2.5.
Galois types

In this section we will discuss
Galois types
, by which we mean 1-cocycles

\tau:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}).

Here, we view
\Gamma
as acting on
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})
simultaneously via group automorphisms on
\widehat{G}
and on the coefficients
\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}
(as when studying Galois cohomology).
We use the term “Galois type” loosely, in that we will refer to closely related 1-cocycles taking values in related groups such as
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
by the same name.

Remark 2.19
.

The terminology “Galois type” is not used in
[
Le+23
]
, which works with
inertial types
as classified by
[
GHS18
, Section 9]
,
but it is actually the Galois type which dictates descent data for Breuil–Kisin modules.
A key difference between our notion of Galois types and inertial types is that
\Gamma
acts non-trivially on the coefficients (e. g.
\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}
or
\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket
) in the Galois type situation, whereas in the case of inertial types there is no non-trivial action on the coefficients.
In
Section

2.5.4
we will elucidate the relation between Galois types and inertial types, and in
Section

7.1
we give an explicit example of how an inertial type gives rise to a Galois type.

2.5.1.
Group cohomology

The following results are well known.

Lemma 2.20

(Shapiro’s Lemma)
.

Assume that
\mathbb{F}\supset\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
Fix a factor
\mathbb{F}_{i}\subset\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{F}_{j}\cong\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Then the projection
\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\to\mathbb{F}_{i}
induces bijections

\displaystyle\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\right)^{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\cong\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{i})^{I}

\displaystyle H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cong H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{i})\right).

Proof.

Recall the
\Gamma
-group

\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{\Gamma}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})=\left\{f:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})|f(\delta\theta)={{}^{\delta}f(\theta)}\text{ for all }\delta\in I,\,\theta\in\Gamma\right\},

with group operation given by pointwise multiplication, and
with
\Gamma
-action given by right translations, i. e.
(\eta f)(\theta)=f(\theta\eta)
for
\eta\in\Gamma
,
f\in\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{\Gamma}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
.
By the non-abelian version of Shaprio’s Lemma
[
Ser97
, Section I.5.8b]
,
it suffices to show that
\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\right)\cong\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{\Gamma}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})

as
\Gamma
-groups.
Recall from
Example

2.1
that we can identify
\mathcal{J}=\{\iota,\iota\varphi^{-1},\dots,\iota\varphi^{-r+1}\}
for some fixed embedding
\iota:\mathbb{F}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}
.
Let us furthermore identify the embedding
\iota\varphi^{-j}
with the integer
j
, so we can write
\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\right)=\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
.
Consider the map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{\Gamma}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})

\displaystyle\to\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto g,\,\,\text{ where }\,\,g_{j}={{}^{\sigma^{j}}f(\sigma^{-j})},

where we remind the reader that
\sigma\in\Gamma
is the Frobenius element.
This map is clearly bijective, since any
f\in\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{\Gamma}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
is uniquely specified by the values
f(1),f(\sigma^{-1}),\dots,f(\sigma^{-r+1})
by the equivariance property.
It is also easy to see that this map is a group homomorphism.
So we need only check that this map is equivariant:

\displaystyle{{}^{\sigma^{j}}(\gamma f)(\sigma^{-j})}={{}^{\sigma^{j}}f(\sigma^{-j}\gamma)}={{}^{\sigma^{j}}f(\sigma^{-j}\gamma\sigma^{j}\sigma^{-j})}={{}^{\sigma^{j}\sigma^{-j}\gamma\sigma^{j}}f(\sigma^{-j})}={{}^{\gamma\sigma^{j}}f(\sigma^{-j})}={{}^{\gamma}g_{j}}

\displaystyle{{}^{\sigma^{j}}(\sigma f)(\sigma^{-j})}={{}^{\sigma\sigma^{j-1}}f(\sigma^{-j+1})}={{}^{\sigma}(g_{\sigma^{-1}j})}=(^{\sigma}g)_{j}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

It is a general fact that
\operatorname{Ind}_{I}^{\Gamma}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{Sets}}\left(\Gamma/I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})\right)
(see for example
[
NSW08
, Page 59]
)
and the right hand side is easily seen to be isomorphic to
\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
as a
\Gamma
-group. The proof above makes this explicit.

Lemma 2.22
.

If
\mathbb{F}\supset\mathbb{F}_{q}
, then the reduction modulo
u
map

H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})\right)

is a bijection.

Proof.

We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})\right),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the horizontal maps are given by reduction modulo
u
and the vertical maps are the bijections from Shapiro’s
Lemma

2.20
.
So it suffices to show that the bottom horizontal map is a bijection, which will follow from deformation theory and the fact that
\#I=e
is prime to
p
.
Note that the reduction modulo
u
map
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\to H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})\right)
is surjective, since it admits a section induced by the inclusion of constants
\mathbb{F}\subset\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket
, so we need only check injectivity.
Since
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)=\varprojlim_{n}H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n})\right)
, it suffices to show that
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n+1})\right)\to H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n})\right)
is injective for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
The square zero extension
J:=u^{n}\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n+1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n+1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n}
yields a short exact sequence of
I
-groups
[
DG80
, Theorem II.4.3.5]

0\to\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{G}(J)\hookrightarrow\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n+1})\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n})\to 1.

For any
[\rho]\in H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n+1})\right)
, let
{{}_{\rho}\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{G}(J)}
denote
\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{G}(J)
with
\rho
-twisted
I
-action
\gamma\cdot v=\rho(\gamma)(^{\gamma}v)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}
.
Then by
[
Ser97
, Section I.5.5]
there is an exact sequence of pointed sets

H^{1}\left(I,{{}_{\rho}\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{G}(J)}\right)\to H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n+1})\right)\to H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n})\right),

where
[\rho]
is the basepoint of the middle set (exactness then means that any class in the middle having mapping to the same class as
[\rho]
is in the image of the left map).
The point is now that
{{}_{\rho}\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{G}(J)}
is an
\mathbb{F}
-module, and since
e=\#I
is invertible in
\mathbb{F}
,
multiplication by
e
acts as an automorphism of
{{}_{\rho}\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{G}(J)}
.
It then follows that
H^{1}\left(I,{{}_{\rho}\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{G}(J)}\right)=0
by
[
Ser97
, Section I.2.4]
, and since
\rho
was arbitrary, that
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n+1})\right)\hookrightarrow H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}[u]/u^{n})\right)
is injective.
∎

2.5.2.
Building theoretic classification of Galois types

Following
[
PR23
, Proposition 5.4]
, we give a classification of types in terms of the enlarged Bruhat–Tits building from
Section

2.3.5
.

Proposition 2.23
.

There is a bijection

H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\backslash\left(\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))\cdot o\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(G^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!)\right)\right),

where the intersection takes place in
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(\widehat{G},\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
, using tamely ramified descent for Bruhat–Tits buildings to identify
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(G^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(\widehat{G},\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Note that
H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))\right)\cong H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!))\right)
by Shapiro’s
Lemma

2.20
,
and
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!))\right)
classifies
G^{*}
-torsors over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!)
which trivialize over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!)
.
By Steinberg’s theorem
[
KP23
, Theorem 2.3.3]
we do in fact have
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!))\right)=0
.

Applying
[
Ser97
, I.5.4 Corollary 1]
(non-abelian version of the long exact sequence in group cohomology induced by a short exact sequence) to the subgroup
\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\subset\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
yields

H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\cong G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\backslash\left(\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))/\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)^{\Gamma}.

The subgroup
\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\subset\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
is the stabilizer of
o
by
[
KP23
, Lemma 4.3.3, Lemma 7.7.10, Lemma 7.7.11, Proposition 8.3.16]
,
and the result follows.
∎

Remark 2.24
.

From the proof and unpacking of
[
Ser97
, I.5.4 Corollary 1]
, the bijection of
Proposition

2.23
can be made explicit as follows.
Let
\tau:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
be a 1-cocycle.
Choose
b\in\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
such that
\tau(\theta)=b^{-1}(^{\theta}b)
for all
\theta\in\Gamma
(and such a coboundary always exists).
Then
\tau
is mapped to the
G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
-orbit of
b\cdot o
.

Remark 2.25
.

In practice, we work with
\tau:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
, where
\mathbb{F}
is finite but large enough to trivialize
\tau
in the
sense that there exists a coboundary
b\in\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
such that
\tau(\theta)=b^{-1}(^{\theta}b)
for all
\theta\in\Gamma
.
Via
Proposition

2.23
,
\tau
can then be described as the
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
-orbit of a point
\widetilde{x}\in\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))\cdot o\cap\mathcal{B}(G^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
.

Remark 2.26
.

For any cohomology class
[\tau]\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)

and any alcove
C\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(G^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!)\right)
,

Proposition

2.23
together with the fact that
G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
acts transitively on alcoves
implies that there exists
g\in\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))

for which the point
x=g\cdot o
lies in the closure of
C
and
[\tau]
is represented by
\tau(\theta)=g^{-1}(^{\theta}g)
.
Moreover, if
C\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!)\right)
, we will see in
Proposition

2.29
below
that we can take
g\in\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
.

Remark 2.27
.

The bijection of
Proposition

2.23
is
\varphi
-equivariant:
If
g\cdot o
corresponds to
\tau(\theta)=g^{-1}(^{\theta}g)
,
then
\varphi(g\cdot o)=\varphi(g)\cdot o
corresponds to
\varphi\tau(\theta)=\varphi(g^{-1}(^{\theta}g))=\varphi(g)^{-1}(^{\theta}\varphi(g))
.

As a part of the above
Proposition

2.23
, we have that any Galois type
[\tau]\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
is represented by
some
\tau
of the form
\tau(\theta)=g^{-1}(^{\theta}g)
, where
g\in\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
.
The following result shows that we may even take
g\in\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
.

Lemma 2.28
.

The natural map

N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\backslash\left(\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))\cdot o\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(T^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\backslash\left(\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))\cdot o\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(G^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!)\right)\right)

is bijective.

Proof.

We start by proving injectivity.
This follows from the fact
[
KP23
, Corollary 4.2.25]
that if
x,x^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
and there exists
g\in G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
such that
g\cdot x^{\prime}=x
, then there exists
n\in N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
such that
n\cdot x^{\prime}=x
.

It remains to show surjectivity.
Let
x=b\cdot o
be fixed by
\Gamma
, where
b\in\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
.
Since
G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
acts transitively on the alcoves of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(G^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
,
there is some
g\in G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
such that
g\cdot x
is in the closure of any given alcove, and in particular we can ensure that
x^{\prime}=g\cdot x=gb\cdot o\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(T^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{T},\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))^{\Gamma}
.
By
[
KP23
, Corollary 4.2.25]
again there exists
n\in N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
such that
n\cdot o=gb\cdot o=x^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 2.29
.

Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\backslash\left(\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))\cdot o\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(T^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\backslash\left(\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))\cdot o\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(G^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!)\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the vertical maps are given by sending the class of
g\cdot o
to
\tau(\theta)=g^{-1}(^{\theta}g)
.
Then the arrows marked
\sim
are bijective, the left vertical arrow is injective and the bottom horizontal arrow is surjective.

Proof.

Immediate from
Proposition

2.23
and
Lemma

2.28
.
∎

The above result implies that any Galois type
[\tau]\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
is represented by a
1
-cocycle
\tau
of the form
\tau(\theta)=n^{-1}(^{\theta}n)
, where
n\in\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
.
It will be convenient to have an even more control over what
n
can be.

Proposition 2.30
.

Let
\dot{N}\subset\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
be a subgroup which is stable under the action of
\Gamma
, and let
\dot{N}^{0}:=\dot{N}\cap\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
.
Consider the map

(10)

\dot{N}^{\Gamma}\backslash\left(\dot{N}\cdot o\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(T^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\right)\to N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\backslash\left(\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))\cdot o\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(T^{*},\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\right).

Then we have the following:

(A)

If
\dot{N}
contains the image of the map
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}\cong\left(X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\right)^{\Gamma}\subset X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\to\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
6
6

6

The identification
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}\cong\left(X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\right)^{\Gamma}
depends on a choice of
j\in\mathcal{J}
, just like in
Lemma

2.20
.
given by
\lambda\mapsto u^{\lambda}
(where the action of
\Gamma
on
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}
given by (
9
)), then (
10
) is surjective.

(B)

If the map
\dot{N}^{\Gamma}\subset\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))^{\Gamma}=N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\to\widetilde{W}^{*}
is surjective (where
\widetilde{W}^{*}
denotes the Iwahori–Weyl group for
G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
from
Section

2.2.1
), then (
10
) is injective.

Consequently, if the assumptions in both (A) and (B) are satisfied, so that (
10
) is a bijection,
then the map
H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\dot{N}^{0}\right)\twoheadrightarrow H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
is surjective and admits a canonical section.
In concrete terms, given any
1
-cocycle
\tau:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
, there exists
n\in\dot{N}
for which
\tau
is coboundarous to the
1
-cocycle
\tau^{\prime}:\Gamma\to\dot{N}^{0}
given by
\tau^{\prime}(\theta)=n^{-1}(^{\theta}n)
.
Moreover, if
n_{1},n_{2}\in\dot{N}
and
\tau_{1}(\theta)=n_{1}^{-1}(^{\theta}n_{1})
,
\tau_{2}(\theta)=n_{2}^{-1}(^{\theta}n_{2})
, then
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are coboundarous by an element of
\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
if and only if
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are coboundarous by an element of
\dot{N}^{0}
.

Proof.

We first show (A).
So let
n\in\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
and assume that the point
x=n\cdot o
is fixed by
\Gamma
.
By
Lemma

2.8
there exists
\lambda\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}
and
w\in W^{\mathcal{J}}
such that the image of
n
in the Iwahori–Weyl group
\widetilde{W}^{\mathcal{J}}=X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\rtimes W^{\mathcal{J}}
is
u^{\lambda}w
.
Since the action of
\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
factors through
\widetilde{W}^{\mathcal{J}}
,
we see that

x=n\cdot o=u^{\lambda}w\cdot o=u^{\lambda}\cdot o=o-\lambda,

using
w\cdot o=o
.
Now, recall from
Section

2.3.4
that action map
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
given by
\nu\mapsto u^{\nu}\cdot o=o-\nu

is injective and
\Gamma
-equivariant with respect to the action of
\Gamma
on
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}
given by (
9
).
The condition that
x
is fixed by
\Gamma
is therefore equivalent to the condition that
\lambda
is fixed by
\Gamma
.
In sum, we have shown that
x=u^{\lambda}\cdot o
for some
\lambda\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}
, which under the assumption of (A) implies that (
10
) is surjective.

We now turn to (B).
That is, suppose
n_{1},n_{2}\in\dot{N}
and there is
g\in N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
such that
n_{1}\cdot o=gn_{2}\cdot o
.
The assumption of (B) implies that we can find
h\in\dot{N}^{\Gamma}
such that the images of
g,h
in
\widetilde{W}^{*}
concide.
Since the action of
N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
factors through
\widetilde{W}^{*}
, it follows that
n_{1}\cdot o=hn_{2}\cdot o
.
This shows that (
10
) is injective.

The additional statements are immediate from the fact that we now have a diagram as in
Proposition

2.29
with the respective roles of
\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
,
\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
, and
N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
being replaced with
\dot{N}
,
\dot{N}^{0}
, and
\dot{N}^{\Gamma}
.
∎

Remark 2.31
.

The above
Proposition

2.30
shows that all Galois types are represented by
1
-cocycles of the form
\tau(\theta)=u^{-\lambda}(^{\theta}u^{\lambda})
for some
\lambda\in\left(X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\right)^{\Gamma}
.
In particular,
\tau(\sigma)=1
and
\tau(\gamma)=\omega(\gamma)^{\lambda}
, where
\omega^{\lambda}
denotes the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q}\right)^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{F}_{j}=\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{T}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\mapsto 1\otimes a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

If
j\in\mathcal{J}
is identified with an embedding
\iota_{j}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{F}}
, then
the projection of
\omega^{\lambda}
onto the
j
’th factor
\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{j})=\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}})
is
\iota_{j}(\omega)^{\lambda_{j}}
(because
{{}^{\gamma}u}=\iota_{j}(\omega(\gamma))u
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{j}(\!(u)\!)
).
For generic
\lambda
, this depends on the value
j
.
As we will see in
Lemma

2.39
, we can often make the value of the projection of
\tau(\gamma)
onto the
j
’th factor independent of
j
at the cost of changing
\tau(\sigma)
into a non-trivial element.

Example 2.32
.

What is a minimal
\dot{N}\subset\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
satisfying the conditions (A) and (B) in
Proposition

2.30
?
An obvious choice is to let
\dot{N}
be the subgroup generated by the image of
\left(X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\right)^{\Gamma}
and
\widehat{N}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{J}})
, because
\widehat{N}(\mathbb{Z})
maps surjectively onto
W^{\mathcal{J}}
(the finite Weyl group) as we saw in the proof of
Lemma

2.8
.
But more optimally, we can replace
\widehat{N}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{J}})
by another minimal subgroup which maps surjectively onto
W^{\mathcal{J}}
.
For example, if
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
or
\operatorname{PGL}_{n}
, then we can embed
W^{\mathcal{J}}\subset\widehat{N}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{J}})
, and consequently also
\widetilde{W}^{\mathcal{J}}\subset\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
, but this is not possible if
\widehat{G}=\operatorname{SL}_{n}
.

We are not going to use the following result, but include it here for completeness. It gives an upper bound on the number of Galois types.

Proposition 2.33
.

The map
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}\subset X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\to H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
given by sending
\lambda
to the class represented by the
1
-cocycle
\tau(\theta)=u^{-\lambda}(^{\theta}u^{\lambda})
is surjective.
Moreover, this map factors through the Tate cohomology group
\widehat{H}^{0}\left(I,X_{*}(\widehat{T})\right)=N\left(X_{*}(\widehat{T})_{I}\right)\backslash X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}
, where
N:X_{*}(\widehat{T})_{I}\to X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}
is the norm map.
In particular, if
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{T}
, we have a surjection
eX_{*}(\widehat{T})\backslash X_{*}(\widehat{T})\twoheadrightarrow H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
.

Proof.

The first statement (about surjectivity) is immediate from
Proposition

2.30
.
Using Shapiro’s
Lemma

2.20
we make an identification
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\cong H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
which depends on a choice of
j\in\mathcal{J}
.
We then see that the given map factors through the map
\delta:X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}\to H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
, which maps
\lambda\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}
to the class
\delta(\lambda)
represented by the
1
-cocycle
\tau(\gamma)=u^{-\lambda}(^{\gamma}u^{\lambda})
.
Therefore, we need only show that
\delta
factors through
\widehat{H}^{0}\left(I,X_{*}(\widehat{T})\right)
.
Recall that the discrete valuation of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!)
yields a short exact sequence

0\to\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\to\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e\cdot\operatorname{val}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{*}(\widehat{T})\to 0,

where we need to scale the valuation by
e
because
\operatorname{val}(u)=1/e
.
The long exact sequence in
I
-group cohomology then gives the exact sequence

\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!))^{I}=T^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e\cdot\operatorname{val}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\to H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!))\right)=0,

where the last equality is due to Steinberg’s Theorem (or just Hilbert 90 if
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{T}
).
It now follows from
[
KP23
, Corollary 11.7.3]
that
e\cdot\operatorname{val}(T^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!)))\subset X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}
coincides with the image of the norm map
N:X_{*}(\widehat{T})_{I}\to X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}
,
so indeed
\delta
yields an isomorphism

N(X_{*}(\widehat{T})_{I})\backslash X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}=\widehat{H}^{0}\left(I,X_{*}(\widehat{T})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{T}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right).

Finally, note that if
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{T}
, then
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}=X_{*}(\widehat{T})
and
N(X_{*}(\widehat{T}))=eX_{*}(\widehat{T})
since
e=\#I
.
∎

2.5.3.
Frobenius invariant types

Galois types arising from Breuil–Kisin modules with descent data are not completely arbitrary,
since they have the property of being “Frobenius invariant” in the following sense.

Definition 2.34
.

We say that a cohomology class
[\tau]\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
is

Frobenius invariant
if
\varphi([\tau])=[\varphi\tau]=[\tau]
. We say that a
1
-cocycle
\tau\in Z^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)

is
strictly Frobenius invariant
if
\varphi\tau=\tau
on the level of
1
-cocycles.
(This definition also works with other rings such as
\mathcal{O}
or
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
in place of
\mathbb{F}
.)

The following result shows that any type arising from Breuil–Kisin modules with descent data indeed is Frobenius invariant.
The classical case of this result is discussed in
[
Car+19
, Remark 3.3.3]
.

Proposition 2.35
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
.
Then the map

H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur},\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\hookrightarrow H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur},\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket[(u^{e}+p)^{-1}])\right)

is injective, where
\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}\subset E^{\text{ur}}
is the ring of integers in the maximal unramified extension of
E
.

Proof.

By Shapiro’s
Lemma

2.20
we may replace
\Gamma
by
I
and
\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur},\mathcal{J}}
by
\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}
.
Consider the composition

H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\to H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}\llbracket u\rrbracket[(u^{e}+p)^{-1}])\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mod u}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\overline{E})\right).

The first isomorphism is a consequence of deformation theory and the fact that
e=\#I
is prime to
p
(more precisely, by the argument in the proof of
Lemma

2.22
).
The map
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\to H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}\llbracket u\rrbracket[(u^{e}+p)^{-1}])\right)
is injective provided that the composition is injective, which is what we will show.

Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}:I\to\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}})
be two 1-cocycles for which the composites
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}:I\to\widehat{G}(\overline{E})
are coboundarous.
Since
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}})
,
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
are just homomorphisms,
and being coboundarous means being conjugate.
The question is then whether the two elements
\rho_{1}(\gamma),\rho_{2}(\gamma)\in\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}})
, which are conjugate by an element of
\widehat{G}(\overline{E})
, must be conjugate by an element of
\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}})
.

This is where we invoke Lafforgue’s theory of pseudo-characters.
As explained in
[
Tho19
, Section 3]
, one can construct certain functions
\operatorname{tr}\rho_{1}
and
\operatorname{tr}\rho_{2}
(analogous to the trace),
and the fact that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are conjugate by an element of
\widehat{G}(\overline{E})
implies that
\operatorname{tr}\rho_{1}=\operatorname{tr}\rho_{2}
(where we use that
\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}\subset\overline{E}
).
On the other hand, we will show in a moment that reduction modulo
\varpi
yields a bijection

(11)

H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}})\right)\cong H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}})\right).

If we let
\overline{\rho}_{1},\overline{\rho}_{2}:I\to\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}})
denote the reductions modulo
\varpi
of
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
,
then the equality
\operatorname{tr}\rho_{1}=\operatorname{tr}\rho_{2}
implies that
\operatorname{tr}\overline{\rho}_{1}=\operatorname{tr}\overline{\rho}_{2}

by universality of the construction of these pseudo-characters.
By
[
Tho19
, Theorem 3.3]
applied to homomorphisms
I\to\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}})
, it then follows that
\overline{\rho}_{1}
and
\overline{\rho}_{2}
are coboundarous.
By (
11
), so are
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
.

It remains to show the bijection (
11
).
This is again a consequence of deformation theory and the fact that
e=\#I
is prime to
p
.
However, since
\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}
is not complete, the argument from the proof of
Lemma

2.22
is not directly applicable.
But for each finite extension
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}\supset\mathbb{F}
and cooresponding extension
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\supset\mathcal{O}
, we have a bijection
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})\right)\cong H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\prime})\right)
,
and by
[
Ser97
, Section I.2.2]
we then obtain
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}})\right)\cong\varinjlim H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})\right)\cong\varinjlim H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\prime})\right)\cong H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}})\right)
.
∎

Remark 2.36
.

Whereas the above shows that
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\hookrightarrow H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\text{ur}}\llbracket u\rrbracket[(u^{e}+p)^{-1}])\right)

is injective, it is not true that
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)\to H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}\llbracket u\rrbracket[(u^{e}+p)^{-1}])\right)
is injective.
The reasons are that (1)
\overline{\mathbb{F}}\llbracket u\rrbracket[(u^{e}+p)^{-1}]=\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!)
, so there is no “reduction modulo
u
” map, (2)
[
Tho19
, Theorem 3.3]
doesn’t apply to
1
-cocycles
I\to\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!))
because
I
acts non-trivially on
\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!)
and (3) because
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
is not algebraically closed.
In fact,
H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(u)\!))\right)=0
by Steinberg’s Theorem
[
KP23
, Theorem 2.3.3]
,
which implies that it’s possible for Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
to have any “mixed” type in the sense of
[
Car+19
]
.
An explicit example illustrating this fact can be found in
[
CL18
, Remark 3.7]
.

We can interpret Frobenius invariance of Galois types in terms of our classification given by
Proposition

2.23
.

Lemma 2.37
.

Let
[\tau]\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
be a Galois type which via the bijection of

Proposition

2.23
is represented by a point
x=g\cdot o
, where
g\in\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
.

(1)

Then
[\tau]
is Frobenius invariant if and only if there exists
c\in G^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
such that
c\cdot\varphi(x)=x
.

(2)

After passing to a finite unramified extension, there exists a representative
\tau
for the class
[\tau]
which is
strictly
Frobenius invariant, i. e. satisfies
\varphi\tau=\tau
on the level of
1
-cocycles.
More precisely, for a finite unramified extension
L^{\prime}\supset L
, let
\Gamma^{\prime}=\operatorname{Gal}(L^{\prime}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
,
q^{\prime}
be the order of the residue field of
L^{\prime}
, and let
[\tau]^{\prime}\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma^{\prime},\widehat{G}(\left(\overline{\mathbb{F}}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}\right)\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
denote the image of
[\tau]
(induced by the map on
1
-cocycles which maps a representative
1
-cocycle
\tau:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}((\overline{\mathbb{F}}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q})\llbracket u\rrbracket)
to the composite
\Gamma^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{G}((\overline{\mathbb{F}}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q})\llbracket u\rrbracket)\subset\widehat{G}((\overline{\mathbb{F}}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}})\llbracket u\rrbracket)
).
Then there exists such an extension
L^{\prime}\supset L
and a representative
1
-cocycle
\tau^{\prime}:\Gamma^{\prime}\to\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
for the class
[\tau]^{\prime}
which is strictly Frobenius invariant.

Proof.

(1) is obvious from
Remark

2.27
, so let us show (2).
Let
\tilde{\tau}
be some representative for the class
[\tau]
.
Note that by
Lemma

2.22
and
Remark

2.31
we may assume that
\tilde{\tau}
takes values in
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})
as long as
\mathbb{F}\supset\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
The assumption that
\tilde{\tau}
is Frobenius invariant means that there exists a coboundary
b\in\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
such that
b\varphi(\tilde{\tau}(\theta))(^{\theta}b)^{-1}=\tilde{\tau}(\theta)
for all
\theta\in\Gamma
.
Note that if
H=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathbb{F}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\widehat{G}
, then we can identify
\varphi:\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})
with the relative Frobenius of
H
applied to
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-points.
Hence the map
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})
given by
c\mapsto c^{-1}\varphi(c)
can be identified with the Lang isogeny of
H
, which must be surjective by Lang’s theorem
[
Mil17
, Theorem 17.96]
.
Therefore, there exists some finite extension
\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}}\supset\mathbb{F}_{q}
and
c\in\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q^{\prime}})
such that
b=c^{-1}\varphi(c)
.
Our strictly Frobenius invariant
1
-cocycle is then
\tau^{\prime}(\theta):=c\tilde{\tau}(\theta)(^{\theta}c)^{-1}
for
\theta\in\Gamma
.
Indeed, we now have

\varphi\tau^{\prime}(\theta)=\varphi(c)\varphi\tilde{\tau}(\theta)(^{\theta}\varphi(c))^{-1}=cb\varphi\tilde{\tau}(\theta)(^{\theta}b)^{-1}(^{\theta}c)^{-1}=c\tilde{\tau}(\theta)(^{\theta}c)^{-1}=\tau^{\prime}(\theta)

for all
\theta\in\Gamma
.
∎

Remark 2.38
.

The proof above relied on Lang’s theorem to find a solution
c
of the equation
b=c^{-1}\varphi(c)
.
We now give an explicit alternative method.
Namely, choose
\iota_{0}\in\mathcal{J}
, in which case we can identify
\mathcal{J}=\{\iota_{0},\iota_{0}\varphi^{-1},\dots,\iota_{0}\varphi^{-r+1}\}
as in
Example

2.1
.
Given an element
g\in\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})
and
j\in\{0,1,\dots,r-1\}
let
g_{j}\in\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{\iota_{0}\varphi^{-j}})
denote its projection onto the factor corresponding to
\iota_{0}\varphi^{-j}
.
We then have
\varphi(g)_{j}=g_{j-1}
.
We find a solution by choosing
c_{0}
arbitrarily, and recursively defining
c_{j}=b_{j}^{-1}c_{j-1}
for
j=1,2,\dots,r-1
, i. e.
c_{j}=b_{j}^{-1}b_{j-1}^{-1}\cdots b_{1}^{-1}c_{0}
.
Then
c
solves
b=c^{-1}\varphi(c)
if and only if
c_{0}=b_{0}^{-1}c_{r-1}
, i. e. if and only if
b_{0}b_{1}\cdots b_{r-1}=1
.
Now the point is that even if
\tilde{b}:=b_{0}b_{1}\cdots b_{r-1}\neq 1
, this is an element of a finite group, hence
\tilde{b}^{s}=1
for some
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Therefore we can solve the equation by passing from coefficients in
\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}=\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q}
to
\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q^{s}}
(and further extending
\mathbb{F}
if needed).

Lemma 2.39
.

Let
[\tau]\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
be a Frobenius invariant Galois type.
After passing to a finite unramified extension (in the sense of
Lemma

2.37
(2)), there exists
\lambda\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}\cong\left(X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\right)^{\Gamma}
and
w\in\widehat{N}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{J}})

such that if we set
n:=w^{-1}u^{\lambda}
, then the
1
-cocycle
\tau(\theta)=n^{-1}(^{\theta}n)
represents the class
[\tau]
and is
strictly
Frobenius invariant (i. e.
\varphi\tau=\tau
).

Proof.

Let
\dot{N}\subset\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
be the subgroup generated by the image of
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}
and
\widehat{N}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{J}})
.
By
Proposition

2.30
it follows that there exists
n^{\prime}=w^{\prime-1}u^{\lambda^{\prime}}\in\dot{N}
such that
\tau^{\prime}(\theta)=n^{\prime-1}(^{\theta}n^{\prime})
is a representative for the class
[\tau]
,
and also that there exists
b\in\dot{N}^{0}=\dot{N}\cap\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
such that
b\varphi\tau^{\prime}(\theta)(^{\theta}b)^{-1}=\tau^{\prime}(\theta)
for all
\theta\in\Gamma
.
As in the proof of
Lemma

2.37
it suffices to find
c\in\dot{N}
such that
b=c^{-1}\varphi(c)
.
Although Lang’s theorem doesn’t apply, we can still use the explicit method of
Remark

2.38
.
∎

Remark 2.40
.

If
n\in\widehat{N}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
and
\tau(\theta)=n^{-1}(^{\theta}n)
is the associated
1
-cocycle, then
\varphi
is strictly Frobenius invariant (i. e.
\varphi\tau=\tau
) if and only if
n\varphi(n)^{-1}\in N^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!))
(i. e.
n\varphi(n)^{-1}
is fixed by
\Gamma
).

Example 2.41
.

Suppose
L=\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}\left((-p)^{1/e}\right)
and
\widehat{G}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}
. Since
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is semisimple and simply connected,
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
acts simply transitively on the chambers of
\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
, where
\widehat{T}\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is the diagonal torus.
If
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
denotes the base alcove, it follows that Galois types
[\tau]\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)

are classified by vertices
\operatorname{SL}_{2}\left(\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!)\right)\cdot o\cap\mathcal{C}
. For
p=7
and
e=24
this is illustrated by the colored nodes in
Fig.

2
.

Consider
x=u^{(n,-n)}\cdot o=o-\frac{1}{e}(n,-n)
, classifying a type
[\tau]
.
For
p=7
and
e=24
the colored nodes in
Fig.

2
correspond to such
x
where
n\in\{0,-1,\dots,-12\}
.
The point
x
classified the type
[\tau]
represented by
\tau(\sigma)=u^{-(n,-n)}(^{\sigma}u^{(n,-n)})=1
and
\tau(\gamma)=u^{-(n,-n)}(^{\gamma}u^{(n,-n)})=\omega(\gamma)^{(n,-n)}
.
Here,
u^{(n,-n)}
represents the element of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{0}(\!(u)\!))\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{1}(\!(u)\!))
for which both factors is what is typically denoted
u^{(n,-n)}
.
In particular, note that
\omega(\gamma)^{(n,-n)}=\left(\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(n,-n)},\iota_{1}(\omega(\gamma))^{(n,-n)}\right)=\left(\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(n,-n)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(pn,-pn)}\right)
, where
\iota_{0},\iota_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{p^{2}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}
are the two embeddings.

o
o+(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2})

Figure 2.

The base alcove of
\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
where
\widehat{T}\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}
is the diagonal torus, for
p=7
and
e=24
.
The large nodes are vertices of
\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
and the small nodes are vertices of
\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!)\right)
.
The white nodes are vertices of
\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!)\right)
which are not in the orbit of
o
.
The green nodes classify types which are Frobenius invariant, and the red nodes classify types which are not Frobenius invariant.
Since the base alcove is a fundamental domain for the
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
action, we see that there are (under the chosen parameters) exactly 13 classes of Galois types
\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
, of which 7 are Frobenius invariant.

What does it mean for
x=u^{(n,-n)}\cdot o\in\mathcal{A}(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
to represent a Frobenius invariant type? As we have meantioned, this is equivalent to
x
and
\varphi(x)=u^{(pn,-pn)}\cdot o
being in the same
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
orbit.
There are two ways this can happen:

(1)

If there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
v^{(m,-m)}\cdot\varphi(x)=x
, i. e. if
(p-1)n\equiv 0\mod e
.

(2)

If there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
w_{0}v^{(m,-m)}\cdot\varphi(x)=x
, where
w_{0}
is the reflection across
o
(corresponding to the unique element of the finite Weyl group of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
).
This is equivalent to
(p+1)n\equiv 0\mod e
.

This is how one can determine the coloring of the nodes in
Fig.

2
.

As a concrete example, consider
x=u^{(-3,3)}\cdot o
for
p=7
and
e=24
, corresponding to the second green node from the left (including
o
) in
Fig.

2
.
Then since
(7+1)(-3)\equiv 0\mod 24
, the type classified by
x
, which is represented by
\tau(\theta)=u^{-(-3,3)}(^{\theta}u^{(-3,3)})
for
\theta\in\Gamma
, is Frobenius invariant.
To make this even more explicit, note that
\tau(\sigma)=1
and
\tau(\gamma)=\left(\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-3,3)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-21,21)}\right)
.
Using the observation that
\varphi
acts on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{0})\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
by swapping the factors, we find that

\varphi\tau(\sigma)=1=\tau(\sigma)
and
\varphi\tau(\gamma)=\left(\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-21,21)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-3,3)}\right)
.
Note that
\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-21,21)}=\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(3,-3)}
since
\gamma^{e}=1
and
-21\equiv 3\mod e=24
.
Therefore, if we let
b=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\right)
, then
b\varphi\tau(\theta)(^{\theta}b^{-1})=\tau(\theta)
for all
\theta\in\Gamma
, showing that
[\tau]
is Frobenius invariant.

Note that our representative
\tau
for the class
[\tau]
corresponding to
x
is Frobenius invariant, but not strictly so.
However, we can follow the method of
Remark

2.38
to produce a representative which is strictly Frobenius invariant.
Since
b_{0}b_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}
has order
2
, we should according to
Remark

2.38
pass to an unramified extension of order
2
.
Let us first see what goes wrong if we don’t:
Define
g=(g_{0},g_{1})\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
by choosing
g_{0}=1\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{0}(\!(u)\!))
and let
g_{1}=b_{1}^{-1}g_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
.
Consider now the Galois type
\tilde{\tau}:\Gamma\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
with
\tilde{\tau}(\theta)=g\tau(\theta)(^{\theta}g)^{-1}
for
\theta\in\Gamma
.
Then we have
\tilde{\tau}(\gamma)=\left(\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-3,3)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(21,-21)}\right)=\varphi\tilde{\tau}(\gamma)
(since
-3\equiv 21\mod 24
), but
\tilde{\tau}(\sigma)=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\right)\neq\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\right)=\varphi\tilde{\tau}(\sigma)
.
So let us now pass to
L^{\prime}=\mathbb{Q}_{p^{4}}\left((-p)^{1/e}\right)
, in which case the type
\tau
gives rise to

\tau^{\prime}:\Gamma^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Gal}(L^{\prime}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\operatorname{SL}_{2}\left(\mathbb{F}_{0}\right)\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{3})

with
\tau^{\prime}(\gamma)=\left(\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-3,3)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(3,-3)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-3,3)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(3,-3)}\right)

and
\tau^{\prime}(\sigma)=1
.
Then
b=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\right)

gives a coboundary
b:\varphi\tau^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tau^{\prime}
.
But now
b_{0}b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}=(b_{0}b_{1})^{2}=1
, so according to
Remark

2.38
, we can define

c_{0}=1
,
c_{1}=b_{1}^{-1}c_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
,
c_{2}=b_{2}^{-1}c_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}
,
c_{3}=b_{3}^{-1}c_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
, in which case we also have
b_{0}^{-1}c_{3}=1=c_{0}
.
Then
\tilde{\tau}(\theta)=c\tau^{\prime}(\theta)(^{\theta}c)^{-1}
is strictly Frobenius invariant, because
\tilde{\tau}(\gamma)=\left(\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-3,3)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-3,3)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-3,3)},\iota_{0}(\omega(\gamma))^{(-3,3)}\right)

and
\tilde{\tau}(\sigma)=\left(c_{0}c_{3}^{-1},c_{1}c_{0}^{-1},c_{2}c_{1}^{-1},c_{3}c_{2}^{-1}\right)=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\right)
.

2.5.4.
Inertial types vs. Galois types

In the literature (e.g.
[
GHS18
]
,
[
Le+23
]
,
[
Car+19
]
), the descent datum for a Breuil–Kisin module is usually specified in terms on an inertial type.
We spell out how this is related to our Galois type.
For this it is convenient to use the following enlargement of
\Gamma
, namely

\Gamma_{W}:=I\rtimes\mathbb{Z}=\{\gamma,\sigma:\gamma^{e}=1,\sigma\gamma\sigma^{-1}=\gamma^{p}\}.

The following is consistent with
[
GHS18
, Section 9]
.

Definition 2.42
.

An
inertial type
is a homomorphism
\tau^{\prime}:I\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
which extends to a 1-cocycle
\Gamma_{W}\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
.
Here, we view
\mathbb{F}
as having trivial
\Gamma_{W}
-action, so
\Gamma_{W}
acts on
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
purely via group automorphisms on
\widehat{G}
.

If
\tau^{\prime}
is an inertial type, note that the extension
\Gamma_{W}\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
necessarily factors through a finite quotient
\Gamma^{\prime}
in which
\sigma^{r^{\prime}}=1
,
and conversely, any 1-cocycle
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
yields by restriction an inertial type.
In principle one can first classify all inertial types, and let the splitting field
L
be dictated by the inertial type.
Note also that since Breuil–Kisin modules and the property of being crystalline satisfy unramified descent, it is harmless to modify
L
by an unramified extension.

There is a discrepancy between 1-cocycles
\tau^{\prime}:\Gamma_{W}\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})

(which come from inertial types) and
\tau:\Gamma_{W}\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})
(which correspond to our notion of Galois types).
Although
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})\cong\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j})
, the issue is that
\sigma
has a permuting effect on the factors.
However, under the assumption of Frobenius invariance, one can pass between these notions by using the isomorphism
\varphi\tau\to\varphi
to “linearize” the Galois type, yielding an inertial type.
This is akin to the usual construction of a Weil–Deligne representation from the semilinear descent data attached to a potentially crystalline representation.
We record this construction in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.43
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
.
Let
\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J},\text{triv}}
denote
\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}=\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q}
equipped with the trivial
\Gamma
-action.
Then for any choice of
j\in\mathcal{J}
we have equivalences of groupoids

\displaystyle Z^{1}\left(\Gamma_{W},\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\right)\right)^{\varphi=1}

\displaystyle\cong Z^{1}\left(\Gamma_{W},\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J},\text{triv}}\right)\right)^{\varphi=1}\cong Z^{1}\left(\Gamma_{W},\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle\left(\tau,b:\varphi\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\,\,\,\,\,\left(\tau^{\prime},b:\varphi\tau^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tau^{\prime}\right)\,\,\,\,\,\mapsto\tau^{\prime}_{j}

where
\tau^{\prime}(\gamma)=\tau(\gamma)
and
\tau^{\prime}(\sigma)=\varphi^{-1}(b^{-1}\tau(\sigma))=\tau(\sigma)\psi(b)^{-1}
, and we view
\mathbb{F}_{j}
as having trivial
\Gamma
-action.
Here, we recall that
\psi:\widehat{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{G}
denotes the group automorphism of
\widehat{G}
determined by
\sigma
,
and the superscript
\varphi=1
denotes homotopy fixed points of groupoids (so elements of for example
Z^{1}\left(\Gamma_{W},\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\right)\right)^{\varphi=1}

are pairs
(\tau,b)
, where
\tau:\Gamma_{W}\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})
is a
1
-cocycle and
b:\varphi\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tau
is a coboundary).

Proof.

Let
H
be any
\Gamma_{W}
-group on which
I\subset\Gamma_{W}
acts trivially.
Then we have a bijection

\displaystyle Z^{1}\left(\Gamma_{W},H\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\{(x,y)\in H\times H|x^{e}=1,y(^{\sigma}x)y^{-1}=x^{p}\}

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\mapsto(\rho(\gamma),\rho(\sigma)).

We will apply this to
H=\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\right),\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J},\text{triv}}),\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j})
.
Note that when
H=\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\right)
, we have
{{}^{\sigma}x}=\varphi\psi(x)=\psi\varphi(x)
,
but when
H=\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J},\text{triv}})
or
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j})
we have
{{}^{\sigma}x}=\psi(x)
.

Now let
\left(\tau,b\right)\in Z^{1}\left(\Gamma_{W},\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\right)\right)^{\varphi=1}
and let
\tau^{\prime}
be as in the statement of the lemma.
We first show that
\tau^{\prime}
is a
1
-cocycle.
Let
x=\tau(\gamma)=\tau^{\prime}(\gamma)
,
y=\tau(\sigma)
, and
y^{\prime}=\varphi^{-1}(b^{-1}y)
.
Note that
b:\varphi\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tau
being a coboundary means that
b\varphi(x)b^{-1}=x
and
b\varphi(y)=y(^{\sigma}b)^{-1}=y\psi(\varphi(b))^{-1}
.
We have

\displaystyle y^{\prime}\psi(x)y^{\prime-1}

\displaystyle=\varphi^{-1}(b^{-1}y)\psi(x)\varphi^{-1}(b^{-1}y)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\varphi^{-1}\left(b^{-1}y\varphi\psi(x)y^{-1}b\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi^{-1}\left(b^{-1}x^{p}b\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi^{-1}\left(\varphi(x)^{p}\right)

\displaystyle=x^{p},

which shows that
\tau^{\prime}
is indeed a
1
-cocycle.
We leave it to the reader to show that
b:\varphi\tau^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tau^{\prime}
is a
1
-coboundary, and therefore that
(\tau,b)\mapsto(\tau^{\prime},b)
is well-defined.
Once the map is seen to be well-defined, it is easily seen to be an equivalence, since we can solve for
y
in terms of
y^{\prime}
to define the inverse map.

It remains to see that projection onto the
j
’th factor yields an equivalence
Z^{1}\left(\Gamma_{W},\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J},\text{triv}}\right)\right)^{\varphi=1}\cong Z^{1}\left(\Gamma_{W},\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j})\right)
.
The point is now that since
\Gamma_{W}
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J},\text{triv}})=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j})
,
a
1
-cocycle
\tau^{\prime}:\Gamma_{W}\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J},\text{triv}})
corresponds to a collection of
1
-cocycles
\{\tau_{j}^{\prime}:\Gamma_{W}\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j})\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
,
and the coboundary
b
gives equivalences between all the different
1
-cocycles
\tau_{j}^{\prime}
,
j\in\mathcal{J}
.
We leave the remaining details to the reader.
∎

Remark 2.44
.

The above
Lemma

2.43
together with
Proposition

2.35
makes precise the statement that
a Galois type arising as descent data for Breuil–Kisin modules have the same inertial type “in each factor”, i. e. is “unmixed” in
the terminology of
[
Car+19
]
.

Remark 2.45
.

In
[
Le+23
]
they use the parametrization of tame inertial types from
[
GHS18
, Section 9]
giving 1-cocycle
\Gamma_{W}\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
.
The corresponding Galois type (they do not use this terminology) is then the corresponding element on the left hand side in
Lemma

2.43
.

2.5.5.
Genericity

According to
Section

2.5.2
, we describe our inertial type
\tau
as a point
x
in the apartment.
Our notion of
genericity
of
\tau
will be given in terms of a notion of genericity of
x
.

Definition 2.46
.

Let
y\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
and
d\in\mathbb{R}
.
We say that
y
is
d
-generic
if
for all
a\in\Phi\left(G^{*},T^{*}\right)^{+}
there exists

n_{a}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

n_{a}+\frac{d}{p}<\langle a,y-o\rangle<n_{a}+1-\frac{d}{p}.

Remark 2.47
.

The above definition depends on the prime
p
, and it is
only possible to be
d
-deep if
d<\frac{p}{2}
. This bound is not sharp: For example, if the root system is of type
A_{2}
, then it
is only possible to be
d
-deep if
d<\frac{p}{3}
(see
Fig.

3
on page
3
).

Remark 2.48
.

When
d<0
, it is vacous to say that
y
is
d
-generic.
However, it will be convient to allow negative values of
d
in order to have uniform statements, as for example in
Lemma

5.10
.

Remark 2.49
.

If
x=w^{-1}u^{\lambda}\cdot o
, where
w\in\widehat{N}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}})
and
\lambda\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}
,
then
x-o=-\frac{1}{e}w^{-1}\lambda
, so
\langle a,x-o\rangle=-\frac{1}{e}\langle wa,\lambda\rangle
.
So we see that
x
is
d
-generic if and only if for each
a\in\Phi(G^{*},T^{*})
there exists an integer
n_{a}\in\mathbb{Z}

such that
n_{a}+\frac{d}{p}<\frac{1}{e}\langle a,\lambda\rangle<n_{a}+1-\frac{d}{p}
.

We will relate our notion of genericity to that of
[
Le+23
]
, specializing to the case where
\widehat{G}=\prod_{j=0}^{f-1}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
is the dual group of

G=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{K}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
is the Weil restriction of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, where
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\subset K
is an unramified extension of degree
f
.
According to Definition 2.1.10 in loc. cit.,
a cocharacter
\mu\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})=X^{*}(T)
is said to be
d
-deep
if for each positive root
a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})^{+}
there exists
n_{a}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(12)

pn_{a}+d<\langle a,\mu+\eta\rangle<p(n_{a}+1)-d,

where
\eta
is the sum of of the fundamental dominant coweights
\omega_{a}^{\vee}
, satisfying
\langle a,\omega_{a^{\prime}}^{\vee}\rangle=\delta_{aa^{\prime}}
(Kronecker
\delta
).
Given
\mu\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})
and
s\in W
(the finite Weyl group for
\widehat{G}
),
one associates an inertial type
\tau(s,\mu+\eta):I\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
as in
[
Le+23
, Example 2.4.1]
.
Following
Remark

2.45
and
Lemma

2.22
this gives rise to a Galois type
\widetilde{\tau}:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
,
which assuming
\mathbb{F}
is sufficiently large is classified by a point
\widetilde{x}=n\cdot o\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
using
Proposition

2.29
.
We then have the following.

Lemma 2.50
.

Assume that
\mu
is
(d+1)
-deep according to (
12
) as well as “lowest alcove”, meaning that

d+1<\langle a,\mu+\eta\rangle<p-d-1

for all positive roots
a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})^{+}
.
Then
\widetilde{x}
is
d
-generic in the sense of
Definition

2.46
.

Proof.

We will follow
[
Le+23
, Example 2.4.1]
, noting that what their
fr
is our
r=[\mathbb{Q}_{q}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]=[L_{0}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
.
Following the conventions of loc. cit. we have
e=p^{r}-1
.
We can for some fixed embedding
\iota:\mathbb{F}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}
write
\tau(s,\mu+\eta)|_{I}=\iota(\omega)^{\lambda}
, where
\lambda\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})
(this
\lambda
corresponds to “
\sum_{k=0}^{fr-1}(F^{*}\circ s^{-1})^{j}(\mu+\eta)
” in loc. cit.).
For any fixed
0\leq i\leq f-1
, we can moreover write

\lambda_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\beta_{j}p^{j},

where each
\beta_{j}=s^{\prime}_{j}(\mu_{j^{\prime}}+\eta_{j^{\prime}})
for some
0\leq j^{\prime}\leq f-1
and
s^{\prime}_{j}\in W
.
The assumption that
\mu+\eta
is
(d+1)
-deep lowest alcove implies that for each
0\leq j\leq r-1
and
a\in\Phi^{\vee}(\mathrm{GL}_{n},T)^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle d+1<\langle a,\beta_{j}\rangle<p-d-1\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{ or }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,-p+d+1<\langle a,\beta_{j}\rangle<-d-1.

Take now any
a\in\Phi^{\vee}(\mathrm{GL}_{n},T)^{+}
; by
Remark

2.49
we have to show that there exists
n_{a}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
n_{a}+\frac{d}{p}<\frac{1}{e}\langle a,\lambda_{i}\rangle<n_{a}+1-\frac{d}{p}
.
We consider two cases:

(1)

d+1<\langle a,\beta_{r-1}\rangle<p-d-1
,

(2)

-p+d+1<\langle a,\beta_{r-1}\rangle<-d-1
.

Assume we are in case (1).
It will be useful to observe that

(p-d-1)\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}p^{j}=(p-d-1)\frac{p^{r}-1}{p-1}=\left(1-\frac{d}{p-1}\right)(p^{r}-1).

We have

\displaystyle\langle a,\lambda_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\langle a,\beta_{j}\rangle p^{j}

\displaystyle<(p-d-1)\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}p^{j}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{d}{p-1}\right)(p^{r}-1).

Dividing by
e=p^{r}-1
, it follows that

\frac{1}{e}\langle a,\lambda_{i}\rangle<1-\frac{d}{p-1}<1-\frac{d}{p}.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\langle a,\lambda_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\langle a,\beta_{j}\rangle p^{j}

\displaystyle>(-p+d+1)\sum_{j=0}^{r-2}p^{j}+(d+1)p^{r-1}

\displaystyle=-(p-d-1)\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}p^{j}+p^{r}

\displaystyle=-\left(1-\frac{d}{p-1}\right)(p^{r}-1)+p^{r}

\displaystyle=1+(p^{r}-1)\frac{d}{p-1}.

Dividing by
e=p^{r}-1
again, we obtain

\frac{1}{e}\langle a,\lambda_{i}\rangle>\frac{1}{e}+\frac{d}{p-1}>\frac{d}{p}.

This completes the proof in case (1), and case (2) is similar.
∎

x
o
o+(1,0,0)
o+(1,1,0)

Figure 3.

A snapshot of the apartment
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
, where
\widehat{T}\subset\mathrm{GL}_{3}
is the diagonal torus (the same one that we saw in
Fig.

1
).
We have chosen (arbitrary) parameters
p=19
and
e=36
.
The genericity is represented by the shade of red, where darker red means more generic.
We have marked an (arbitrary)
2
-generic point
x
which classifies a Galois type via
Proposition

2.23
, and in general any Galois type
\tau
will be classified by a special vertex for the apartment
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!)\right)
(i. e. at a point where the thin gray lines intersect).

2.6.
Invariant pushforward and
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors

In this section we specialize some of the discussion of
Appendix

A
, and in particular
Section

A.3
,
to the situation of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
For the definition of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors, we refer the reader to
Appendix

A
.
We will use the notation and assumptions from
Section

2.1.3
and
Section

2.2
.

Proposition 2.51
.

Let
X
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant smooth affine scheme over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
Then
(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}X
is a smooth affine group scheme over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
and
\pi_{*}^{\Gamma}X\cong(\pi_{0})_{*}^{\Gamma_{0}}\left((\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}X\right)
is a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

A.10
.
∎

Note that by descent for affine morphisms, any
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsor over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}
in the fpqc topology is representable by a smooth affine scheme over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}
.
The following lemma shows that all reasonable definitions of
type
for
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}
are equivalent and coincide with
Definition

A.4
.

Proposition 2.52
.

Let
R
be an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra,
\widehat{G}
a
\Gamma
-equivariant smooth affine group scheme over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}
, and let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists an fpqc covering
\{V_{i}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
and isomorphisms of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{V_{i}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{Q}_{V_{i}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}}
.

(2)

There exists an étale covering
\{V_{i}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
and isomorphisms of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{V_{i}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{Q}_{V_{i}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}}
.

(3)

For every geometric point
\overline{x}:\operatorname{Spec}\overline{F}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}
there exists an isomorphism of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{\overline{x}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{x}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}}
.

(4)

For every geometric point
\overline{x}:\operatorname{Spec}\overline{F}\to\operatorname{Spec}(R)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}
(the zero section) there exists an isomorphism of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{\overline{x}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{Q}_{\overline{x}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}}\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}}
.

(5)

There exists an étale covering
R\to R^{\prime}
and isomorphisms of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{R^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{Q}_{R^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}
, where subscript
R^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
