means base change to the zero section
R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
then to
R^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

(6)

(Assuming
\mathbb{F}\supseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
.) There exists an étale covering
R\to R^{\prime}
,
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and isomorphisms of
(I,\widehat{G})
-torsors
\mathcal{P}_{R^{\prime}_{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{Q}_{R^{\prime}_{j}}
, where subscript
R^{\prime}_{j}
denotes base change to
R^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}R_{j}^{\prime}
as in the previous point and then to the
j
’th component.

Proof.

This is a consequence of
Proposition

A.13
.
∎

Remark 2.53
.

The analogue of
Proposition

2.52
for
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}\to\mathfrak{S}_{R}
in place of
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}
is also true, with the same proof.

We explain how
Proposition

2.52
relates to the study of Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors.
We fix a Galois type
[\tau]\in H^{1}(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}))\cong H^{1}(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}))\cong H^{1}(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathfrak{S}_{L,\mathcal{O}}))
,
and as in
Lemma

A.3
this gives rise to a
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsor
{{}^{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}
over
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
whose underlying
\widehat{G}
-torsor is trivial.
For any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
, the
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors on
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
of
type
\tau
are those which are equivalent to (the base change of)
{{}^{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}
in the sense of any of the equivalent conditions of
Proposition

2.52
.
As a consequence of
Corollary

A.9
, the groupoid of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors over
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
of type
\tau
is equivalent to the groupoid of

\mathcal{G}
-torsors over
\mathfrak{S}_{R}
, where
\mathcal{G}=\pi_{*}^{\Gamma}{{}_{\tau}\widehat{G}}
is the invariant pushforward.
In
Section

3.1
we will identify
\mathcal{G}
with a Bruhat–Tits group scheme over
\mathfrak{S}_{R}
in the sense of Pappas–Zhu
[
PZ13
]
.
The Bruhat–Tits group scheme
\mathcal{G}
will depend on a choice of
x
corresponding to
\tau
via
Proposition

2.23
.

2.7.
Assumptions and fixed choices

In this section we summarize some of the assumptions and fixed choices of parameters that will be used in the remainder of the paper, excluding the appendices.

Throughout, we will rely on the notation introduced in
Section

2
, in particular
Section

2.1
and
Section

2.2
.
We emphasize that we only deal with
tamely
ramified extensions over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
(no wildly ramified extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
occur in this paper).

We allow the possibility that
I
acts non-trivially on
\widehat{G}
(corresponding to the case when the dual group
G
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is not unramified),
and we will state whenever we make the assumption that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
(corresponding to the case when
G
is unramified).

At various points we need to invoke certain technical assumptions on the group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
that we construct in
Section

3.1.1
.
From
Section

3.1.5
onwards we invoke
Assumption ConFib
which is that
\mathcal{G}_{x}
has connected fibers (which is automatic by
Proposition

3.7
when
\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I}
from
Section

2.2.2
is torsion-free).
For any result relying on the negative loop group
L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}
(introduced in
Section

3.3
) we invoke
Assumption Dil
(including in
Theorem

6.6
, which is one of our main results).

2.7.1.
Fixed choices

We fix a
Galois type
, by which we mean a class
[\tau]\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)
.
We assume that
\tau
is
Frobenius invariant
, meaning that
\varphi[\tau]\sim[\tau]
.
This is a condition which is satisfied by all Galois types which describe descent data for Breuil–Kisin modules in characteristic zero by
Proposition

2.35
(at least when
I
acts trivially).

We let
w\in\widehat{N}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{J}})
and
\lambda\in\left(X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}\right)^{\Gamma}\cong X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}
(where
\Gamma
acts on
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\mathcal{J}}
as in (
9
))
be elements such that
n:=w^{-1}u^{\lambda}\in\widehat{N}(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{J}}[u^{\pm 1}])
trivializes
\tau
in the sense that

\tau(\theta)=n^{-1}(^{\theta}n)
for all
\theta\in\Gamma
.
Up to isomorphism, such
w,\lambda
always exists by
Lemma

2.39
(after enlarging
E
if necessary).
Moreover, we may assume that
\tau
is
strictly
Frobenius invariant (i. e.
\varphi\tau=\tau
on the level of
1
-cocycles), or equivalently that
n\varphi(n)^{-1}
is fixed by
\Gamma
(see
Remark

2.40
).
Note that if the action of
I
fixes
w
, then
\tau|_{I}=\omega^{\lambda}
as explained in
Remark

2.31
.

Recall the Chevalley valuation
o\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
, and let

x:=n\cdot o=o-\frac{1}{e}w^{-1}\lambda.

Note that
x
is fixed by
\Gamma
, since
\theta(x)=(^{\theta}n)\cdot\theta(o)=n\tau(\theta)\cdot o=n\cdot o
, where we use that
\tau(\theta)\in\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
stabilizes
o
.

Remark 2.54
.

Note that
n
being in the normalizer of
\widehat{T}
implies that
\widehat{T}=n\widehat{T}n^{-1}
is
\Gamma
-stable.

Remark 2.55
.

For readers familiar with
[
Le+23
, Section 5.1]
, it might be instructive to consult some of the explicit examples, in particular
Section

7.1
.
As pointed out in
Remark

7.1
, our
\lambda
corresponds to
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
in
[
Le+23
, Section 5.1]
,
and similarly our
w
corresponds to
s_{\text{or}}^{\prime}
in loc. cit..

The assumption
\varphi[\tau]=[\tau]
implies by
Lemma

2.37

that there exists
c\in G^{*}(\mathcal{O}[v^{\pm 1}])
such that

c\cdot\varphi(x)=x.

Since our choice of
\tau
is strictly Frobenius invariant we may (and will) take
c=n\varphi(n)^{-1}
.

Remark 2.56
.

Almost no results rely on the fact that
c=n\varphi(n)^{-1}
, except
for
Proposition

4.11
, which gives the interpretation of the quotient stack
\mathcal{Y}:=\left[L\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\wedge\varpi}/^{c,\varphi}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\wedge\varpi}\right]
as the moduli stack of Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors of type
\tau
.

We also fix a dominant cocharacter
\mu\in X_{*}(T^{*}_{E})^{\text{dom}}
.

Table

2
summarizes the most important choices that are fixed throughout the remainder of this work, excluding appendices.

n\in\widehat{N}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u^{\pm 1}])

n=w^{-1}u^{\lambda}

\tau:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u])

\tau(\theta)=n^{-1}(^{\theta}n)

x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)

x=n\cdot o=o-\frac{1}{e}w^{-1}\lambda

c\in G^{*}(\mathcal{O}[v^{\pm 1}])

c=n\varphi(n)^{-1}
,
c\cdot\varphi(x)=x

Table 2.
Choices that are fixed for the reminder of the paper. Note that the choice of
n
controls all other entries of the table.

3.
Bruhat–Tits Group Schemes and Loop Groups

The purpose of this chapter is to introduce the various Bruhat–Tits group schemes and loop groups which play a role in the study of the stack
\mathcal{Y}
.

3.1.
Bruhat–Tits group schemes over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}

In this section we introduce the group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
and certain related group schemes.
We provide three different ways of thinking about
\mathcal{G}_{x}
:

(1)

In
Section

3.1.1
we define
\mathcal{G}_{x}
as a certain invariant pushforward, making the connection to Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors transparent (in view of
Section

1.4
, as well as
Section

2.6
and
Appendix

A
).

(2)

In
Section

3.1.2
, we identify
\mathcal{G}_{x}
with the group schemes of Pappas–Zhu
[
PZ13
]
, under the assumption that
\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})
is torsion-free.

(3)

In
Section

3.1.3
, we show that we can identify
\mathcal{G}_{x}
with a dilation of
\widehat{G}
in some cases.

We carefully describe root groups in
Section

3.1.5
, as this will be important in later computations.
In addition, we extend the construction of
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
in
Proposition

3.19
to obtain groups of the form
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
where
f
is a concave function on the root system.

Remark 3.1
.

We mention some related work that we will not address further.
In addition to
[
PZ13
]
, the paper
[
Lev16
]
constructs the groups
\mathcal{G}_{x}
for possibly wildly ramified Weil restrictions,
and related Pappas–Zhu local models.
As the group scheme of Pappas–Zhu is a Bruhat–Tits group scheme over a 2-dimensional base, the paper
[
BP24
]
also seems relevant, as it develops a theory of Bruhat–Tits group schemes over higher dimensional bases (although they focus on the case of multivariate power series rings over fields).

3.1.1.
\mathcal{G}
as invariant pushforward

View
\widehat{G},\widehat{B},\widehat{T},e
as a Chevalley group
scheme over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with the diagonal action
of the group
\Gamma
.
Let
n=w^{-1}u^{\lambda}\in\widehat{N}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u^{\pm 1}])
,
\tau
, and
x
be as in
Section

2.7.1
.
Consider the groups over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
given by
7
7

7

Suppose
\widehat{G}=\operatorname{Spec}\Lambda
and
\widehat{G}[u^{-1}]=\operatorname{Spec}\Lambda[u^{-1}]
, where
\widehat{G}[u^{-1}]:=\widehat{G}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}\setminus\{0\}}
and
\Lambda[u^{-1}]:=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u]}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u^{\pm 1}]
.
Then the automorphism
\operatorname{Ad}_{n}:\widehat{G}[u^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{G}[u^{-1}]
corresponds to an automorphism
\operatorname{Ad}_{n}^{*}:\Lambda[u^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda[u^{-1}]
.
What we mean by
n\widehat{G}n^{-1}
is then the spectrum of the subring
\operatorname{Ad}_{n}^{*}(\Lambda)\subset\Lambda[u^{-1}]
.
Similar remarks apply to
n\widehat{B}n^{-1}
and
n\widehat{T}n^{-1}
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}

\displaystyle:=n\widehat{G}n^{-1},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{x}

\displaystyle:=n\widehat{B}n^{-1},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{x}

\displaystyle:=n\widehat{T}n^{-1}=\widehat{T}.

We will often suppress the subscript
x
.
Over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
we have an isomorphism of

\Gamma
-equivariant group schemes

(13)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ad}_{n}:{{}_{\tau}}\widehat{G}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto ngn^{-1}

where
{{}_{\tau}}\widehat{G}
denotes the group scheme
\widehat{G}
with the
\tau
-twisted

\Gamma
-action

\theta\cdot g=\tau(\theta)(^{\theta}g)\tau(\theta)^{-1}
.
We have a natural identification

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}[u^{-1}]\cong\widehat{G}[u^{-1}]
over

\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, via
which we can identify the
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u]
-points of

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
as

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u]\right)=n\widehat{G}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u]\right)n^{-1}\subset\widehat{G}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u^{\pm 1}]\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u^{\pm 1}]\right).

More generally, we have similar identifications on
R
-points,
where
R
is any
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u]
-algebra in which
u
is not a zerodivisor.

Note that

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}},\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}

are all stable under the
I
-action since
\tau(\gamma)=\omega(\gamma)^{\lambda}
.
We define

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}

\displaystyle:=(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}},

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{x}

\displaystyle:=(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}},

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle:=(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}.

These are smooth affine group schemes over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
by
Proposition

2.51
.

The group schemes
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
and
\breve{\mathcal{T}}
have residual actions of

\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma/I
which are equivariant over the base

\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
We can then let

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{x}

\displaystyle:=(\pi_{0})_{*}^{\Gamma_{0}}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\cong\pi_{*}^{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle:=(\pi_{0})_{*}^{\Gamma_{0}}\breve{\mathcal{T}}\cong\pi_{*}^{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}.

Again, these are smooth affine group schemes over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
by
Proposition

2.51
.

As a consequence of (
13
) and
Corollary

A.9
, we obtain:

Corollary 3.2
.

For any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
we have equivalences of groupoids

\displaystyle\{(\Gamma,\widehat{G})\text{-torsors over }\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}\text{ of type }\tau\}\simeq

\displaystyle\{(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})\text{-torsors over }\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}\text{ of trivial type}\}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\{(\Gamma_{0},\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x})\text{-torsors over }\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}\}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{G}_{x}\text{-torsors over }\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}\},

where being of type
\tau
can be interpreted in terms of
Proposition

2.52
.

Remark 3.3
.

These equivalences can be made explicit, by inspecting the ingredients going into
Corollary

A.9
.
The first equivalence is given by the associated bundle construction using
{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}
, the trivial
\widehat{G}
torsor with
\tau
-twisted
\Gamma
-action,
and the remaining equivalences are given by invariant pushforward.
Note that these equivalences are functorial in
R
.

Remark 3.4
.

There is an analogous result of the above in which
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}
is replaced with
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
,
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{R}
is replaced with
\operatorname{Spec}\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)\llbracket v\rrbracket
, and
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}
is replaced with
\mathfrak{S}_{R}
.
This is the case of interest in the study of Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors, as in
Section

1.4
.

3.1.2.
\mathcal{G}
as the group scheme of Pappas–Zhu

The goal of this section is to identify
\mathcal{G}_{x}
as defined above with the group schemes of
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
, or more precisely give a criterion for when we can do so.
That is, we will prove the following result:

Proposition 3.5
.

Assume that
\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I}
is torsion-free.
Then
\mathcal{G}_{x}
can be identified with the Bruhat–Tits group scheme for
G^{*}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
attached to
x
in the sense of
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
.
More precisely, we have first of all identifications of buildings

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(G^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(G^{*}_{E(\!(v)\!)},E(\!(v)\!)\right).

with respect to which we will view
x
interchangeably as a member of any one of the buildings.
Now
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with connected fibers, satisfying the following properties:

(1)

\mathcal{G}_{x}[v^{-1}]=G^{*}
;

(2)

The base change of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
to
E\llbracket v\rrbracket
or
\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket
(along the obvious maps) is the parahoric group scheme for
G^{*}_{E(\!(v)\!)}
or
G^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)}
(respectively) attached to
x
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the unique smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with connected fibers for which
\mathcal{G}_{x}[v^{-1}]=G^{*}
and
\mathcal{G}_{x}(E\llbracket v\rrbracket)\subset G^{*}(E(\!(v)\!))
is the parahoric subgroup determined by
x
.

The proof of this result will be given at the end of the subsection, and as we will see the assumption on
\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I}
is used to ensure connectedness of fibers of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
The claim about identifications of buildings is covered in
[
PZ13
]
, so we will not provide a complete proof of this fact.
We will use the terminology “Bruhat–Tits group scheme in the sense of Pappas–Zhu” multiple times in the remainder of this subsection without spelling out all the required properties as we have done in
Proposition

3.5
.

We will begin our discussion over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, then over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, and finally over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
In this subsection, we will for
j\in\mathcal{J}
let
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{j}

denote the base changes of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}},\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
to
\mathcal{O}_{j}[u]
,
and let
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{j},\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{j},\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{j}
denote the base changes of
\breve{\mathcal{G}},\breve{\mathcal{B}},\breve{\mathcal{T}}
to
\mathcal{O}_{j}[v]
.

As a first step towards understanding
Proposition

3.5
, we begin by discussing identifications of the apartments from
Section

2.3
with analogously defined apartments over
E^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)
.
Following
[
PZ13
, Section 4.1]
we can for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
identify
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},E_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)
, and therefore identify

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},E^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right).

This identifies the Chevalley valuation
o
on the left hand side with the obvious Chevalley valuation
o
on the right hand side,
and it is compatible with the obvious actions of
\widehat{N}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))
on the left and right hand sides,
as well as the
\Gamma
-actions.
By taking
\Gamma
-fixed points, we have a resulting identification
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*}_{E(\!(v)\!)},E(\!(v)\!)\right)
.

Note that the point
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
can be interpreted as a point of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},E^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
,
and
x_{j}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)
as a point of
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},E_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
j\in\mathcal{J}
.
Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{j}
is the Bruhat–Tits group scheme for
\widehat{G}_{j}
attached to
x_{j}
in the sense of Pappas–Zhu.

Proof.

We note that
\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}_{j}(\!(u)\!))
acts on the building
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(\widehat{G},E_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)

by restricting the action of
\widehat{G}(E_{j}(\!(u)\!))
via the natural map
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{j}(\mathcal{O}_{j}(\!(u)\!))=\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}_{j}(\!(u)\!))\hookrightarrow\widehat{G}(E_{j}(\!(u)\!))
.
Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{j}(E_{j}\llbracket u\rrbracket)=n_{j}\widehat{G}(E_{j}\llbracket u\rrbracket)n_{j}^{-1}
and the action of
\widehat{G}(E_{j}(\!(u)\!))
on the building is compatible with the conjugation on subgroups,
it follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{j}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{j}[u]}\operatorname{Spec}E_{j}\llbracket u\rrbracket
is the parahoric group scheme attached to the hyperspecial vertex
x_{j}=n_{j}\cdot o_{j}
.
The claim then follows from the uniqueness part of the proof of
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
(in Section 4.2.1).
∎

Since the
I
-covering

\pi_{I}:\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}\to\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
restricts to an
étale
I
-torsor away from 0, we see that the fibers of

\breve{G}:=\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}[v^{-1}]
are connected reductive groups. On
the other hand, the fibers of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}
and
\breve{\mathcal{T}}

lying over the zero section
v=0
may be disconnected.
This turns out to be the only obstruction to identifying
\breve{\mathcal{G}}
with the group schemes of Pappas–Zhu.
Before proceeding, we give a criterion for connectedness of fibers.

Proposition 3.7
.

If
\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I}
(see
Section

2.2.2
for the definition) is torsion-free, then
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
has connected fibers.

Proof.

Since
I
acts diagonally on
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u]=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{O}_{j}[u]
we may without loss of generality assume that
\#\mathcal{J}=1
and ignore the
\mathcal{J}
in what follows.
By the observations above, it suffices to show that the fibers of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
over
v=0:\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}\hookrightarrow\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
are connected,
which by
[
Sta
,
Lemma 055J
]
are connected if and only if the special fiber over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}
is connected.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket
, which is a parahoric group scheme for
\widehat{G}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!)}
attached to
x
.
Let
\pi^{\prime}:\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket
sending
v
to
u^{e}
, and let
\mathcal{H}=(\pi^{\prime})_{*}^{I}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
.
Note that
\mathcal{H}
is identified with the fiber of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
over
\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket
since
\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket\cong\mathcal{O}[u]\otimes_{\mathcal{O}[v]}\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket
,
so we have to show that the special fiber of
\mathcal{H}
is connected.
For this question, we may and will replace
\mathbb{F}
by its algebraic closure.
In particular, we can then identify
\breve{G}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)}=G^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)}
.

We will now use the notation
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!))^{1},\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!))^{0},G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1},G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}
as in
[
KP23
]
.
It follows from
[
KP23
, Lemma 2.6.16 (4)]
that

(14)

(\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!))^{1})^{I}=\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!))^{1}\cap\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!))^{I}=G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}.

Let
x^{\prime}
denote the projection of
x
(which is a point of the enlarged building) to the restricted building
\mathcal{B}(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!))
.
By
[
KP23
, Lemma 7.7.10, Proposition 7.7.11]
the subgroup
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\subset\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!))
is the stabilizer of
x^{\prime}
in
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!))^{1}
.
So by (
14
), it follows that

\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket)=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket)^{I}=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\cap G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}

is the stabilizer of
x^{\prime}
in
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}
, i. e.
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket)=G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x^{\prime}}
.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is a smooth integral model for
G^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)}
, it follows by
[
KP23
, Corollary 2.10.11, Axiom 4.1.20]

that
\mathcal{H}
has connected fibers if and only if
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x^{\prime}}=G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{x^{\prime}}
.

The assumption that
\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I}
is torsion free implies that
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}=G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}
by
[
KP23
, Corollary 11.6.2]
.
In particular, it is implied that we have an equality of stabilizers
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x^{\prime}}=G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{x^{\prime}}
,
so
\mathcal{H}
has connected fibers.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

It is natural to ask whether
Proposition

3.7
has a converse.
Let us say a few word about this in the case that
x
is
0
-generic.
We will use the assumptions and notation from the proof of
Proposition

3.7
without comment.

Let
\mathcal{C}
denote the alcove (called chamber in
[
KP23
]
) containing
x^{\prime}
, and let
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{\mathcal{C}}
denote the stabilizer of
\mathcal{C}
in
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
(but not necessarily pointwise stabilizer).
Define
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{C}}:=G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{\mathcal{C}}\cap G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}
(which is consistent with
[
KP23
, Section 7.7]
),
and
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}:=G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{\mathcal{C}}\cap G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}
.
Since the action of
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
on
\mathcal{B}(G^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
is via simplicial automorphisms, we have

G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x^{\prime}}\subseteq G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{C}}
(this is where we use that
x^{\prime}
is
0
-generic).
On the other hand we have
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{x^{\prime}}=G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}
by
[
KP23
, Lemma 7.4.4, Proposition 7.6.4]
(see also
[
KP23
, Remark 7.7.7]
).
It follows from
[
KP23
, Proposition 11.6.1, Corollary 11.6.2]
that the Kottwitz homomorphism induces an isomorphism

G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{C}}/G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{\mathcal{C}}\cong\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I,\text{tor}}
.
Combining the above, we obtain a short exact sequence

(15)

1\to G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x^{\prime}}/G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{x^{\prime}}\to\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I,\text{tor}}\to G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{C}}/G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x^{\prime}}\to 1.

As in the proof of
Proposition

3.7
, connectivity of the fibers of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
is equivalent to having an equality
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x^{\prime}}=G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{x^{\prime}}
.
The exact sequence (
15
) shows that
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
may have connected fibers even if
\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I,\text{tor}}\neq 0
, but this discrepancy is accounted for
by the existence of elements
g\in G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}
which stabilize
\mathcal{C}
but not
x^{\prime}
.

Example 3.9
.

Suppose
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
with trivial
\Gamma
-action, in which case we have
G^{*}=\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
.
In this case,
Proposition

3.7
applies.
We can see this by noting that
\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})_{I}=\pi_{1}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})=\mathbb{Z}
.
Alternatively, by
Lemma

2.11

\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})_{I}
being torsion free is equivalent to
\widehat{G}_{\text{der}}=\operatorname{SL}_{n}
being simply connected.

Example 3.10
.

Let
\widehat{G}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}
, with
\Gamma
acting trivially on
\widehat{G}
, so again
G^{*}=\widehat{G}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}
.
Then we have
\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})_{I}=\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})=\mathbb{Z}/3
, and therefore
Proposition

3.7
does not apply.
For the convenience of the reader we briefly recall the computation
\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})=\mathbb{Z}/3
.
According to
[
KP23
, Definition 11.3.2]
,
\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})=X_{*}(\widehat{T})/X_{*}(\widehat{T}^{\prime})
, where
\widehat{T}\subset\operatorname{PGL}_{3}
is the diagonal torus, and
\widehat{T}^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{SL}_{3}
is the diagonal torus.
Corresponding to the identification of the cocharacters of the diagonal torus of
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
with
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, we have
X_{*}(\widehat{T}^{\prime})=\mathbb{Z}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}
(the quotient by the “diagonal”
\mathbb{Z}
), and
X_{*}(\widehat{T}^{\prime})=\{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}|a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}=0\}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
It is now straightforward to verify that the map
X_{*}(\widehat{T})/X_{*}(\widehat{T}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}/3
given by the sum, i. e.
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\mapsto a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}
, is an isomorphism.

Although we can’t use
Proposition

3.7
, we can still reason about connectivity of fibers of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
for various possible
x
.
We will do this shortly.
As in the proof of
Proposition

3.7
, we work over a fixed connected component corresponding to a choice of
j\in\mathcal{J}
.

We remark that the
Kottwitz homomorphism

\kappa:\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))\to\pi_{1}(\operatorname{PGL}_{3})=\mathbb{Z}/3
is realized as the composition of the determinant
\operatorname{det}:\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))\to\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)^{\times}/\left(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)^{\times}\right)^{3}
followed by the valuation map
\operatorname{val}:\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)^{\times}/\left(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)^{\times}\right)^{3}\to\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}=\ker\kappa
by
[
KP23
, Proposition 11.5.4]
, we have a fairly explicit description of
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}
;
for example,
\operatorname{diag}(1,v,v)\notin\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}
, because
\kappa(g)=2
.
We also have
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}=\kappa^{-1}\left(\pi_{1}(\operatorname{PGL}_{3})_{I,\text{tor}}\right)=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
by
[
KP23
, Lemma 11.5.2]
.

Following the proof of
Proposition

3.7
, connectivity of the fibers of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
boils down to whether

(16)

\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{x}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{x}

We consider a few values of
x
:

(1)

x=o
the given Chevalley valuation. Then (
16
) is satisfied by
[
KP23
, Lemma 7.7.10, Proposition 7.7.11]
, so the fibers of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
are connected.

(2)

x=o+(1/3,0,-1/3)
. Now
x
is the barycenter of the alcove
\mathcal{C}
containing it. Therefore, any
g\in\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
which stabilizes
\mathcal{C}
also stabilizes
x
.
So
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{x}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{\mathcal{C}}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{\mathcal{C}}^{\dagger}
.
Using the short exact sequence (
15
) (which holds by the same proof since
x
is
0
-generic) it follows that
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x}/\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{x}\cong\mathbb{Z}/3
, so in particular (
16
) is not satisfied, and
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
has disconnected fibers.

(3)

Let
\mathcal{C}
be as in (2), and let
x
be any point of
\mathcal{C}
which is different from the barycenter.
Consider the element

g=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&v^{-1}\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)).

Then the action of
g
on
\mathcal{A}(\operatorname{PGL}_{3},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
is a rotation through
2\pi/3
centered at the barycenter of
\mathcal{C}
.
Therefore,
g\in\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{\mathcal{C}}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{\mathcal{C}}^{\dagger}
, but since
x
is not the barycenter,
g\notin\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x}
.
In fact,
g
generates a subgroup of
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{C}}/\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x}
of order 3,
so the map
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{PGL}_{3})\to\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{C}}/\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}_{x}
from (
15
) must be an isomorphism,
and therefore
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{1}
.
So in this case, the fibers of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
are connected.

Observe that with
x
as in (2), the element
g
from (3) generates
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))_{x}/\operatorname{PGL}_{3}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))^{0}_{x}
.

Having discussed connectedness of fibers at length, we return to the issue of relating our group schemes to those of Pappas–Zhu.
Recall that
\breve{G}=(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\widehat{G}
is a connected reductive group scheme over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
For
j\in\mathcal{J}
let
\breve{G}_{j}
denote the base change to the
j
’th component
\mathcal{O}_{j}[v^{\pm 1}]
,
and recall that
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{j}
denotes the base change to the
j
’th component
\mathcal{O}_{j}[v]
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Assume that
\breve{\mathcal{G}}
has connected fibers (which holds if
\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I}
is torsion-free by
Proposition

3.7
).

(1)

Then for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
the group scheme
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{j}
is the Bruhat–Tits group scheme for
G^{*}_{j}
over
\mathcal{O}_{j}[v]
attached to
x_{j}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\left(G^{*}_{j},\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(v)\!)\right)
in the sense of Pappas–Zhu.

(2)

\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the Bruhat–Tits group scheme for
G^{*}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
attached to
x
in the sense of Pappas–Zhu.

Proof.

By the proof of
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
, the group scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{j}[v]
for
\widehat{G}_{E_{j}}
attached to
x_{j}
is the netural connected component of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}
.
Claim (1) is then obvious.
Since
\pi_{0}:\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
is an étale
\Gamma_{0}
-torsor,
\mathcal{G}_{x}=(\pi_{0})_{*}^{\Gamma_{0}}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
is a smooth affine group scheme with connected fibers.
Moreover,
\mathcal{G}_{x}[v^{-1}]=G^{*}
by construction and
\mathcal{G}_{x}(E\llbracket v\rrbracket)=\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}(E^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket)^{\Gamma_{0}}=\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}(E^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket)\cap G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))\subset\breve{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(v)\!))
is
the parahoric subgroup attached to
x
by part (1).
Claim (2) then follows from the uniqueness part of the proof of
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.5
.

Immediate from
Proposition

3.7
and
Proposition

3.11
.
∎

3.1.3.
\mathcal{G}
as dilations

In some cases, the group schemes of Pappas–Zhu coincide with certain dilations, see e. g.
[
MRR23
, Example 3.3]
.
The approach via dilations also used in
[
Le+23
]
and
[
Lee23
]
.
In
Proposition

3.16
below we describe conditions ensuring that the group schemes from
Section

3.1.1
are dilations.

We need the following definition.

Definition 3.12
.

A point
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
is said to be
lowest alcove
if

0\leq\langle a,x-o\rangle<1

for all
a\in\Phi(G^{*},T^{*})^{+}
, or equivalently, if via (
5
)
x
corresponds to an element
\beta\in X_{*}(T^{*})
with
-1<\langle a,\beta\rangle\leq 0\}
.

Remark 3.13
.

Note that
x
is
0
-generic lowest alcove if and only if
0<\langle a,x-o\rangle<1
for all
a\in\Phi(G^{*},T^{*})^{+}
.

Remark 3.14
.

In
Definition

3.12
, we have non-strict inequality
0\leq\langle a,x-o\rangle
but strict inequality
\langle a,x-o\rangle<1
.
This is intentional, because it is what we need in
Proposition

3.16
.
Note that there is a unique vertex which is lowest alcove, namely the Chevalley valuation
o
.

Remark 3.15
.

Although the group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x}
depends on
x
in absolute terms, for our purposes (studying the stack
\mathcal{Y}
which does not depend on the choice of
x
) we are free to replace
x
by any point in its
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
-orbit by
Proposition

2.23
.
Since
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
acts transitively on alcoves, it is often the case that the orbit of
x
intersects the lowest alcove.
For example, this is always true when
x
is
0
-generic.
On the other hand, this is not true if
x\neq o
is a vertex in the closure of the lowest alcove and
\widehat{G}
is a semisimple and simply connected group such as
\operatorname{SL}_{n}
.

Proposition 3.16
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, that
\mathbb{F}\supseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
\mathcal{G}_{x}
has connected fibers (e. g. if
\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})
is torsion-free by
Proposition

3.7
), and
x
is lowest alcove.
Then
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is isomorphic to the dilation of
\widehat{G}
(as a group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
) in a parabolic subgroup
\widehat{P}_{x}\subset\widehat{G}
along the zero section
v=0
.
This parabolic
\widehat{P}_{x}
is generated by
\widehat{T}
and root groups
U_{a}
where
\langle a,x-o\rangle>0
.
8
8

8

This
\widehat{P}_{x}
can also be described using the dynamic method as
\underline{P}_{\widehat{G}}(x-o)
in the notation of
[
Con14
, Theorem 4.1.7]
.

In particular, if
x
is
0
-generic, then
\widehat{P}_{x}=\widehat{B}
is the Borel subgroup.

Proof.

Since
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
and
\mathbb{F}\supseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
, we have by
Remark

2.7
that
G^{*}=\widehat{G}
,
and since
\mathcal{G}_{x}
has connected fibers,
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the group scheme of Pappas–Zhu by
Proposition

3.11
.
Now let
\widehat{P}_{x}
be as in the statement of the proposition, and define
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\prime}
as the dilation of
\widehat{G}
in
\widehat{P}_{x}
along
v=0
.
Then
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\prime}
is a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with connected fibers by
[
MRR23
, Theorem 3.2]
,
and
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\prime}(E\llbracket v\rrbracket)\subset\widehat{G}(E(\!(v)\!))
consists of elements which belong to
\widehat{P}_{x}
modulo
v
.
By interpreting
x
as a valuation of the root datum as in
Section

2.3.3
, it is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\prime}(E\llbracket v\rrbracket)

is exactly the parahoric subgroup attached to
x
(and this is where the lowest alcove assumption is used).
The result then follows from the proof of uniqueness in
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

The use of the term “parabolic” in
Proposition

3.16
is slightly abusive, because we allow the possibility that
x=o
in which case
\widehat{G}=\widehat{P}_{x}
.

3.1.4.
Some assumptions

By default, our
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is defined via an invariant pushforward as in
Section

3.1.1
.
In most of what follows, we impose assumptions on
\mathcal{G}_{x}
to ensure that we can identify
\mathcal{G}_{x}
with the group schemes of Pappas–Zhu or as a dilation.
Let us state these assumptions precisely.

Assumption ConFib
.

We assume that
\mathcal{G}_{x}
has connected fibers.

Unless otherwise stated, we will assume
Assumption ConFib
from now on.

It follows from the discussion of
Section

3.1.2

that
\mathcal{G}_{x}
has connected fibers if and only if
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
has connected fibers,
which is always satisfied when
\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I}
is torsion-free.

We will also have the following assumption, which is stronger than
Assumption ConFib
.

Assumption Dil
.

We assume that we are in the situation of
Proposition

3.16
.
That is, there is an explicitly defined parabolic
\widehat{P}_{x}\subset\widehat{G}
(where we also allow
\widehat{P}_{x}=\widehat{G}
)
such that
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the dilation of
\widehat{G}
in
\widehat{P}_{x}
along the zero section
v=0
.

By
Proposition

3.16
, this assumption is satisfied when
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
(corresponding to the assumption that
G
is unramified),

\mathbb{F}\supset\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
\mathcal{G}_{x}
has connected fibers, and
x
is lowest alcove in the sense of
Definition

3.12
(which is actually mild, see
Remark

3.15
).
Under
Assumption Dil
, we will write
\widehat{P}=\widehat{P}_{x}
,
we will write
\widehat{P}=\widehat{M}\widehat{U}
for the Levi decomposition, and
\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}\subset\widehat{G}
for the unipotent subgroup opposite to
\widehat{U}
(all defined over
\mathcal{O}
).

The above
Assumption Dil
will be used in the construction of the negative loop group in
Section

3.3
,
and consequently any result using the negative loop group will also impose this assumption.

3.1.5.
Root groups

We are now going to describe certain root groups of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
which are defined over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
Under the assumption that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
we will also describe root groups of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}
which are defined over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

For each root
a\in\Phi\left(\widehat{G},\widehat{T}\right)
we
have a root group
\widetilde{U}_{a}\subset\widehat{G}
, which we view as being over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
The pinning of
\widehat{G}
means that
we have a chosen identification

\widetilde{U}_{a}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,t]
,
and for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
it is then natural to identify

u^{n}\widetilde{U}_{a}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,u^{-n}t],

where

\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,u^{-n}t]\subset\operatorname{Frac}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,t]\right)

is a subring.
More generally, we allow
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{J}}
, in which case we allow the following abuse of notation:

\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,u^{-n}t]:=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{O}_{j}[u,u^{-n_{j}}t],

and consequently

u^{n}\widetilde{U}_{a}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,u^{-n}t]=\bigsqcup_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{j}[u,u^{-n_{j}}t].

The same remarks apply to
\breve{U}_{a}
, for dilations with respect to powers of
v
.

The root group
\widetilde{U}_{a}\subset\widehat{G}
yields a root group

\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}:=n\widetilde{U}_{wa}n^{-1}=u^{w^{-1}\lambda}\widetilde{U}_{a}u^{-w^{-1}\lambda}=u^{\langle a,w^{-1}\lambda\rangle}\widetilde{U}_{a}=u^{\langle wa,\lambda\rangle}\widetilde{U}_{a}

for
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
.

Now assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, in which case we identify
\breve{G}:=(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\widehat{G}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}\setminus\{0\}}=\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}[v^{-1}]
with
\widehat{G}_{\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}\setminus\{0\}}
.
Hence
\breve{G}
admits a canonical extension to
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, namely
\widehat{G}_{\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}}
.
The root group
U_{a}\subset\widehat{G}
then yields a root group
\breve{U}_{a}\subset\widehat{G}_{\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}}
over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
For each
a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})
, we define

\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}:=(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}\right).

We then have

\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}=\left(u^{\langle a,w^{-1}\lambda\rangle}\widetilde{U}_{a}\right)^{I}=v^{\lceil\frac{1}{e}\langle a,w^{-1}\lambda\rangle\rceil}\breve{U}_{a}=v^{\lceil-\langle a,x-o\rangle\rceil}\breve{U}_{a}.

Remark 3.18
.

One can define “root groups” for
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
more generally when

I
does not act trivially on
\widehat{G}
, analogously to the description of root groups for quasi-split connected reductive groups.
See for example
[
Zhu14
, Section 5.1]
.

3.1.6.
The open cell

The map

(17)

\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x}:=\prod_{a\in\Phi^{-}}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}\times\prod_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}

is an open immersion, and

\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
is
fiberwise dense
[
BT84
, 2.2.10, 3.9.4]
(see also
[
PZ13
, Proof of Theorem 4.1]
).
If
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, we define

\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x}:=\widetilde{\pi}_{*}^{I}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x}\right)=\prod_{a\in\Phi^{-}}\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}\times\breve{\mathcal{T}}\times\prod_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}.

By
[
CGP15
, Proposition A.5.2, Proposition A.8.10]
(see also
Proposition

2.51
)
it follows from (
17
) that the natural map
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x}\hookrightarrow\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
is also an open immersion.

3.1.7.
Root groups attached to concave functions

For any
m\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
(notation as in
[
KP23
, Section 1.6]
)
we also have the root group

\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,m}:=u^{\lceil-e\langle a,x-o\rangle+em\rceil}\widetilde{U}_{a}=u^{\langle wa,\lambda\rangle+\lceil em\rceil}\widetilde{U}_{a}.

Clearly

\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}
.

Let

f:\widehat{\Phi}\left(\widehat{G},\widehat{T}\right)\to\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
be
a concave function
[
KP23
, Definition 7.3.1]
.
We allow the possibility
f(0)>0
which corresponds to a valuation
condition on the torus, which we now describe.
Given
m^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
, we define

\mathbb{G}_{m,m^{\prime}}:=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}\left[u,t^{-1},u^{-\lceil em^{\prime}\rceil}(t-1)\right],

and for the split torus

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}=\mathbb{G}_{m}^{r}
we define

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m^{\prime}}=\mathbb{G}_{m,m^{\prime}}^{r}
.
Going back to the concave function
f
, we define

\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}:=\prod_{a\in\Phi^{-}}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f(a)}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{f(0)}\times\prod_{{a}\in\Phi^{+}}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f(a)}.

Now assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
.
Then we define

\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f}:=(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f(a)}\right)=v^{\lceil-\langle a,x-o\rangle+f(a)\rceil}\breve{U}_{a}.

We also define

\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{m}:=\widetilde{\pi}_{*}^{I}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}\right).

Again, since
I
acts trivially we can actually identify
\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{m^{\prime}}=\mathbb{G}_{m,\lceil m^{\prime}\rceil}^{r}
, where
r
is the rank of
\widehat{T}
.
Indeed, we can verify this as follows when
\widehat{T}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
:

\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{m}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket\right)=\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket\right)\right)^{I}=\left(1+u^{\lceil em\rceil}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket\right)^{I}=1+v^{\lceil m\rceil}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket.

Finally, we define

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}

\displaystyle:=(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\right)=\prod_{a\in\Phi^{-}}\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f}\times\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{f(0)}\times\prod_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f}.

3.1.8.
The group schemes
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}

We can construct group schemes
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
for a concave function
f
, extending
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]

in our situation.

Proposition 3.19
.

Assume that
f\geq 0
. Then we can construct a smooth affine group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with connected fibers with the following properties:

(1)

The group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}|_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}\setminus\{0\}}
is the group scheme
G^{*}
.

(2)

The base change of
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
to
\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket
or
E\llbracket v\rrbracket
is the smooth integral model of
G^{*}_{\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket}
or

G^{*}_{E\llbracket v\rrbracket}
(respectively) attached to
x
and
f
(in the sense of
Section

2.3.3
and
[
KP23
, Theorem 8.5.2]
).

Moreover,
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
is the unique smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with connected fibers such that
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}[v^{-1}]=G^{*}

and
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(E\llbracket v\rrbracket)\subset G^{*}(E(\!(v)\!))
is the subgroup which is denoted
G^{*}(E(\!(v)\!))_{x,f}
in
[
KP23
, Definition 7.3.3]
.

Proof.

The uniqueness follows from the proof of uniqueness in
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
.
As the proof there shows,
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
is already uniquely characterized by (1) and the
E
part of (2).

For the remainder of the proof, we show the analogous statement for
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, from which the statement will follow by taking invariant pushforward.
To simplify notation we assume that
\#\mathcal{J}=1
and ignore the
\mathcal{J}
.
We also carry out the proof under the assumption that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, so that the root groups of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
are defined.
But as in
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
, the general case follows by arguing instead with
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
and passing to the netural connected component invariant pushforward (although the passage to neutral connected components is likely redundant by
Assumption ConFib
).

By the induction argument used in the proof of
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
, based on
[
Yu02
]
,
9
9

9

In fact, the motivation of
[
Yu02
]
appears to have been the construction of smooth integral models like
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
, where we allow
f\geq 0
to be a concave function.
The proof of
[
KP23
, Theorem 8.5.2]
is also based on Yu’s method.

it suffices to show that if
0\leq f\leq f^{\prime}\leq f+1
then we can describe
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f^{\prime}}
as a dilation of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
.
More precisely, our induction hypothesis is that:

(A)

\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
satisfies the properties of the proposition,

(B)

\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\hookrightarrow\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is open, and dense in geometric fibers,

The base case with
f(0)=0
is taken care of by the proof of
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
, so we will assume that
f^{\prime}(0)>0
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
denote the base change of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
along the section
v=0:\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}\hookrightarrow\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, and similarly for other related schemes over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
Let
Q
be the set theoretic image of
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}\to\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
.
The crux of the proof is to show that
Q\subset\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is a closed subgroup, from which it will follow similarly to the argument in
[
PZ13
, 177-178]

that
\mathcal{G}_{x,f^{\prime}}
is the dilation of
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
in
Q
along
v=0
.

Let
F\in\{\overline{E},\overline{\mathbb{F}}\}
, and note that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}(F\llbracket v\rrbracket)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}(F\llbracket v\rrbracket)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}(F),}

where the hooked arrows are injective and the two-headed arrows are surjective (by smoothness).
The assumption
f^{\prime}(0)>0
implies that
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}(F\llbracket v\rrbracket)\subset\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}(F\llbracket v\rrbracket)
is a subgroup by
[
KP23
, Proposition 7.3.12]
.
Therefore the points underlying
Q
are closed under the group operations.

Observe that
\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f}
is a dilation (or Néron blowup
[
MRR23
, Definition 3.1]
) for each
a\in\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})
,
10
10

10

The hat over
\widehat{\Phi}
means that we allow
a=0
, in which case
\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,m}=\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{m}
.

using the explicit descriptions from
Section

3.1.7
and the assumption
f^{\prime}\leq f+1
.
More precisely, it is the dilation in the identity section along
v=0
if there occurs a jump in the filtration at
a
, and the trivial dilation otherwise.
Since dilations are compatible with products it follows that
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}\subset\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}
is also a dilation.
In fact, it is precisely the dilation of
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}
in
Q
along
v=0
.

By making the previous paragraph explicit we can see that the image
Q_{a}
of
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f^{\prime}}\to\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,f}
is closed for each
a\in\widehat{\Phi}(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})
.
It follows that
Q^{\prime}=\prod_{a<0}Q_{a}\times Q_{0}\times\prod_{a>0}Q_{a}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}
is also closed.
Note that
Q
is now the image of the multiplication map
Q^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
.
If
f(0)>0
, then
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\cong\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
by (B) and
[
KP23
, Proposition 7.3.12]
, so
Q
is closed in
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
by the above.
But if
f(0)=0
we need an additional argument.

Let
\widetilde{Q}
be the closure of
Q
in
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
.
Then
Q_{E}=\widetilde{Q}_{E}
and
Q_{\mathbb{F}}
is the neutral connected component of
\widetilde{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}
, since
the fibers of
Q
are closed by
[
Bor91
, I Proposition 1.3]
, and
Q
is open in
\widetilde{Q}
since
Q
is closed in
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}

(which is open in
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
by (B)).
It therefore suffices to show that the fibers of
\widetilde{Q}
are geometrically connected, but this follows from the argument in
[
PZ13
, 177]
.
This completes the proof that
Q
is a closed subgroup of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
.

Now define
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f^{\prime}}
as the dilation of
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
in
Q
along
v=0
.
We have to check that
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f^{\prime}}
satisfies properties (A), (B).
General properties of Néron blowups
[
MRR23
, Theorem 3.2]
dictate that
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f^{\prime}}
is a smooth affine group scheme over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with connected fibers, and
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f^{\prime}}[v^{-1}]=\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}[v^{-1}]=G^{*}
.
Note that
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}
is the dilation of
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}
in
Q
along
v=0
.
Since
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\hookrightarrow\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is open and dense in geometric fibers, it follows by
[
MRR23
, Corollary 2.8]

that so is
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f^{\prime}}
, verifying property (B).
In fact, the universal property of dilations
[
MRR23
, Proposition 2.6]
and the fact that
Q\subset\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}
imply that if
R
is a
\mathcal{O}[v]
-algebra in which
v
is not a zerodivisor and which is
v
-adically complete,
11
11

11

We can relax the assumption that
R
is
v
-adically complete – it suffices that (the image of)
v
is contained in the Jacobson radical of
R
.
The crucial part is that the only open subset of
\operatorname{Spec}R
containing
\operatorname{Spec}R/vR
is
\operatorname{Spec}R
itself.
Since
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\subset\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is open by (B), it then follows that
\operatorname{Spec}R\to\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
factors through
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}

if and only if
\operatorname{Spec}R/vR\to\operatorname{Spec}R\to\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
factors through
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}
. Consequently, we have
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}(R)=\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}(R/vR)\times_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}(R/vR)}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}(R)
, so

\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}(R)=Q(R/vR)\times_{\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}(R/vR)}\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}(R)=Q(R/vR)\times_{\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}(R/vR)}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}(R)=\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f^{\prime}}(R)
.

then
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}(R)=\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f^{\prime}}(R)
.
In particular, for
F\in\{E,\mathbb{F}\}
we have by definition of
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}
and
[
KP23
, Proposition 7.3.12]
that
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f^{\prime}}(F\llbracket v\rrbracket)\subset\widehat{G}(F(\!(v)\!))
is the subgroup attached to
x
and
f
.
This shows that property (2) holds, finalizing the proof of (A).
∎

Remark 3.20
.

We will use the notation
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
which are to
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
, respectively, what
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
is to
\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
In fact,
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is exactly as in the proof above.

We point out a few special cases of
Proposition

3.19
.
In fact, these are more or less the only cases we will need.

Example 3.21
.

Suppose
f=n
. For any
\mathcal{O}[v]
-algebra
R
we then have

\mathcal{G}_{x,n}(R)=\ker\left(\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)\to\mathcal{G}_{x}(R/v^{n}R)\right).

To see this, note that the right hand side is a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with connected fibers by the proof of
Lemma

B.3
.
It has the required property over
E\llbracket v\rrbracket
by
[
KP23
, Proposition 8.5.16]
, so by the uniqueness in
Proposition

3.19
it must agree with
\mathcal{G}_{x,n}
.

Example 3.22
.

Impose
Assumption Dil
.
Recall that under this assumption, the group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x}=\mathcal{G}_{x,0}
is the dilation of
\widehat{G}
in
\widehat{P}_{x}
along
v=0
. Then for
f=0^{+}
, the group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x,0^{+}}
is the dilation of
\widehat{G}
in
\widehat{U}
along
v=0
.

When
f>0
, the group schemes
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
has unipotent special fiber:

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
f>0
be a concave function.
Then
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}\times_{v=0}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}
is a split unipotent group scheme over
\mathbb{F}
.
12
12

12

Recall that being
split unipotent
means being an iterated extension of
\mathbb{G}_{a}
.

Proof.

By construction,
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket
is the smooth integral model for
G^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)}
attached to
x
and
f

in the sense of
[
KP23
, Theorem 8.5.2]

The special fiber of this group scheme, which coincides with
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}\times_{v=0}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}
, is unipotent by
[
KP23
, Corollary 8.5.12]
,
and in fact split unipotent by
[
Bor91
, Corollary 15.5.ii]
since
\mathbb{F}
is finite (and in particular perfect).
∎

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
f>0
be a concave function.
Let
R
be a
p
-adically complete
\mathcal{O}
-algebra.
Then all
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsors on
R\llbracket v\rrbracket
are trivial.
Moreover, if
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}:=\mathcal{G}_{x,f}\times_{v=0}\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}
, then all
\overline{G}_{x,f}
-torsors on
R
are trivial.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsor over
\operatorname{Spec}R\llbracket v\rrbracket
.
Then
\mathcal{P}
is smooth affine by descent, so
\mathcal{P}(R\llbracket v\rrbracket)\neq\emptyset

only if
\mathcal{P}(R)\neq\emptyset
only if
\mathcal{P}(R/\varpi R)\neq\emptyset
(by
p
-adic completeness of
R
).
Note that
\mathcal{P}(R/\varpi R)
is also the set of sections of
\mathcal{P}\times_{v=0}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}
, which is a torsor for the group
C:=\mathcal{G}_{x,f}\times_{v=0}\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}
.
This set of sections is non-empty since
C
is unipotent by
Proposition

3.23
, and all torsors for a unipotent group scheme over an affine space are trivial.
This fact is proved in
[
Gro58
, Propopsition 1]
, but for convenience of the reader we give a short argument.
The vanishing of
H^{1}_{\et}\left(\operatorname{Spec}R/\varpi R,C\right)
follows from the vanishing of
H^{1}_{\et}\left(\operatorname{Spec}R/\varpi R,\mathbb{G}_{a}\right)=H^{1}\left(\operatorname{Spec}R/\varpi R,\mathcal{O}_{R/\varpi R}\right)=0
,
where the first equality is due to
[
Sta
,
Proposition 03DW
]
, and
the second due to Serre vanishing
[
Sta
,
Lemma 01XB
]
.
∎

3.2.
Loop groups

We will now discuss various loop groups for the groups constructed in
Section

3.1
.
Some general properties of loop groups are also recalled in
Appendix

B
.

3.2.1.
Positive loop groups

Given a scheme
X
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we define two functors on
\mathcal{O}
-algebras

\displaystyle L^{+}_{0}X(R)

\displaystyle=X(R\llbracket v\rrbracket),

\displaystyle L^{+}X(R)

\displaystyle=X(R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket),

where
R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
is viewed as an
\mathcal{O}[v]
-algebra via
v\mapsto v
.
These loop schemes are the specializations of the global loop scheme
[
PZ13
, Section 6.2.4]
at
v=0
and
v=-p
, respectively.

Note that if
R
is
p
-adically complete,
then
R\llbracket v\rrbracket=R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
by
Section

2.1.4
,
and consequently
L^{+}_{0}X(R)=L^{+}X(R)
.
But this property notably fails for
R=E
(and more generally if
p\in R^{\times}
).

Similarly, we define positive loop groups for schemes over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
as well.
That is, if
X
is defined over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we set

\displaystyle L^{+}_{0}X(R)

\displaystyle=X\left(\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)\llbracket v\rrbracket\right),

\displaystyle L^{+}X(R)

\displaystyle=X\left(\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\right).

Similarly, if
X
is defined over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we set

\displaystyle L^{+}_{0}X(R)

\displaystyle=X\left(\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)\llbracket u\rrbracket\right),

\displaystyle L^{+}X(R)

\displaystyle=X\left(\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)\llbracket u^{e}+p\rrbracket\right),

where we remind the reader that
\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)\llbracket u^{e}+p\rrbracket:=\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)[u]^{\wedge(u^{e}+p)}
.
Note that when
R
is
p
-adically complete, we have
\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)\llbracket u^{e}+p\rrbracket=\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)\llbracket u\rrbracket
by
Lemma

2.5
.

3.2.2.
Congruence loop groups

If
H
is a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we define for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
the jet groups

\displaystyle L^{n}_{0}H(R)

\displaystyle=H(R[v]/v^{n}),

\displaystyle L^{n}H(R)

\displaystyle=H(R[v+p]/(v+p)^{n}).

We then define the congruence loop groups
L^{+n}_{0}H
and
L^{+n}H
as the kernels of the short exact sequences

(18)

\displaystyle 1\to L^{+n}_{0}H\to L^{+}_{0}H\to L^{n}_{0}H\to 1,

\displaystyle 1\to L^{+n}H\to L^{+}H\to L^{n}H\to 1,

where we note that these sequences are right exact by smoothness of
H
.

If
H
is defined over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we define
L^{n}_{0}H
and
L^{n}H
similarly as above.
But if
H
is defined over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we define

\displaystyle L^{n}_{0}H(R)

\displaystyle=H\left(\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)[u]/u^{en}\right),

\displaystyle L^{n}H(R)

\displaystyle=H\left(\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)[u]/(u^{e}+p)^{n}\right).

In both cases (
H
defined over
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
or
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
), we define
L^{+n}_{0}H
and
L^{+}H
as kernels as above.

Even when
R\llbracket v\rrbracket=R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
, we typically have
L^{+n}_{0}H(R)\neq L^{+n}H(R)
, for the simple reason that
R\llbracket v\rrbracket/v^{n}\neq R\llbracket v\rrbracket/(v+p)^{n}
.
The following lemma enables us to compare these congruence subgroups when
p^{a}R=0
.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
R
be a
\mathbb{Z}/p^{a}
-algebra where

a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. If

n\geq a
, then

\displaystyle L^{+n}H(R)

\displaystyle\subset L^{+n-a+1}_{0}H(R),

\displaystyle L^{+n}_{0}H(R)

\displaystyle\subset L^{+n-a+1}H(R).

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we also have

L^{+p^{a-1}n}_{0}H(R)=L^{+p^{a-1}n}H(R).

Proof.

The first statement follows from the fact that

(v+p)^{n}\in v^{n-a+1}R[v]
and
v^{n}\in(v+p)^{n-a+1}R[v]
.
Both of these facts are consequences of the binomial theorem. Another
consequence of the binomial theorem is the identity

(v+p)^{p^{a-1}}\equiv v^{p^{a-1}}\mod p^{a}
, which implies the
second statement.
∎

We will often use the following result without comment.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and let
\mathcal{G}_{x,n}
denote the group scheme from
Proposition

3.19
.
Then
L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,n}=L^{+n}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
Similarly,
L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,n}=L^{+n}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
and
L^{+}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x,n}=L^{+n}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
.

Proof.

Immediate from
Example

3.21
,
Lemma

B.3
and the definitions.
∎

3.2.3.
Lie algebras of congruence loop groups

We also make some remarks about the Lie algebras of congruence loop groups.
Let
J\to R\to\overline{R}
be a square zero extension of

\mathcal{O}
-algebras. The are two natural ways to interpret the
Lie algebra
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}(J)
, either as the
Lie algebra of the congruence loop group
L^{+n}\mathcal{G}
, or as
the kernel of

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)\to\operatorname{Lie}L^{n}\mathcal{G}(J)
.
In fact these two definitions agree by commutativity of the following
diagram:

\begin{CD}\operatorname{Lie}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}(J)@>{}>{}>L^{+n}\mathcal{G}(R)@>{}>{}>L^{+n}\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)@>{}>{}>L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)@>{}>{}>L^{+}\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\operatorname{Lie}L^{n}\mathcal{G}(J)@>{}>{}>L^{n}\mathcal{G}(R)@>{}>{}>L^{n}\mathcal{G}(\overline{R}).\end{CD}

Indeed, by the 9-lemma the topmost horizontal row is exact if and only if the leftmost vertical column is exact.

It is clear that the above remarks also apply to
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+n}_{0}\mathcal{G}(J)
.

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
J\to R\to\overline{R}
be a square zero extension, where
pJ=0
.
Assume that
R
and
\overline{R}
are
p
-adically complete so that
L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)=L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}(R)
as explained above.
Then there is a natural identification

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}(J)\cong\operatorname{Lie}L^{+n}_{0}\mathcal{G}(J)

and in fact these are equal as subgroups of
L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)
.

Intuitively this is the fact that
v+p=v
when
p=0
.

Proof.

The map

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)\to\operatorname{Lie}L^{n}\mathcal{G}(J)

corresponds to the map

\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}(J\llbracket v\rrbracket)\to\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}(J[v]/(v+p)^{n})

given by functoriality of
\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}
in square
zero extensions. When
pJ=0
, then
J[v]/(v+p)^{n}=J\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}[v]/(v+p)^{n}=J\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}[v]/v^{n}=J[v]/v^{n}
,
so

1\to\operatorname{Lie}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}(J)\to\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}(J\llbracket v\rrbracket)\to\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}(J[v]/v^{n})\to 1

is exact, implying that
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}(J)
is
also the congruence subgroup with respect to
v^{n}
.
∎

3.2.4.
Iwahori decomposition

We will need Iwahori decomposition in the following form.

Proposition 3.28
.

Let
f\geq 0
be a concave function (possibly zero).
The map
L^{+}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\hookrightarrow L^{+}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is an open immersion,
and if
f>0
or
x
is
0
-generic, then it is an isomorphism.
Similarly, if
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, then the map
L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\hookrightarrow L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is an open immersion,
and if
f>0
or
x
is
0
-generic, then it is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We will prove the latter statement under the assumption that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, and the former statement can be proved similarly.
If
f=0
then the map
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\hookrightarrow\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is an open immersion by
Section

3.1.6
,
and in general
\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\hookrightarrow\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is an open immersion by the proof of
Proposition

3.19
.
By
Proposition

B.1
it follows that
L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\hookrightarrow L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is an open immersion.

Now assume that
f(0)>0
or
x
is
0
-generic.
To check if the open immersion
L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\hookrightarrow L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}
is an isomorphism,
it suffices to show surjectivity on
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
and
\overline{E}
-points, where it is a consequence of
[
KP23
, Proposition 7.3.12]
(Iwahori decomposition).
∎

Corollary 3.29
.

Let
f\geq 0
be a concave function.
Assume that
f>0
or
x
is
0
-generic.
Then
L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}=\left(L^{+}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f}\right)^{\Gamma}
.
If moreover
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
,
then
L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}=(L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,f})^{\Gamma_{0}}
.

3.2.5.
Quotient loop groups

We will identify the quotient
L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}/L_{0}^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
in the following cases:

(a)

f=n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

(b)

f=0^{+}
and
x
is 0-generic.

For case (a), we have by (
18
) and
Lemma

3.26

L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}/L_{0}^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,n}\cong L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}/L^{+n}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}\cong L^{n}\mathcal{G}_{x},

where
L^{n}\mathcal{G}_{x}=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathcal{O}[v]/v^{n}}_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{G}_{x}
, and in particular this is a smooth affine group scheme.

For case (b), consider the torus
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
which is the restriction of
\mathcal{T}
to the fiber
v=0
.
Let
h:L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\overline{\mathcal{T}}
be given as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h:L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\prod_{a<0}L^{+}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}\times L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}\times\prod_{a>0}L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}\right)^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}^{+}\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{T}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mod v}

where the first identification is a consequence of
Corollary

3.29
and the fact that
x
is
0
-generic.
Note that
\operatorname{pr}
is a group homomorphism because the conjugation action of the torus normalize the root groups, and consequently so is
h
.
The kernel of
h
is seen to be
L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,0^{+}}
by
Corollary

3.29
again,
and surjectivity of the reduction mod
v
map follows from smoothness of
\mathcal{T}
.
To summarize, we have have an isomorphism

(19)

L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}/L_{0}^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,0^{+}}\cong\overline{\mathcal{T}}.

Moreover, the map
L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}\twoheadrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{T}}
has a canonical section, which ultimately comes from the section
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{O}\llbracket v\rrbracket

of the reduction mod
v
map.

Remark 3.30
.

If
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
and
\mathbb{F}\supseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
, then we identify
\overline{\mathcal{T}}=\widehat{T}
.

Remark 3.31
.

For a random concave function
f\geq 0
, it is not always the case that
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
is a normal subgroup of
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}
, but
this is true whenever
f
is constant by
[
KP23
, Proposition 7.3.17]
.

3.2.6.
Loop groups

Recall that
\mathcal{G}_{x}[v^{-1}]=G^{*}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}[u^{-1}]=\widehat{G}
.
We define a functors on
\mathcal{O}
-algebras

\displaystyle L_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=G^{*}\left(R(\!(v)\!)\right),

\displaystyle L\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R(\!(v+p)\!)\right),\,\,\,\,\,\text{where }R(\!(v+p)\!)=R[v]^{\wedge(v+p)}\left[(v+p)^{-1}\right],

\displaystyle L_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}(R)=\widehat{G}\left(\left(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)(\!(u)\!)\right).

Note that
L_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}(R)
has an obvious
\Gamma
-action, and
L_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=L_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}(R)^{\Gamma}
.

Remark 3.32
.

If
p^{a}R=0
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, then
R(\!(v)\!)=R(\!(v+p)\!)
by
Lemma

2.5
, and consequently
L\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=L_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=L_{0}G^{*}(R)
.

3.3.
The negative loop group

In this section we introduce the negative loop group
L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}
, which is in some sense “complementary” to the positive loop group
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
We will use it to construct convenient open charts for the affine Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}
that we will introduce later on.
For reasons that will soon become apparent, we impose
Assumption Dil
throughout this section.

For the constant group
\widehat{G}
, one can define the negative loop group as

(20)

L^{--}\widehat{G}(R)=\ker\left(\widehat{G}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v=\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{G}(R)\right),

where
R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\subset R(\!(v+p)\!)
is the subring of polynomials in the variable
\frac{1}{v+p}
.
Then the multiplication map
L^{+}\widehat{G}\times L^{--}\widehat{G}\to L\widehat{G}
is an open immersion, as shown for example in
[
Zhu16
, Lemma 2.3.5]
.
This fact is useful, as it implies that
L^{--}\widehat{G}
-translates give open charts of the affine Grassmannian
L^{+}\widehat{G}\backslash L\widehat{G}
.

In the present case, we want a negative loop group
L^{--}\mathcal{G}\subset L\mathcal{G}
such that the multiplication map
L^{+}\mathcal{G}\times L^{--}\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow L\mathcal{G}
is an open immersion.
But we can not use (
20
) since the symbol
\mathcal{G}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)
is not a priori defined.
One possibility of making sense of this symbol is to make an appropriate extension of
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
Even still, it is not clear which extension of
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
one should choose; roughly speaking, such an extension corresponds to a choice of parahoric structure at
v=\infty
.
Our idea is to choose the parahoric structure at
v=\infty
which is perfectly complementary to the parahoric structure at
v=0
.

The point
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
gives a valuation of root datum as we explained in
Section

2.3.3
.
That is, for each root
a\in\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})
, the point
x
describes
lower bounds
for the valuations of elements in the root group
U_{a}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))\subset\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
, and the parahoric subgroup corresponding to
x
is generated by elements satisfying this bound.
We can then think of this same point
x
as describing complementary
upper bounds
for the ‘‘antivaluations’’
13
13

13

If
\operatorname{val}:\mathbb{F}(\!(v^{-1})\!)\to\mathbb{Z}
is the discrete valuation with
\operatorname{val}(v^{-1})=1
, then the “antivaluation” is
-\operatorname{val}
.

of elements in the root group
U_{a}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v^{-1})\!))\subset\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v^{-1})\!))
, giving a parahoric subgroup of
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v^{-1})\!))
.
If
\eta:=x-o\in X(\widehat{T})_{\mathbb{R}}
, so that
x_{0}:=x=o+\eta\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
,
then the above parahoric subgroup of
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v^{-1})\!))
is described by the point
x_{\infty}:=o-\eta\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v^{-1})\!)\right)
.

We define
\mathcal{G}_{x}
over
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
by gluing the group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x}=\mathcal{G}_{x_{0}}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}[v]
with
the “complemenary” group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x_{\infty},0^{+}}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O},\infty}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}[v^{-1}]
.
Because of
Assumption Dil
, the group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x}
over
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
is the dilation of the constant group scheme
\widehat{G}
over
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
in
\widehat{P}
at
v=0
and in
\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}
at
v=\infty
.

For any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
we set

L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R):=\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right).

Here, we think of
\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]
as
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{-p\}
,
and we will discuss this identification in more detail in the proof of
Lemma

3.35
.
The functor
L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}
is an Ind-scheme over
\mathcal{O}
.

Example 3.33
.

When
x=o
is the Chevalley valuation,
\mathcal{G}_{x}=\widehat{G}
as a group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
Over
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we see that
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the dilation of
\widehat{G}
in the identity section along
v=\infty
.
By the universal property of dilations,
L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}=L^{--}\widehat{G}
is then compatible with (
20
).

Remark 3.34
.

Although we have now defined
\mathcal{G}_{x}
as a group scheme over
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we remark that for any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
,
the maps from
\operatorname{Spec}R\llbracket v\rrbracket
,
\operatorname{Spec}R(\!(v)\!)
,
\operatorname{Spec}R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
, or
\operatorname{Spec}R(\!(v+p)\!)
into
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
factor through
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
Hence the symbols
L_{0}^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}
,
L_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}
,
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}
, and
L\mathcal{G}_{x}
are not ambiguous, i. e. the extension of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
does not affect these (loop group) functors in any way.

The following result shows that our definition is compatible with
[
Le+23
, Definition 3.2.1]
.

Lemma 3.35
.

For any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
we have
14
14

14

Note that
\frac{v}{v+p}=1-\frac{p}{v+p}\in R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]
, and if we set
\tilde{t}=\frac{1}{v+p}
, then
R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]/\frac{v}{v+p}R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]=R[\tilde{t}]/(1-p\tilde{t})=R\left[\frac{1}{p}\right]
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{A\in\widehat{G}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right):A\mod\frac{1}{v+p}\in\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}(R)\,\,\text{ and }\,\,A\mod\frac{v}{v+p}\in\widehat{P}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{p}\right]\right)\right\}.

In particular, if
p
is nilpotent in
R
, we have

\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)=\left\{A\in\widehat{G}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right):A\mod\frac{1}{v+p}\in\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}(R)\right\}.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by computing sections in various affine charts of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}
.
We have closed subschemes
v=0,v=-p,v=\infty:\operatorname{Spec}R\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}
which we will just denote
\{0\},\{-p\},\{\infty\}
, respectively.
Consider the two coordinates on the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{\infty\}
given by
v
and
t=v+p
centered at
0
and
-p
(respectively).
We can then identify:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{\infty\}=\operatorname{Spec}R[v]=\operatorname{Spec}R[v+p],

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{0\}=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v}\right],

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{-p\}=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{t}\right]=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right],

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{\infty,-p\}=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[v+p,\frac{1}{v+p}\right]=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[v,\frac{1}{v+p}\right],

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{\infty,0,-p\}=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[v,\frac{1}{v},\frac{1}{v+p}\right],

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{0,-p\}=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v+p},\frac{v+p}{v}\right]=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v},\frac{v}{v+p}\right]=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v},\frac{1}{v+p}\right].

Note that the closed subscheme
\{0\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{-p\}
corresponds to the ideal
\frac{v}{v+p}R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\subset R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]
.
Indeed, the intersection
R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\cap vR\left[v,\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\subset R\left[v,\frac{1}{v+p}\right]
is the principal ideal
\left(\frac{v}{v+p}\right)=\left(1-\frac{p}{v+p}\right)
,
and we can see this as follows.
Suppose that
a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\in R
and
a_{0}+\frac{a_{1}}{v+p}+\cdots+\frac{a_{n}}{(v+p)^{n}}\in vR\left[v,\frac{1}{v+p}\right]
, where we may assume that
a_{n}\neq 0
.
Then we can write

(21)

a_{0}+\frac{a_{1}}{v+p}+\cdots+\frac{a_{n}}{(v+p)^{n}}=\frac{vg(v)}{(v+p)^{k}}

for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and polynomial
g(v)\in R[v]=R[v+p]
, where
g(-p)\neq 0
.
By rewriting the right hand side as a Laurent polynomial in
v+p
, we find that
n=k
.
Multiplying (
21
) by
(v+p)^{n}
and setting
v=0
we obtain
p^{n}a_{0}+p^{n-1}a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}=0
, and therefore

a_{0}+\frac{a_{1}}{v+p}+\cdots+\frac{a_{n}}{(v+p)^{n}}=\left(1-\frac{p}{v+p}\right)\left(a_{0}+\frac{pa_{0}+a_{1}}{v+p}+\cdots+\frac{p^{n-1}a_{0}+p^{n-2}a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n-1}}{(v+p)^{n-1}}\right).

We now compute sections of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
, first in the affine chart
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{\infty,-p\}=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[(v+p)^{\pm 1}\right]
.
Since
v
is a nonzerodivisor in
R\left[(v+p)^{\pm 1}\right]=R\left[v,(v+p)^{-1}\right]
, we have by the universal property of dilations that

\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[(v+p)^{\pm 1}\right]\right)=\left\{A\in\widehat{G}\left(R\left[(v+p)^{\pm 1}\right]\right):A\mod v\in\widehat{P}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{p}\right]\right)\right\}.

Next, we compute sections in the affine chart
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}\setminus\{0,-p\}=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v+p},\frac{1}{v}\right]
.
Using the universal property of dilations again we see that

\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p},\frac{1}{v}\right]\right)=\left\{A\in\widehat{G}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p},\frac{1}{v}\right]\right):A\mod\frac{1}{v+p}\in\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}\left(R\right)\right\},

where we note that
\left(\frac{1}{v+p}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{v}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{v+p},\frac{1}{v}\right)\subset R\left[\frac{1}{v+p},\frac{1}{v}\right]
(the ideal corresponding to
v=\infty
), since
\frac{1}{v}\left(1-\frac{p}{v+p}\right)=\frac{1}{v+p}
and
\frac{1}{v}=\frac{1}{v+p}\left(1+\frac{p}{v}\right)
.
By using the Zariski open cover
