\operatorname{Spec}R\left[(v+p)^{\pm 1}\right]\cup\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v+p},\frac{v+p}{v}\right]=\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]
,
we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)=\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[(v+p)^{\pm 1}\right]\right)\cap\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p},\frac{v+p}{v}\right]\right),

where the intersection takes place in
\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[v,\frac{1}{v+p},\frac{1}{v}\right]\right)
.
Combining the above, we see that the sections of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
take the described form.
∎

Our next goal is to show that
L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}
indeed has the expected property that
L^{--}\mathcal{G}\times L^{+}\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow L\mathcal{G}
is an open immersion.
Following the strategy of
[
Zhu16
, Lemma 2.3.5]
, we prove that (1)
L^{--}\mathcal{G}\cap L^{+}\mathcal{G}=\{1\}
, with the intersection taking place in
L\mathcal{G}
,
and (2)
\operatorname{Lie}L^{--}\mathcal{G}+\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}=\operatorname{Lie}L\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 3.36
.

We have
L^{+}\mathcal{G}\cap L^{--}\mathcal{G}=\{1\}
on noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, i. e.
L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)\cap L^{--}\mathcal{G}(R)=\{1\}
for any noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
.

Proof.

Let
R
be a noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebra.
Then
\operatorname{Spec}R\llbracket v\rrbracket\cup\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v}\right]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}
is an fpqc covering by
[
Sta
,
Lemma 00MB
]
, so we get a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}_{x}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v}\right]\right)\times\mathcal{G}_{x}(R\llbracket v\rrbracket)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}(R(\!(v)\!))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{G}(R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{G}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v}\right]\right)\times\widehat{G}(R\llbracket v\rrbracket)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{G}(R(\!(v)\!))}}

in which both rows are equalizers. Note that
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R})=\widehat{G}(R)
since
\widehat{G}
is a constant affine scheme.
By construction,
\widehat{G}(R)\cap\mathcal{G}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v}\right]\right)=\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}(R)
and
\widehat{G}(R)\cap\mathcal{G}_{x}(R\llbracket v\rrbracket)=\widehat{P}(R)
,
so
\mathcal{G}_{x}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R})=\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}(R)\cap\widehat{P}(R)=\{1\}
.
Using the fpqc covering
\operatorname{Spec}R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\cup\operatorname{Spec}R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]
instead, we find that

\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\right)\cap\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)=\mathcal{G}_{x}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R})=\{1\}.

∎

We will now discuss the Lie algebra.
Let
J\to R\to\overline{R}
be a square zero extension.
We define
J(\!(v+p)\!)\to R(\!(v+p)\!)\to\overline{R}(\!(v+p)\!)
as the kernel, and similarly
J\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]
and
J\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
.
Since
J(\!(v+p)\!)=\frac{1}{v+p}J\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\oplus J\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
and
vJ(\!(v+p)\!)=\frac{v}{v+p}J\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\oplus vJ\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
, we have for any constant group
H
over
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{R}
the identities

(22)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lie}H\left(J(\!(v+p)\!)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v+p}\operatorname{Lie}H\left(J\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)\oplus\operatorname{Lie}H\left(J\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\right),

(23)

\displaystyle v\operatorname{Lie}H\left(J(\!(v+p)\!)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{v}{v+p}\operatorname{Lie}H\left(J\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)\oplus v\operatorname{Lie}H\left(J\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\right).

We will in particular apply these identities with
H=\widehat{G},\widehat{U}^{\text{op}},\widehat{P}
.
Note that the decomposition

(24)

\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{G}=\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}\oplus\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{P}

is compatible with (
22
) and (
23
).

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
J\to R\to\overline{R}
be a square zero extension of noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebras. Then

\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(J\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)\oplus\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(J\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\right)=\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(J(\!(v+p)\!)\right).

Proof.

The assumption that
R
is noetherian ensures that
v
is a non-zerodivisor in
R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket

[
Fie71
]
(this property fails if
R
has infinite
p
-torsion), so
v
is also a non-zerodivisor in
R(\!(v+p)\!)
.
By
Remark

3.34
,
Assumption Dil
, and the universal property of dilations, we then have

\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(R(\!(v+p)\!)\right)=\left\{A\in\widehat{G}\left(R(\!(v+p)\!)\right)|A\mod v\in\widehat{P}\left(R(\!(v+p)\!)/vR(\!(v+p)\!)\right)\right\},

and we may describe
\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(\overline{R}(\!(v+p)\!)\right)
similarly.
By the decomposition (
24
), it follows that we can identify
\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(J(\!(v+p)\!)\right)\subset\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{G}\left(J(\!(v+p)\!)\right)
as the sub-Lie algebra

(25)

\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(J(\!(v+p)\!)\right)=v\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}\left(J(\!(v+p)\!)\right)\oplus\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{P}\left(J(\!(v+p)\!)\right).

We can also show (similarly to the above for the first identification, using
Lemma

3.35
for the second identification)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(J\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\right)

\displaystyle=v\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}\left(J\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\right)+\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{P}\left(J\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lie}\mathcal{G}_{x}\left(J\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{v}{v+p}\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}\left(J\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right)+\frac{1}{v+p}\operatorname{Lie}\widehat{P}\left(J\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right),

The sum of these two equations is seen to be (
25
) using (
22
) with
H=\widehat{P}
and (
23
) with
H=\widehat{U}^{\text{op}}
.
∎

Proposition 3.38
.

The multiplication map
m:L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}\times L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}\hookrightarrow L\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a formally étale monomorphism on noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebras.
15
15

15

By “formally étale monomorphism on noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebras” we mean that when we restrict these loop group functors to noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, the map is a monomorphism and satisfies the definition of being formally étale with respect to square zero extensions of noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebras.

Proof.

This is just like
[
Le+23
, Lemma 3.2.6]
. We reproduce the proof here for the convenience of the reader.

We first show that
m
is a monomorphism. Let
R
be an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra and
g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)
,
h_{1},h_{2}\in L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)
.
If
g_{1}h_{1}=g_{2}h_{2}
, then
g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}=h_{2}h_{1}^{-1}
, so it is immediate from
Lemma

3.36
that
g_{1}=g_{2}
and
h_{1}=h_{2}
.

It remains to verify the formally étale property.
Let
J\to R\to\overline{R}
be a square zero extension of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, and consider the lifting problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}\overline{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{+}\mathcal{G}\times L^{--}\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\mathcal{G}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{x},\overline{y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

That is, we have an element
g\in L\mathcal{G}(R)
and a decomposition
\overline{g}=\overline{x}\overline{y}\in L\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})
, with
\overline{x}\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})
and
\overline{y}\in L^{--}\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})
.
The solution to the lifting problem is given by lifts
x\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)
of
\overline{x}
and
y\in L^{--}\mathcal{G}(R)
of
\overline{y}
such that
g=xy
.
Note that since
m
is a monomorphism, the solution is necessarily unique, so it suffices to show that one exists.

Since both
L^{+}\mathcal{G}
and
L^{--}\mathcal{G}
are formally smooth (by smoothness of
\mathcal{G}
), we can choose
some
lifts
x^{\prime}\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)
of
\overline{x}
and
y^{\prime}\in L^{--}\mathcal{G}(R)
of
\overline{y}
.
Then
(x^{\prime})^{-1}g(y^{\prime})^{-1}\in L\mathcal{G}(R)
reduces to
\overline{x}^{-1}\overline{g}\overline{y}^{-1}=\overline{x}^{-1}\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{y}^{-1}=1
in
L\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})
,
so
(x^{\prime})^{-1}g(y^{\prime})^{-1}\in\operatorname{Lie}L\mathcal{G}(J)
.
By
Lemma

3.37
there exists
a\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)
and
b\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{--}\mathcal{G}(J)
such that

(x^{\prime})^{-1}g(y^{\prime})^{-1}=ab
, and then
g=(x^{\prime}a)(by^{\prime})
, so
x=x^{\prime}a
and
y=by^{\prime}
solves the lifting problem.
∎

3.4.
Frobenius

Just as we constructed
\mathcal{G}_{x}
in
Section

3.1
, we may construct
\mathcal{G}_{\varphi(x)}
in the same way.
From
Section

2.4.2
we see that
\varphi(x)=\varphi(n)\cdot o
.
Note that

(26)

\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}=\varphi^{*}(n\widehat{G}n^{-1})\cong\varphi(n)\widehat{G}\varphi(n)^{-1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}.

However, it does not follow trivially that
\varphi^{*}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\cong\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}
, because the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

is not cartesian.
16
16

16

However, note that the corresponding diagram away from the origin
\{0\}
(i. e. after inverting
v
) is cartesian. So is the diagram with
\pi_{0}:\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
in place of
\pi_{I}
.

There is a comparison map

\varphi^{*}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}=\varphi^{*}(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\to(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}=\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)},

but it is not necessarily an isomorphism, as the following (typical) example shows.

Example 3.39
.

Consider the case that
L=\mathbb{Q}_{p}\left((-p)^{1/(p-1)}\right)
(where
p>2
),
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{2}
, and
x=u^{(0,1)}\cdot o
.
Note that
e=p-1
,
\#\mathcal{J}=1
, and
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}=\mathcal{G}_{x}
in this case.
Let
R
be an
\mathcal{O}[u]
-algebra in which
u
is a non-zerodivisor.
Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}(R)=u^{(0,1)}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(R)u^{-(0,1)}(R)=\begin{pmatrix}R&u^{-1}R\\
uR&R\end{pmatrix}
,
and
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}(R)=u^{(0,p)}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(R)u^{-(0,p)}=\begin{pmatrix}R&u^{-p}R\\
u^{p}R&R\end{pmatrix}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}
(this notation is slightly abusive since
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
only consists of invertible matrices).
On the other hand, if
R
is an
\mathcal{O}[v]
-algebra in which
v=u^{p-1}
is a non-zerodivisor, then

\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=\begin{pmatrix}R&R\\
vR&R\end{pmatrix}
and
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=\begin{pmatrix}R&R\\
v^{p}R&R\end{pmatrix}
,
but
\mathcal{G}_{\varphi(x)}(R)=\begin{pmatrix}R&v^{-1}R\\
v^{2}R&R\end{pmatrix}
.

Remark 3.40
.

It is actually
\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}=(\pi_{I})_{*}^{I}\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
as well as
\mathcal{G}_{\varphi(x)}=\pi_{*}^{\Gamma}\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
that is relevant for our purposes, and not
\varphi^{*}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
and
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
This is important for the presentation (
2
) of the moduli stack of Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors of fixed type.
We will see this again in
Section

4.3.2
.

Remark 3.41
.

Let us also remark that
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x}=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,\varphi(x)}
, as a special case of
Lemma

5.1
.
This implies that
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x}=\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{\varphi(x)}
, which provides an alternative way to see the isomorphism (
26
), by evaluating at
E^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket
-points and using the uniqueness part of the proof of
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
again.
One may similarly justify the description of
\varphi^{*}\mathcal{G}_{x}
in
Example

3.39
.

3.4.1.
Comparing
\mathcal{G}_{\varphi(x)}
and
\mathcal{G}_{x}

Recall from
Section

2.7.1
that we have made a choice of
c\in G^{*}(\mathcal{O}[v^{\pm 1}])
such that
c\cdot\varphi(x)=x
.
It is then clear that we have an isomorphism

\operatorname{Ad}_{c}:\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}

of group schemes over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
This is
\Gamma
-equivariant since
c
is fixed by
\Gamma
, so by functoriality of invariant pushforward we obtain isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ad}_{c}:\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ad}_{c}:\mathcal{G}_{\varphi(x)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{G}_{x}.

Example 3.42
.

In the situation of
Example

3.39
, the element
c=v^{(0,-1)}
satisfies
c\cdot\varphi(x)=x
, since
v^{(0,-1)}\begin{pmatrix}R&v^{-1}R\\
v^{2}R&R\end{pmatrix}v^{-(0,-1)}=\begin{pmatrix}R&R\\
vR&R\end{pmatrix}
.

3.4.2.
Frobenius endomorphism of loop groups

We now turn to the Frobenius endomorphisms of loop groups.
The construction in
Section

2.4
yields a
\Gamma
-equivariant endomorphism
\varphi:L_{0}\widehat{G}\to L_{0}\widehat{G}
, where
L_{0}\widehat{G}(R)=\widehat{G}\left((R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q})(\!(u)\!)\right)
.
Taking
\Gamma
-fixed points we obtain an endomorphism

\varphi:L_{0}G^{*}\to L_{0}G^{*},

where
L_{0}G^{*}(R)=G^{*}\left(R(\!(v)\!)\right)
.
Moreover, we can view
L_{0}^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}\subset L_{0}G^{*}
as a subfunctor.
We will need the operator

(27)

\displaystyle\varphi_{c}:L_{0}G^{*}

\displaystyle\to L_{0}G^{*}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto c\varphi(A)c^{-1}.

Lemma 3.43
.

We have
\varphi_{c}\left(L_{0}^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}\right)\subset L_{0}^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}
.

Proof.

It is immediate from (
26
) that
\varphi\left(L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\right)\subset L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}
, where for
y=x,\varphi(x)
we have
L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{y}(R)=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{y}\left((R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{q})(\!(u)\!)\right)
and we view
L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{y}\subset L_{0}\widehat{G}
as a subfunctor.
Since
c\cdot\varphi(x)=x
it then follows that
\varphi_{c}\left(L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\right)=c\varphi\left(L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\right)c^{-1}\subset cL_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}c^{-1}=L_{0}^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
.
Taking
\Gamma
-fixed points, the result follows.
∎

4.
The affine grassmannian and the stack
\mathcal{Y}

In this section we introduce the two main geometric objects of interest.
The first object is the affine Grassmannian, as described in
[
PZ13
, Section Section 6.2.6]
.
This is the global affine Grassmannian for
\mathcal{G}
specialized to
v=-p
, and we will denote it by
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}
and refer to it simply as “the affine Grassmannian”.
The second object is the moduli stack of Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors, denoted
\mathcal{Y}
.
We will work with
\mathcal{Y}
as a quotient stack.

We keep the notation and assumptions from
Section

2.7
and
Section

3
.
In particular, we make
Assumption ConFib
by default.

4.1.
The affine Grassmannian

We define the affine Grassmannian is the fppf quotient sheaf over
\mathcal{O}

\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}:=\left[L^{+}\mathcal{G}\backslash L\mathcal{G}\right],

i. e. the fppf sheafification of the functor
R\mapsto\mathcal{G}\left(R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\right)\backslash\mathcal{G}\left(R(\!(v+p)\!)\right)
.
The fppf sheaf
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}
is a sheaf in the fpqc topology by
[
Ces24
, Proposition 1.6]
, and therefore agrees with the “fpqc-sheafification” definition often taken in the literature.
This is the specialization at
v=-p
of the global affine Grassmannian for
\mathcal{G}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}

[
PZ13
, Section 6.2]
.
By
[
PZ13
, Proposition 6.5]

\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}
is a projective Ind-scheme over
\mathcal{O}
.

In what follows we will often use the convention
\operatorname{Gr}:=\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}
to simplify notation.

4.1.1.
The special and generic fiber

We reiterate that
\operatorname{Gr}
(without any subscript) is the mixed characteristic affine Grassmannian for
\mathcal{G}
.
Following
[
PZ13
, Corollary 6.6]
, we describe its generic fiber
\operatorname{Gr}_{E}
and special fiber
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}
.
It will be helpful to keep in mind that over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we have
p
is invertible in the generic fiber
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{E}
, but
0=-p
in the special fiber
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Let
\operatorname{Gr}_{E}=\left[L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{E}\backslash L\mathcal{G}_{E}\right]
denote the generic fiber of
\operatorname{Gr}
over
E
.
Because of the identity
[
PZ13
, Section 6.2.6]

\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathcal{O}[v]}\operatorname{Spec}E\llbracket v+p\rrbracket\cong G^{*}_{E}\times_{E}\operatorname{Spec}E\llbracket v+p\rrbracket,

it follows that we can identify
\operatorname{Gr}_{E}
with the affine Grassmannian for the connected reductive group
G^{*}_{E}
over
E
.

Similarly, we let
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}=\left[L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}\backslash L\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}\right]
denote the special fiber of
\operatorname{Gr}
over
\mathbb{F}
.
This is the affine flag variety over
\mathbb{F}
for the connected reductive group
G^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)}
over
\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)
and the parahoric group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket}
.

4.1.2.
Pappas–Zhu local model

We first recall the definition of the Schubert variety for
\mu
.
By Cartan decomposition for
G^{*}_{\overline{E}}=\widehat{G}_{\overline{E}}
we have

(28)

L\mathcal{G}(\overline{E})=\widehat{G}\left(\overline{E}(\!(v+p)\!)\right)=\bigsqcup_{\nu\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{\text{dom}}}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(\overline{E})\cdot(v+p)^{\nu}\cdot L^{+}\mathcal{G}(\overline{E}).

The Schubert variety
\operatorname{Gr}_{E}^{\leq\mu}\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{E}
(as described in for example
[
Zhu16
, Section 2.1]
)
is the reduced closed subscheme with
\overline{E}
-points

(29)

\operatorname{Gr}_{E}^{\leq\mu}(\overline{E})=\bigsqcup_{\nu\in X_{*}(T^{*}_{E})^{\text{dom}},\nu\leq\mu}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(\overline{E})\cdot(v+p)^{\nu}\cdot L^{+}\mathcal{G}(\overline{E}),

where
\nu\leq\mu
means that
\mu-\nu
is a sum of positive roots.

Definition 4.1

(
[
PZ13
, Definition 7.1]
)
.

The
Pappas–Zhu local model
for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mu
, denoted
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}=\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\leq\mu}
, is the reduced closure of
\operatorname{Gr}_{E}^{\leq\mu}
in
\operatorname{Gr}
.

Remark 4.2
.

The Pappas–Zhu local model
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
is often denoted
M_{\mathcal{G},\mu}
, for example in
[
PZ13
]
.

Note that
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
is a projective scheme over
\mathcal{O}
by (Ind-)projectivity of
\operatorname{Gr}
.
The generic fiber of
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
is (clearly) the Schubert variety
\operatorname{Gr}_{E}^{\leq\mu}
, and the special fiber is identified by
[
PZ13
, Theorem 9.3]
in terms of the admissible set
\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)
of
\mu
, which we recall below.

4.1.3.
Digression: The admissible set and the special fiber

We will recall the description and significance of the admissible set
\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)
.
By definition, the admissible set
\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)
consists of elements of the Iwahori–Weyl group
\widetilde{W}^{*}
that we defined in
Section

2.2.1
, so let us first recall some facts about the Iwahori–Weyl group.
When
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
and
\mathbb{F}\supset\mathbb{F}_{q}
, then
G^{*}=\widehat{G}
by
Remark

2.7
and we have
\widetilde{W}^{*}=\widehat{N}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))/\widehat{T}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket)
.
Now, if
x
is
0
-generic (so the connected stabilizer of
x
in
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
is an Iwahori subgroup), then the Iwahori–Weyl group parametrizes double cosets, in the sense that the map

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}^{*}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,\mathbb{F}}\backslash L\mathcal{G}_{x,\mathbb{F}}/L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,\mathbb{F}}=\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}/L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}

\displaystyle\mapsto L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}\widetilde{w}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}

is a bijection
[
KP23
, Theorem 7.8.1]
. More generally, if
x
is not
0
-generic, we can define
W_{x}\subset\widetilde{W}^{*}
as the stabilizer of the facet containing
x
in the affine Weyl group
W^{\text{aff}}\subset\widetilde{W}^{*}
, and by loc. cit. we have a bijection

(30)

\displaystyle W_{x}\backslash\widetilde{W}^{*}/W_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}/L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}.

We now recall some facts from the study of affine flag varieties.
The double coset
S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w})=L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}\widetilde{w}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}
is called an (open) affine Schubert cell, and if
W_{x}\widetilde{w}W_{x}\neq W_{x}\widetilde{w}^{\prime}W_{x}
, the associated affine Schubert cells
S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w})
and
S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w}^{\prime})
are disjoint by (
30
).
The closure of
S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w})
in
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}
is the affine Schubert variety
S(\widetilde{w})\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}
.
The Bruhat ordering on
\widetilde{W}^{*}
is then characterized by the property that

S(\widetilde{w})=\bigsqcup_{W_{x}\widetilde{w}^{\prime}W_{x}\in W_{x}\backslash\widetilde{W}^{*}/W_{x},\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\leq\widetilde{w}}S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w}^{\prime}),

where this is the disjoint union on the underlying points (not as schemes).
The Bruhat ordering also has a combinatorial description, which we now recall.

First consider the affine Weyl group
W^{\text{aff}}\cong\Phi(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})\mathbb{Z}\rtimes W
, which is a Coxeter group and can be identified with the Iwahori–Weyl group of the simply connected cover of the derived group of
\widehat{G}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
denote an alcove whose closure contains
x
.
Let
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\subset W^{\text{aff}}
denote the affine reflections across walls bounding
\mathcal{C}
.
For any
\widetilde{z}\in W^{\text{aff}}
we may then write
\widetilde{z}
as an expression

(31)

\widetilde{z}=s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{l}},

which geometrically corresponds to a “gallery walk” from the alcove
\mathcal{C}
to the alcove
\widetilde{z}\mathcal{C}
.
We call the expression (
31
)
reduced
if
l=l(\widetilde{z})
is minimal.
If
\widetilde{z}^{\prime}\in W^{\text{aff}}
, then
\widetilde{z}^{\prime}\leq\widetilde{z}
in the Bruhat order if and only if there exists a reduced expression for
\widetilde{z}
such that a reduced expression for
\widetilde{z}^{\prime}
can be obtained by deleting some of the
\widetilde{s}_{i_{j}}
’s from the right hand side of (
31
).

We have a decomposition

\widetilde{W}^{*}=W^{\text{aff}}\rtimes\widetilde{W}^{*}_{\mathcal{C}},

where
\widetilde{W}^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}\subset\widetilde{W}^{*}
is the stabilizer of
\mathcal{C}
in
\widetilde{W}^{*}
.
One way to observe this is that
W^{\text{aff}}
acts simply transitively on the set of alcoves in
\mathcal{A}\left(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
, implying that
W^{\text{aff}}\cap\widetilde{W}^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}=\{1\}
in
\widetilde{W}^{*}

and
W^{\text{aff}}
acts transitively on
\widetilde{W}^{*}/\widetilde{W}^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}
via left translations.
The Bruhat ordering on
\widetilde{W}^{*}
is then induced from the Bruhat ordering on
W^{\text{aff}}
.
More precisely, if
\widetilde{w}=\widetilde{z}\widetilde{t}
and
\widetilde{w}^{\prime}=\widetilde{z}^{\prime}\widetilde{t}^{\prime}
, where
\widetilde{z},\widetilde{z}^{\prime}\in W^{\text{aff}}
and
\widetilde{t},\widetilde{t}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{W}^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}
, then
\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\leq\widetilde{w}
if and only if
\widetilde{t}=\widetilde{t}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{z}^{\prime}\leq\widetilde{z}
(and if
\widetilde{t}\neq\widetilde{t}^{\prime}
then
\widetilde{w}
and
\widetilde{w}^{\prime}
are incomparable).

We now return to the admissible set, giving a heuristic description in the unramified case.
Consider
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}(\mathcal{O})
. This contains the classes represented by
(v+p)^{\mu}
, as well as
(v+p)^{w\mu}
for any
w\in W
.
Therefore,
S^{\circ}(v^{w\mu})=L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}v^{w\mu}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}\subset\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F}}
for every
w\in W
.
At the same time,
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F}}\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}
is closed, so contains the closure

S(v^{w\mu})=\bigsqcup_{W_{x}\widetilde{w}^{\prime}W_{x}\in W_{x}\backslash\widetilde{W}^{*}/W_{x},\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\leq v^{w\mu}}S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w}^{\prime}).

The union of all these
\widetilde{w}^{\prime}
as
w
ranges through
W
is precisely the admissible set of
\mu
. That is,

\operatorname{Adm}(\mu):=\{\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{W}^{*}|\text{there exists }w\in W\text{ such that }\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\leq v^{w\mu}\}.

In
Section

7.1.5
we give a detailed description of the admissible set in one example. If
x
is not
0
-generic, we define

\operatorname{Adm}^{x}(\mu):=W_{x}\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)W_{x}

as the image of
\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)
in
W_{x}\backslash\widetilde{W}^{*}/W_{x}
.

We have the following result, completely characterizing the special fiber
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Suppose
p\nmid\pi_{1}(G^{*})
.
Then
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F}}
is geometrically reduced and we have
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}=\bigsqcup_{W_{x}\widetilde{w}^{\prime}W_{x}\in\operatorname{Adm}^{x}(\mu)}S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w}^{\prime})
.
In particular, if
x
is
0
-generic, then
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}=\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)}S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

This is
[
PZ13
, Theorem 9.1, Theorem 9.3]
.
Note that the (easy) inclusion
\supset
is explained above.
∎

Corollary 4.4
.

Suppose
p\nmid\pi_{1}(G^{*})
.
If
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, then the irreducible components of
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
are exactly
\{S(v^{w\mu})\}_{w\in W}
.

4.1.4.
Bounded loop group

We define a “bounded” subspace
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\subset L\mathcal{G}
as the fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Since
L\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}
is a left
L^{+}\mathcal{G}
-torsor, so is
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
.
In particular,
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
is representable by an affine scheme, using the fact that
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
is a projective scheme together with
[
Sta
,
Lemma 01SG
]
and
[
Sta
,
Section 0244
]
.
This is in contrast to
L\mathcal{G}
, which is only an Ind-scheme.

The scheme
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
is reduced by
Lemma

B.4
.
Note that the
\overline{E}
-points of
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
are precisely given by (
29
) by Cartan decomposition, and the reduced
closure of
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{E}}
in
L\mathcal{G}
is
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
by
Lemma

B.5
.
Since
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{E}}
is evidently stable under both left and right translation by
L^{+}\mathcal{G}(\overline{E})
,
its reduced closure
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
must also be stable under both left and right translation by
L^{+}\mathcal{G}
.

4.1.5.
Covers of the affine Grassmannian

For a concave function
f\geq 0
, we will consider the cover of the affine Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{f}
defined as the fppf quotient sheaf

\operatorname{Gr}_{f}:=\left[L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}\backslash L\mathcal{G}\right],

where we recall the group
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
from
Proposition

3.19
.
The natural map
\operatorname{Gr}_{f}\to\operatorname{Gr}
is a
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}/L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsor whenever
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
is normal in
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}
.

We recall the most important cases from
Section

3.2.5
. If
f=n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, then
\operatorname{Gr}_{n}\to\operatorname{Gr}
is a
L^{n}\mathcal{G}_{x}
-torsor.
If
f=0^{+}
and
x
is
0
-generic, then
\operatorname{Gr}_{0^{+}}\to\operatorname{Gr}
is a
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
-torsor.

We also consider covers
\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu}:=\left[L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}\backslash L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\right]
, and the above remarks still apply.

4.1.6.
Affine charts in terms of the negative loop group

In this subsection we invoke
Assumption Dil
.
Define
L^{--,\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{--,\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{--}\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\mathcal{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Lemma 4.5
.

L^{--,\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
is a scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

With respect to an embedding
\widehat{G}
into affine space over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
elements of
L^{--,\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)
(for some
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
) is determined by its “entries” corresponding to the coordinates of affine space,
each being an element of
R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]
. In fact, the coefficients of these entries gives the Ind-scheme structure of
L^{--}\mathcal{G}
.
The
\leq\mu
conditon implies that the poles will be bounded, i. e. that the coefficients of
(v+p)^{-n}
will be zero for
n\gg 0
.
Hence there are finitely many coefficients to account for, so
L^{--,\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
is a scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{O}
.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O})
.
The map

\displaystyle L^{--,\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto AX

is an open immersion.

Proof.

It follows from
Proposition

3.38
that the map is a formally étale monomorphism on noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, and it is locally of finite type by
Lemma

4.5
.
By
[
Sta
,
Lemma 02HX
]
it follows that the map is smooth, hence an étale monomorphism, hence an open immersion by
[
Sta
,
Theorem 025G
]
.
∎

Given
z\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O})
, we define
U(z)
as the image of the map
L^{--,\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
,
A\mapsto Az
.
By the above proposition,
U(z)\subset\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
is Zariski open.

We now assume that
p\nmid\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})
.
Recall that
Theorem

4.3
identifies the special fiber of
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
in terms of the admissible set of
\mu
, which is in particular
a finite subset of the Iwahori–Weyl group of
\widehat{G}
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(v)\!)
.
Suppose these elements are enumerated by
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
, and we choose representatives in
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{O})
representing these elements, denoted by the same symbols.
Then
U(z_{1})_{\mathbb{F}},\dots,U(z_{n})_{\mathbb{F}}
cover
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}
, and by properness of
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
over
\mathcal{O}

it follows that
U(z_{1}),\dots,U(z_{n})
cover
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
.

Corollary 4.7
.

The
L^{+}\mathcal{G}
-torsor
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
splits Zariski locally on
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
.

Proof.

The torsor splits over each
U(z_{i})
by construction of these charts.
∎

4.2.
The stack
\mathcal{Y}

Consider the right action of
L^{+}\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}
on
L\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}
given by

X\star A=A^{-1}X\varphi_{c}(A),\,\,\,\,\text{ for all }X\in L\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}(R),A\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}(R).

(Recall the notation
\varphi_{c}
from (
27
).)
We call this the
c
-twisted
\varphi
-conjugation action.

We define

\mathcal{Y}:=\left[L\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}/^{\varphi,c}L^{+}\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}\right]

as an fppf quotient stack over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathcal{O}
.
The
(\varphi,c)
over the slash is supposed to remind us that this quotient is with respect to the
c
-twisted
\varphi
-conjugation action.

In contrast to
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G}}
,
we note that the stack
\mathcal{Y}
is typically a genuine stack, and as such is not representable by an Ind-scheme.
In fact,
\mathcal{Y}
is not even of finite type, although it will be Ind-algebraic as a consequence of our results concerning the “bounded” substacks
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
defined below.

4.2.1.
Bounded substacks of
\mathcal{Y}

Recall the subscheme
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\subset L\mathcal{G}
from
Section

4.1.4
.
Since
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
is stable under both left and right translation by
L^{+}\mathcal{G}
, we can define the fppf quotient stack

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}

\displaystyle:=\left[L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}/^{\varphi,c}L^{+}\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}\right].

We will show in
Proposition

6.1
that
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
is a
p
-adic formal
algebraic
stack over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathcal{O}
,
and in
Theorem

6.4
we will show that it is smoothly equivalent to
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}
.

Remark 4.8
.

Via the relation to Galois representations in (
2
), the substack
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}\subset\mathcal{Y}
classifies
Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors with Hodge–Tate weights bounded by
\mu
.

4.2.2.
Covers of
\mathcal{Y}

For
f\geq 0
a concave function, we consider the fppf quotient stack over
\mathcal{O}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{f}

\displaystyle:=\left[L\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\wedge\varpi}/^{\varphi,c}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}^{\wedge\varpi}\right].

Whenever
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
is normal in
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}
, the natural map
\mathcal{Y}_{f}\to\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}
exhibits
\mathcal{Y}_{f}
as an
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}/L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsor over
\mathcal{Y}
.
Again, we emphasize that
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}/L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}=L^{n}\mathcal{G}_{x}
when
f=n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}/L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}=\overline{\mathcal{T}}
when
f=0^{+}
and
x
is
0
-generic,
as explained in
Section

3.2.5
.

4.3.
Moduli interpretation

Throughout this section let
f\geq 0
denote a concave function.
Recall from
Proposition

3.19
that we have smooth affine group schemes
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

4.3.1.
Moduli interpretation of the affine Grassmannian

The stack
\operatorname{Gr}_{f}
has the following moduli interpretation.

Proposition 4.9
.

For any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
, a morphism
\operatorname{Spec}R\to\operatorname{Gr}_{f}
classifies a pair
(\mathcal{P},\beta)
,
where
\mathcal{P}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsor on
R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
, and
\beta
is as follows.
Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}\times^{\mathcal{G}_{x,f}}\mathcal{G}_{x}
denote the associated
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-torsor.
Then
\beta:\mathcal{E}^{0}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-torsors over
R(\!(v+p)\!)
.

Proof.

Let
\operatorname{Gr}^{\prime}
denote the moduli of pairs
(\mathcal{P},\beta)
as above and let
\widetilde{\operatorname{Gr}}
denote the moduli of triples
(\mathcal{P},\beta,\alpha)
, where
\mathcal{P},\beta
are as above and
\alpha:\mathcal{E}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{P}

is a trivialization of the
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsor over
R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
.
Consider the map
L\mathcal{G}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{\operatorname{Gr}}
with
A\mapsto(\mathcal{E}^{0},L_{A},1)
, where
L_{A}
denotes left translation by
A
.
This is indeed an isomorphism, since an inverse is given by
(\mathcal{P},\beta,\alpha)\mapsto\alpha^{-1}\circ\beta
.
The left translation action of
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
on
L\mathcal{G}_{x}
corresponds to the action
B\cdot(\mathcal{P},\beta,\alpha)=(\mathcal{P},\beta,\alpha\circ L_{B}^{-1})
.
With respect to the obvious map
\widetilde{\operatorname{Gr}}\to\operatorname{Gr}^{\prime}
,
(\mathcal{P},\beta,\alpha)\mapsto(\mathcal{P},\beta)
,
the group
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
acts freely and transitively on fibers.
Using smoothness of
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
, there exists for any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
and
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsor
\mathcal{P}
over
\mathfrak{S}_{R}

an étale covering
R\to R^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{P}
becomes trivializable after base change to
\mathfrak{S}_{R^{\prime}}
, which implies that
\widetilde{\operatorname{Gr}}\to\operatorname{Gr}^{\prime}

is an epimorphism.
Hence
\left[L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}\backslash L\mathcal{G}\right]\cong\left[L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}\backslash\widetilde{\operatorname{Gr}}\right]\cong\operatorname{Gr}^{\prime}
.
∎

4.3.2.
Moduli interpretation of
\mathcal{Y}

Let
R
be a
p
-adically complete
\mathcal{O}
-algebra, and recall that
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}=R^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket=R^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
and
\mathfrak{S}_{R}=R\llbracket v\rrbracket=R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
.
Let us first define a map of groupoids

\varphi_{c}^{*}:\{(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})\text{-torsors over }\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}\}\to\{(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})\text{-torsors over }\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}\}

which is natural in
R
.
Namely, given a
(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})
-torsor
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
over
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
, pulling back along the
\Gamma
-equivariant map
\varphi:\operatorname{Spec}\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
yields a
(\Gamma,\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})
-torsor
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{x}
.
We can identify
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}
by (
26
), and recalling the choice of
c\in G^{*}(\mathcal{O}[v^{\pm 1}])
with
c\cdot\varphi(x)=x
, we then have a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism
\operatorname{Ad}_{c}:\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
of group schemes over
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
.
We then define
\varphi_{c}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}=\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\times^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)},\operatorname{Ad}_{c}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
as the associated
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
-torsor.

Note that if the type of a
(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})
-torsor
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
over
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
is trivial, then the type of
\varphi_{c}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is also trivial.
By
Proposition

A.5
it follows that we have a commutative diagram of groupoids

(32)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})\text{-torsors over }\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}\text{ of trivial type}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})\text{-torsors over }\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}\text{ of trivial type}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\mathcal{G}_{x}\text{-torsors over }\mathfrak{S}_{R}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\mathcal{G}_{x}\text{-torsors over }\mathfrak{S}_{R}\},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{c}^{*}}

where the vertical maps are equivalences and the horizontal dashed map by definition is one making the diagram commute.
This is moreover natural in
R
.

Proposition 4.10
.

The following moduli problems in
p
-adically complete
\mathcal{O}
-algebras
R
are equivalent:

(1)

Pairs
(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}},\widetilde{\phi})
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a
(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f})
-torsor over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
of trivial type,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\times^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x,f}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
is the associated
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
-torsor, and
\widetilde{\phi}:\varphi^{*}_{c}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
is a morphism of
(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})
-torsors, where the dashed arrow indicates that this morphism is defined after base change to
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}\left[(u^{e}+p)^{-1}\right]
. (Here,
\operatorname{Spf}
is formed with respect to the ideals generated by
\varpi
.)

(2)

Pairs
(\mathcal{P},\phi)
, where
\mathcal{P}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsor over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{R}
,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}\times^{\mathcal{G}_{x,f}}\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the associated
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-torsor, and
\phi:\varphi^{*}_{c}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a morphism of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-torsors, where the dashed arrow indicates that this morphism is only defined after base change to
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{R}\left[(v+p)^{-1}\right]
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{Y}_{f}
naturally represents the moduli problem (2) (hence also (1)).

Proof.

The equivalence of the moduli problems (1) and (2) is clear from the construction of
\varphi_{c}^{*}
, i. e. commutativity of (
32
).
To see that the moduli problem (2) is represented by
\mathcal{Y}
, one may argue as in the proof of
Proposition

4.9
.
We mention that if
L_{A}:\mathcal{E}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{E}^{0}
denotes an automorphism of the trivial
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-torsor over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{R}
given by left translation by
A\in\mathcal{G}_{x}(\mathfrak{S}_{R})=L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)
,
then
\varphi_{c}^{*}(L_{A})=L_{\varphi_{c}(A)}:\mathcal{E}^{0}\to\mathcal{E}^{0}
is given by left translation by the element
\varphi_{c}(A)\in L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)
(which was defined in
Section

3.4.2
).
∎

When
f=0
, we also have the following result, which provides the interpretation of
\mathcal{Y}
as a moduli stack of Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors of type
\tau
.

Proposition 4.11
.

The fppf stack
\mathcal{Y}
equivalent to the moduli of pairs
(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}},\widetilde{\phi})
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsor over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
of type
\tau
, and
\phi:\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a morphism of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors, where the dashed arrow indicates that this morphism is only defined after base change to
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}\left[(u^{e}+p)^{-1}\right]
.
(Consequently, this moduli problem is equivalent to those of
Proposition

4.10
in the case
f=0
.)

Since this result may be of special interest, we give two proofs.
The first proof is more conceptual, giving an equivalence between the moduli problem and the moduli problem (1) of
Proposition

4.10
.
The second proof is direct, and shows explicitly how to pass between the moduli of Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors and the quotient stack
\mathcal{Y}
.

First proof.

Consider first
n\mathcal{E}^{0}\cong{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}}^{0}
, the trivial
\widehat{G}
-torsor of type
\tau
.
We can identify
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}=n\widehat{G}n^{-1}
as the automorphism group scheme of
n\mathcal{E}^{0}
, so by twisting (see
Section

A.2
)
we have an equivalence

\displaystyle\left\{(\Gamma,\widehat{G})\text{-torsors of type }\tau\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left\{(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})\text{-torsors of trivial type}\right\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\times^{\widehat{G}}\mathcal{E}^{0}n^{-1},

where
\mathcal{E}^{0}n^{-1}\cong(_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0})^{-1}
.
Via this equivalence, a morphism of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors
\widetilde{\phi}:\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
corresponds to a morphism of
(\Gamma,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x})
-torsors

(33)

\left(\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\right)\times^{\widehat{G}}\mathcal{E}^{0}n^{-1}\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\times^{\widehat{G}}\mathcal{E}^{0}n^{-1}.

The point is now that
\varphi^{*}\left(\mathcal{E}^{0}n^{-1}\right)=\mathcal{E}^{0}\varphi(n)^{-1}
is a
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}
-torsor by
Eq.

26
,
but using the isomorphism
\operatorname{Ad}_{c}:\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}
we find that

\varphi^{*}\left(\mathcal{E}^{0}n^{-1}\right)\times^{\operatorname{Ad}_{c}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\cong\mathcal{E}^{0}\varphi(n)^{-1}c^{-1}=\mathcal{E}^{0}n^{-1},

where we have used the assumption that
\tau
is strictly Frobenius invariant and
c=n\varphi(n)^{-1}
.
Therefore,

\left(\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\right)\times^{\widehat{G}}\mathcal{E}n^{-1}\cong\left(\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\right)\times^{\widehat{G}}\left(\varphi^{*}\mathcal{E}n^{-1}\right)\times^{\operatorname{Ad}_{c}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\cong\varphi^{*}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\times^{\widehat{G}}\mathcal{E}n^{-1}\right)\times^{\operatorname{Ad}_{c}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}.

So the morphism
\widetilde{\phi}
, which corresponds to (
33
), in turn corresponds to a morphism

\varphi^{*}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\times^{\widehat{G}}\mathcal{E}n^{-1}\right)\times^{\operatorname{Ad}_{c}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\times^{\widehat{G}}\mathcal{E}n^{-1}.

This implies that the moduli problem in question is equivalent to the moduli problem (1) in
Proposition

4.10
with
f=0
.
∎

Second proof.

We start by making some preliminary observations.
The assumption that
\tau
is strictly Frobenius invariant, i. e.
\varphi\tau=\tau
, implies that
\varphi^{*}{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}={{}_{\varphi\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}={{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}
.
By
Lemma

A.3
, any morphism of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors
L_{C}:{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}\dashrightarrow{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}
over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}

is given by left translation by some element
C\in L\widehat{G}^{\wedge\varpi}(R)
which gives a
1
-cocycle
C:\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tau
, i. e.

(34)

Cn^{-1}(^{\theta}n)(^{\theta}C)^{-1}=n^{-1}(^{\theta}n)

for all
\theta\in\Gamma
(recall that
\tau(\theta)=n^{-1}(^{\theta}n)
).
If we let
A=nCn^{-1}
, we see that (
34
) is equivalent to the condition that
{{}^{\theta}A}=A
for all
\theta
, i. e.
A
is fixed by
\Gamma
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}
be the moduli of triples
(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}},\widetilde{\phi},\alpha)
, where
(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}},\widetilde{\phi})\in\mathcal{Y}(R)
and
\alpha:{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
is a morphism of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
.
We then have an isomorphism
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}L\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\wedge\varpi}
as follows.
Given a triple
(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}},\widetilde{\phi},\alpha)
, the morphism
\widetilde{\phi}
corresponds via
\alpha

to a morphism
L_{C}:{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}\dashrightarrow{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}
, namely
L_{C}=\alpha^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\phi}\circ\varphi^{*}\alpha
.
We then associate to the triple
(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}},\widetilde{\phi},\alpha)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}(R)
the element
A=nCn^{-1}\in\left(L\widehat{G}^{\wedge\varpi}(R)\right)^{\Gamma}=L\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\wedge\varpi}(R)
.

Next, we consider how the choice of
\alpha
affects
A
.
That is, suppose we change
\alpha
into
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha\circ L_{Y}
, where
L_{Y}:{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}
is a morphism of
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
given by left translation by some element
C\in L^{+}\widehat{G}^{\wedge\varpi}(R)
representing a
1
-cocycle
Y:\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tau
(satisfying (
34
) with
Y
in place of
C
).
Then
\widetilde{\phi}
corresponds via
\alpha^{\prime}
to left translation by some element
C^{\prime}
.
We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}\left({}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}\left({}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}L_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\varphi(Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{Y}}

From the diagram we see that
C^{\prime}=Y^{-1}C\varphi(Y)
.
Therefore, if we let
X:=nYn^{-1}\in\left(L^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x}^{\wedge\varpi}(R)\right)^{\Gamma}=L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\wedge\varpi}(R)
and
A^{\prime}:=nCn^{-1}
, then

A^{\prime}=nC^{\prime}n^{-1}=(nYn^{-1})^{-1}(nCn^{-1})(n\varphi(n)^{-1}(\varphi(nYn^{-1}))\varphi(n)n^{-1})=X^{-1}A(c\varphi(X)c^{-1})=A\star X

is the
c
-twisted
\varphi
-conjugate of
A
.

The moduli of Breuil–Kisin
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors is the quotient of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}
where we forget
\alpha
(where we use that a choice of
\alpha
exists étale locally by the correspondence between
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors of type
\tau
and torsors for the smooth group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
, and
Proposition

2.52
).
Since forgetting
\alpha
corresponds to taking the quotient with respect to all possible modifications, we see from the above that this is precisely the quotient stack
\mathcal{Y}=\left[L\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\wedge\varpi}/^{c,\varphi}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\wedge\varpi}\right]
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

In both proofs of
Proposition

4.11
, we used the assumption that
\tau
is strictly Frobenius invariant and
c=n\varphi(n)^{-1}
.
This is in fact the only place where we use this assumption; for all other results it suffices that
c\in G^{*}(\mathcal{O}[v^{\pm 1}])
and
c\cdot\varphi(x)=x
.

4.3.3.
Applications to the existence of sections

From the proof of
Proposition

4.9
we can make the following observation.
Let
R
be a
\varpi
-adically complete
\mathcal{O}
-algebra and let
\operatorname{Spf}R\to\operatorname{Gr}_{f}
be a morphism, classifying
(\mathcal{P},\beta)
.
Then a lift as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spf}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Gr}_{f}}}

exists if and only if
\mathcal{P}
is a trivializable
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsor.
Similarly, if
\operatorname{Spf}R\to\mathcal{Y}_{f}
is a morphism classifying a pair
(\mathcal{P},\phi)
, then a lift as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L\mathcal{G}}^{\wedge\varpi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spf}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{f}}

exists if and only if
\mathcal{P}
is a trivializable
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsor.

From the above we conclude that the obstruction to surjectivity of
L\mathcal{G}(R)\to\operatorname{Gr}_{f}(R)
and
L\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{Y}_{f}(R)

is the existence of non-trivial
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
-torsors over
R/\varpi R
.
(Here we use smoothness of
\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
to reduce the question about existence of non-trivial torsors over
R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
to the same question over
R/\varpi R
.)

Proposition 4.13
.

Assume that
f>0
.
Then for any
p
-adically complete
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
, the maps
L\mathcal{G}(R)\to\operatorname{Gr}_{f}(R)
,
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)\to\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu}(R)
,
L\mathcal{G}(R)\to\mathcal{Y}_{f}(R)
, and
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)\to\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}(R)
are surjective.
In particular, the natural maps
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)\backslash L\mathcal{G}(R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Gr}_{f}(R)
and
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)\backslash L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu}(R)
are bijections, and the maps

\left[L\mathcal{G}(R)/^{\varphi,c}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{Y}_{f}(R)
and
\left[L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)/^{\varphi,c}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}(R)
are equivalences of groupoids.

Proof.

This is immediate from the above and
Proposition

3.24
.
∎

Corollary 4.14
.

Assume that
f>0
.
Then the
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}
-torsor
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
trivializes Zariski locally on
\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
.

Proof.

Let
U=\operatorname{Spec}R\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
be the inclusion of an affine open chart.
There exists a lift
U\to L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
by
Proposition

4.13
, which provides a section over
U
.
∎

5.
Contraction and straightening

The Frobenius endomorphism
\varphi_{c}
from
Section

3.4
has a contracting property, sending congruence subgroups into deeper congruence subgroups.
On the other hand, conjugating by an element
X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
generally does not preserve congruence subgroups.
Quantifying and balancing these effects allow us to determine precisely when the operator
A\mapsto X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1}
is contracting.
The answer is given in
Lemma

5.10
, and when the answer is affirmative we can “straighten” the
c
-twisted
\varphi
-conjugation action into a left translation action, as in
Lemma

5.11
.
This is the key to relating the affine Grassmannian and the stack
\mathcal{Y}
.

5.1.
Contraction properties of the Frobenius endomorphism

We now discuss various contraction properties of the Frobenius endomorphism of
Section

3.4
.
We first consider the general case, and then we will consider the unramified case with extra genericity assumptions on
x
.

Recall that
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)

is actually fixed by
\Gamma
. Hence

\varphi(x)-o=p(x-o).

We will use this fact repeatedly.
For arguments that take place over
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
or
\breve{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
we will also often argue in each component labeled by
j\in\mathcal{J}
without comment, as we have done in previous sections.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
m\in\frac{1}{e}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then we can identify

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,m}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,\varphi(x),pm},

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{pm},

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,m^{+}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,\varphi(x),p\left(m+\frac{1}{e}\right)},

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m^{+}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{p\left(m+\frac{1}{e}\right)}.

Proof.

We begin by proving that
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,m^{+}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,p\left(m+\frac{1}{e}\right)}
.
Recall from
Section

3.1.7
that
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,m^{+}}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,u^{-n}t]
, where
n=\lceil-e\langle a,x-o\rangle+em^{+}\rceil=-e\langle a,x-o\rangle+em+1
, and we use that
\langle a,x-o\rangle=-\frac{1}{e}\langle wa,\lambda\rangle\in\frac{1}{e}\mathbb{Z}
by our construction of
x
.
Consider image of the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u]\otimes_{\varphi,\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u]}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,u^{-n}t]

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Frac}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,t]\right)

\displaystyle a\otimes b

\displaystyle\mapsto a\varphi(b),

is
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,u^{-pn}t]
.
Since

pn=-pe\langle a,x-o\rangle+pem+p=-e\langle a,\varphi(x)-o\rangle+ep\left(m+\frac{1}{e}\right),

we see that
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,\varphi(x),p\left(m+\frac{1}{e}\right)}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,u^{-pn}t]
.
This shows that
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,m^{+}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,p\left(m+\frac{1}{e}\right)}
.

Next, we show that
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m^{+}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{p(m+1)}
.
We may assume that
\widehat{T}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
Then we know from
Section

3.1.7

that
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{m^{+}}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,t^{-1},u^{-n}(t-1)]
, where
n=\lceil em^{+}\rceil=em+1
.
The image of

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}\otimes_{\varphi,\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u]}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,t^{-1},u^{-(m+1)}(t-1)]

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Frac}\left(\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,t]\right)

\displaystyle a\otimes b

\displaystyle\mapsto a\varphi(b)

is precisely
\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,t^{-1},u^{-p(em+1)}(t-1)]
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{p\left(m+\frac{1}{e}\right)}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}^{\mathcal{J}}[u,t^{-1},u^{-p(em+1)}(t-1)]
since
p(em+1)=ep\left(m+\frac{1}{e}\right)
.

We omit the proofs of the remaining two statements, as the proofs are similar to those we have already produced.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
n\in\frac{1}{e}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then

(1)

\varphi\left(L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,n^{+}}\right)\subset L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{\varphi(x),p\left(n+\frac{1}{e}\right)}.

(2)

If
n\geq\frac{1}{e}
, then
\varphi\left(L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,n}\right)\subset L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{\varphi(x),pn}.

Proof.

It follows from
Lemma

5.1
that
\varphi^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x,n^{+}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x,p\left(n+\frac{1}{e}\right)}
.
By
Proposition

3.28
(Iwahori decomposition) we therefore have

\varphi\left(L^{+}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x,n^{+}}\right)=\varphi\left(L^{+}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x,n^{+}}\right)\subset L^{+}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}_{x,p\left(n+\frac{1}{e}\right)}=L^{+}_{0}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{x,p\left(n+\frac{1}{e}\right)}.

Taking
\Gamma
-fixed points, we then obtain (1) by
Corollary

3.29
.
The proof of (2) is similar.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
n\in\frac{1}{e}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then

(1)

\varphi_{c}\left(L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,n^{+}}\right)\subset L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,p\left(n+\frac{1}{e}\right)}.

(2)

If
n\geq\frac{1}{e}
, then
\varphi_{c}\left(L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,n}\right)\subset L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,pn}.

Proof.

This is immediate from
Lemma

5.2
and the fact that conjugation by
c
preserves the congruence level.
∎

5.1.1.
The unramified case with genericity assumptions

In the unramified case (i. e.
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
) we will improve the bounds of
Lemma

5.2
with additional genericity conditions on
x
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
.
Let
d\in\mathbb{R}
and assume that
x
is
d
-generic according to
Definition

2.46
.
For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

(1)

\varphi\left(L_{0}^{+}\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,n}\right)\subset L_{0}^{+}\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,\varphi(x),pn+d}
.

(2)

\varphi\left(L_{0}^{+}\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{n^{+}}\right)\subset L_{0}^{+}\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{p(n+1)}
. (This is actually true without the genericity assumption.)

Proof.

We first show (1).
Let
R
be an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra.
Using the pinning of
\widehat{G}
we identify
L_{0}^{+}\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,x,n}(R)=v^{\lceil-\langle a,x-o\rangle+n\rceil}R^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket
and
L_{0}^{+}\breve{\mathcal{U}}_{a,\varphi(x),pn+d}(R)=v^{\lceil-\langle a,\varphi(x)-o\rangle+pn+d\rceil}R^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket
.
Since
\varphi\left(v^{\lceil-\langle a,x-o\rangle+n\rceil}R^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket\right)\subset v^{p\lceil-\langle a,x-o\rangle+n\rceil}R^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket
it suffices to show that

(35)

p\lceil-\langle a,x-o\rangle+n\rceil\geq\lceil-\langle a,\varphi(x)-o\rangle+pn+d\rceil.

The assumption that
x
is
d
-generic implies that

-n_{a}-1+\frac{d}{p}+n<-\langle a,x-o\rangle+n<-n_{a}-\frac{d}{p}+n,

where
n_{a}\in\mathbb{Z}
is as in
Definition

2.46
.
Multiplying by
p
, we obtain

-pn_{a}-p+d+n<-\langle a,\varphi(x)-o\rangle+pn<-pn_{a}-d+pn.

Therefore,

p\lceil-\langle a,x-o\rangle+n\rceil=-pn_{a}+pn>-\langle a,\varphi(x)-o\rangle+pn+d,

which implies (
35
).

Next, we show (2).
We can identify
L_{0}^{+}\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{n^{+}}(R)=1+v^{\lceil n^{+}\rceil}R^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket=1+v^{n+1}R^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket
,
and then
\varphi\left(1+v^{n+1}R^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket v\rrbracket\right)\subset 1+v^{p(n+1)}R\llbracket v\rrbracket=L_{0}^{+}\breve{\mathcal{T}}_{p(n+1)}(R)
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
.
Let
d\in\mathbb{R}
, and assume that
x
is
d
-generic.
Then for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

(1)

\varphi\left(L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,n^{+}}\right)\subset L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{\varphi(x),pn+d}
.

(2)

\varphi_{c}\left(L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,n^{+}}\right)\subset L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,pn+d}
.

Proof.

It follows from
Lemma

5.4
that
\varphi\left(L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,n^{+}}\right)\subset L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{V}}_{x,pn+d}
(where we use that
d<p
because
x
is
d
-generic).
So by
Proposition

3.28
(Iwahori decomposition) we have
\varphi\left(L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{x,n^{+}}\right)\subset L^{+}_{0}\breve{\mathcal{G}}_{\varphi(x),pn+d}
.
Taking
\Gamma_{0}
-fixed points, we obtain (1).
As in the proof of
Lemma

5.3
, (2) is an
immediate consequence of (1).
∎

5.2.
Congruence bounds for conjugation

Define

h_{\mu}=\max_{a\in\Phi(G^{*},T^{*})}\langle a,\mu\rangle,

the “height” of
\mu
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
n\geq h_{\mu}
be an integer, and let
R
be
an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra. For all

X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)
and all

A\in L^{+n}\mathcal{G}(R)
we have

XAX^{-1}\in L^{+(n-h_{\mu})}\mathcal{G}(R)
.

Proof.

Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
as the pullback

\begin{CD}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}@>{}>{}>L^{+(n-h_{\mu})}\mathcal{G}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
L\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathcal{O}}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}@>{}>{}>L\mathcal{G},\end{CD}

where the bottom horizontal map is given by

(X,A)\mapsto XAX^{-1}
. We have to show that

L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathcal{O}}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}

as closed ind-subschemes of

L\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathcal{O}}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}
. Since both

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
and

L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathcal{O}}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}
are
stable under left translations by
L^{+}\mathcal{G}
(in the first
factor), it suffices to prove that

\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}\times_{\mathcal{O}}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{X}:=\left[L^{+}\mathcal{G}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\right]

as closed subschemes of

\operatorname{Gr}\times_{\mathcal{O}}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}
.
Note that

\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}\times_{\mathcal{O}}L^{+n}\mathcal{G}
is the
reduced closure of its generic fiber

\operatorname{Gr}_{E}^{\leq\mu}\times_{E}L^{+n}G^{*}_{E}
by
Lemma

B.5
.
Therefore it suffices to prove that we have the inclusion

\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{G},E}^{\leq\mu}\times_{E}L^{+n}G^{*}_{E}\subset\mathcal{X},

which can be checked on
\overline{E}
-points because the left hand side is reduced by
Lemma

B.4
.
By definition, this amounts to the statement that

\forall X\in L^{\leq\mu}G_{E}^{*}(\overline{E}),\,\,\forall A\in L^{+n}G_{E}^{*}(\overline{E})\,:\,XAX^{-1}\in L^{+(n-h_{\mu})}G^{*}_{E}(\overline{E}).

By (
28
) (Cartan decomposition),
it suffices to show this for
X
of the form
X=(v+p)^{\nu}
, where
\nu\in X_{*}(T^{*}_{E})
is dominant and
\nu\leq\mu
,
since the set of
X
that satisfy the condition for all
A
are
stable under left and right translation by elements of

L^{+}G_{E}^{*}(\overline{E})
.
By Iwahori decomposition for

L^{+n}G_{E}^{*}(\overline{E})
(here we use
n\geq 1
) it suffices to show that

\operatorname{Ad}_{(v+p)^{\nu}}L^{+n}U_{a}\subset L^{+n-h_{\mu}}U_{a}

for all

a\in\Phi(G^{*}_{E},T^{*}_{E})
, where
U_{a}\subset G_{E}^{*}
is the root subgroup corresponding to
a
.
But
indeed, we have

\operatorname{Ad}_{(v+p)^{\nu}}L^{+n}U_{a}=L^{+n+\langle a,\nu\rangle}U_{a}\subset L^{+n-h_{\mu}}U_{a}

because
n+\langle a,\nu\rangle\geq n-h_{\mu}
.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

Let
a\in\Phi(G^{*}_{E},T^{*}_{E})
be a root for which
h_{\mu}=\langle a,\mu\rangle
.
Then
n+\langle-a,\mu\rangle=n-h_{\mu}
, showing that the bound above is sharp.

Remark 5.8
.

Note that the proof is purely group theoretic, and does not rely on choosing an embedding into
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
.

5.2.1.
Bounds in terms of
v

Recall that
L\mathcal{G}=L_{0}\mathcal{G}
and
L^{+}\mathcal{G}=L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}
when
p
is nilpotent in
R
.
But as we pointed out in
Section

3.2.2
, it is typically still the case that
L^{+n}_{0}\mathcal{G}\neq L^{+n}\mathcal{G}
.
We can combine
Lemma

3.25
and
Lemma

5.6
to obtain bounds in terms of
L^{+n}_{0}\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let

a,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
be such that

n-h_{\mu}-2a+2\geq 0.

Then for any

\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R
with
p^{a}R=0
, all

X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)
and all

A\in L^{+n}_{0}\mathcal{G}(R)
we have

XAX^{-1}\in L^{+n-h_{\mu}-2a+2}_{0}\mathcal{G}(R)
.

Proof.

We have

A\in L^{+n}_{0}\mathcal{G}(R)\subset L^{+n-a+1}\mathcal{G}(R)
by

Lemma

3.25
, so by
Lemma

5.6
we have

XAX^{-1}\in L^{+n-a+1-h_{\mu}}\mathcal{G}(R)
, and by
Lemma

3.25
again we have

L^{+n-a+1-h_{\mu}}\mathcal{G}(R)\subset L^{+n-h_{\mu}-2a+2}_{0}\mathcal{G}(R)
.
∎

5.3.
Contraction and straightening

We will now combine the earlier results of this chapter to discuss when the
c
-twisted
\varphi
-conjugation can be “straightened” into a left translation action, meaning that the orbits for both actions coincide.
The consequences will be explored in
Section

6
.
We will first focus on the unramified case with additional genericity assumptions, and then the general case.

Recall that the group
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
comes equipped with a complete metric, namely the
v
-adic metric
(which is described in detail in
Section

B.2
).

Lemma 5.10

(Contraction)
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
.
Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
,
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{0^{+}\}
, and
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Assume that
x
is
d
-generic, and

(36)

(p-1)\lfloor f\rfloor+d-h_{\mu}-2a+2>0.

Let
R
be an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra such that
p^{a}R=0
and let
X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)
.
Then the operator

\displaystyle\Psi:L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)

\displaystyle\to L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1}

is well-defined, and it is a contraction for the
v
-adic metric.

Proof.

Since
p^{a}R=0
we can identify
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)=L^{+}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
.
Note that (
36
) implies that
p\lfloor f\rfloor+d>\lfloor f\rfloor+h_{\mu}\geq 1
for all possible parameters.

Let
A\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
.
We have
\varphi_{c}(A)\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,p\lfloor f\rfloor+d}(R)
by
Lemma

5.5
(2).
Then
X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1}\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,p\lfloor f\rfloor+d-h_{\mu}-2a+2}(R)
by
Lemma

5.9
.
By (
36
) again,
\Psi(A)=X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1}\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,\lfloor f\rfloor+1}(R)
.
This shows that
\Psi
is well-defined since
\lfloor f\rfloor+1>f
.

Since
f
was arbitrary (the only restraint being satisfying (
36
)), the argument in the previous paragraph shows that
\Psi
maps congruence subgroups into deeper ones, and it is therefore a contraction with respect to the
v
-adic metric.
∎

Lemma 5.11

(Straightening)
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
.
Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
,
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{0^{+}\}
, and
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Assume that
x
is
d
-generic, and

(p-1)\lfloor f\rfloor+d-h_{\mu}-2a+2>0.

Let
R
be an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra such that
p^{a}R=0
and let
X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)
.
Then we have the following:

(1)

For all
A\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
there exists a unique
B\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
such that
X\star A=BX
.

(2)

For all
B\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
there exists a unique
A\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
such that
X\star A=BX
.

Proof.

Let us first show (1). Given
A
we are forced to let

B=A^{-1}X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1},

and we have to show that
B\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
. This is
immediate from
Lemma

5.10
.

It remains to show (2). Consider the operator

\displaystyle\Psi_{B}:L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)

\displaystyle\to L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1}B^{-1}.

This is well-defined and in fact a contraction in the
v
-adic
topology by
Lemma

5.10
. By Banach’s fixed point
theorem it follows that
\Psi_{B}
has a unique fixed point and this
is our
A
.
∎

We also have the following variant, which works without the unramified assumption at the cost of giving a weaker bound.

Lemma 5.12

(Straightening)
.

Let
f,a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, where

(p-1)f-h_{\mu}-2a+2>0.

Let
R
be an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra such that
p^{a}R=0
and let
X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)
.
Then we have the following:

(1)

For all
A\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
there exists a unique
B\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
such that
X\star A=BX
.

(2)

For all
B\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
there exists a unique
A\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
such that
X\star A=BX
.

Proof.

As with the proof of
Lemma

5.11
, the key is to show that the map

\displaystyle\Psi_{B}:L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)

\displaystyle\to L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1}B^{-1}

is a contraction. This can be shown as in the proof of
Lemma

5.10
, using
Lemma

5.3
in place of
Lemma

5.5
.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

Note that
Lemma

5.11
with
f=0^{+}
and
a=1
is closely related to
[
Le+23
, Lemma 5.2.2]
,
and
Lemma

5.12
is closely related to
[
PR09
, Proposition 2.2]
.

5.4.
Infinitesimal contraction and straightening

We will also need an infinitesimal version of the straightening lemma,
for the two different actions of
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)

on
L\mathcal{G}(R)
, given a square zero extension
J\to R\to\overline{R}
.
This in turn will be based on an infinitesimal
version of the contraction lemma. A key feature of working with the Lie algebra
is that we get the bounds for
Lemma

5.11
and
Lemma

5.12

with
a=1
as long as
pJ=0
.

5.4.1.
Some remarks about the Lie algebra action

First, we will say some words about the Lie algebra action.
Let
J\to R\twoheadrightarrow\overline{R}
be a square zero
extension of
\varpi
-adically complete
\mathcal{O}
-algebras. By definition, we have an exact sequence
of groups

1\to\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)\to L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)\to L^{+}G(\overline{R})\to 1.

The Lie algebra
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)
has an
abelian
group structure and a compatible
\overline{R}
-module structure
[
DG80
, Theorem II.4.3.5]
.
Note that
\varphi
as well as conjugation

\operatorname{Ad}_{B}
by any element
B\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)

are
natural
group homomorphisms and therefore restrict to an action on the Lie
algebra
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)
, which we also denote
by
\varphi
and
\operatorname{Ad}_{B}
, respectively.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{X}
is any
\mathcal{O}
-scheme
with an
L^{+}\mathcal{G}
-action. Let

\overline{X}\in\mathcal{X}(\overline{R})
and let

\mathcal{X}(R)_{\overline{X}}
denote the fiber of
X
with respect
to the reduction map
\mathcal{X}(R)\to\mathcal{X}(\overline{R})
.
Let
L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)_{\overline{X}}
be the preimage in

L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)
of the stabilizer of
\overline{X}
in

L^{+}\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})
. Then

L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)_{\overline{X}}
is precisely the stabilizer of

\mathcal{X}(R)_{\overline{X}}
in
L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)
(but not
the pointwise stabilizer in general). In particular,

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)
acts on

\mathcal{X}(R)_{\overline{X}}
and in fact

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)
is the stabilizer of

\mathcal{X}(R)_{\overline{X}}
if and only if the stabilizer of

\overline{X}
in
L^{+}\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})
is trivial.

We now specialize to the case
\mathcal{X}=L\mathcal{G}
. Then for
any
\overline{X}\in L\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})
the left
translation action and
(\varphi,b)
-twisted conjugation action give
two induced actions of
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)
on

L\mathcal{G}_{\overline{X}}
:

\displaystyle B\cdot X

\displaystyle=BX,

\displaystyle X\star A

\displaystyle=A^{-1}X\varphi_{c}(A).

5.4.2.
Infinitesimal contraction and straightening

If
J\to R\to\overline{R}
is a square zero extension of

\mathcal{O}
-algebras, we can equip

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)
with the unique metric so that

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)\subset L^{+}\mathcal{G}(R)
is
a metric subspace with respect to the
v
-adic metric. This is the

v
-adic metric on
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}(J)
.

Lemma 5.14

(Infinitesimal contraction)
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
.
Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
, and
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{0^{+}\}
.
Assume that
x
is
d
-generic, and

(p-1)\lfloor f\rfloor+d-h_{\mu}>0.

Let
J\to R\twoheadrightarrow\overline{R}
be a square
zero extension of
p
-adically complete
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, where
pJ=0
.
Let
X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)
, and consider the operator

\displaystyle\Psi:L\mathcal{G}(R)

\displaystyle\to L\mathcal{G}(R)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1}.

Then
\Psi
restricts to

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(J)\to\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(J)
,
and this is a contraction with respect to the
v
-adic metric on

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(J)
.

Proof.

The proof follows the outline of the proof in the
non-infinitesimal case. We give the proof for
f=0^{+}
and note that the proof for
f=n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
is similar.

We first show that
\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{0^{+}}(J)
is
stable under
\Psi
. Let

A\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{0^{+}}(J)\subset L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{0^{+}}(R)
.
By
Lemma

5.5
we have a morphism of
group schemes
\varphi_{c}:L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{0^{+}}\to L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{d}
which therefore induces

\varphi_{c}:\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{0^{+}}(J)\to\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{d}(J)
.
Since
pJ=0
we have

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{d}(J)=\operatorname{Lie}L^{+d}\mathcal{G}(J)

by
Lemma

3.27
. The morphism

\operatorname{Ad}_{X}:L^{+d}\mathcal{G}\to L^{+d-h_{\mu}}\mathcal{G}

from
Lemma

5.6

induces

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+d}\mathcal{G}(J)\to\operatorname{Lie}L^{+d-h_{\mu}}\mathcal{G}(J)
,
and we have

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+d-h_{\mu}}\mathcal{G}(J)=\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{d-h_{\mu}}(J)\subset\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{0^{+}}(J)

since
pJ=0
and
d-h_{\mu}>0
.

It remains to show that
\Psi
is a contraction. Towards this end it
suffices to show that
\Psi
maps any Lie congruence subgroup

\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{n}
, where

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, into a deeper Lie congruence subgroup. This follows from the
f=n
version of
the argument above.
∎

Lemma 5.15

(Infinitesimal straightening)
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
.
Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
, and
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{0^{+}\}
.
Assume that
x
is
d
-generic, and

(p-1)\lfloor f\rfloor+d-h_{\mu}>0.

Let
J\to R\twoheadrightarrow\overline{R}
be a square
zero extension of
p
-adically complete
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, where
pJ=0
.
Let
X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)_{\overline{X}}
. Then:

(1)

For any
A\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)
there exists a
unique
B\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)
such
that
B\cdot X=X\star A
.

(2)

For any
B\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)
there exists a
unique
A\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)
such
that
B\cdot X=X\star A
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of
Lemma

5.11
.
Let us first prove (1). Given
A
we are forced to let

B=A^{-1}X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1},

and we have to show that

B\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(J)
, which follows
from
Lemma

5.14
.

It remains to show (2). Given
B
, consider the operator

\displaystyle\Psi_{B}:\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto X\varphi_{c}(A)X^{-1}B^{-1}.

This is a contraction by
Lemma

5.14
, so

\Psi_{B}
has a unique fixed point by Banach’s fixed point theorem.
∎

We also have the following version of infinitesimal straightening which holds without the unramified and genericity assumptions.

Lemma 5.16

(Infinitesimal straightening)
.

Let
f,a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, where

(p-1)f-h_{\mu}>0.

Let
J\to R\twoheadrightarrow\overline{R}
be a square
zero extension of
p
-adically complete
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, where
pJ=0
.
Let
X\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)_{\overline{X}}
. Then:

(1)

For any
A\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)
there exists a
unique
B\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)
such
that
B\cdot X=X\star A
.

(2)

For any
B\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)
there exists a
unique
A\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(J)
such
that
B\cdot X=X\star A
.

Proof.

Just like the proof of
Lemma

5.15
(including
Lemma

5.14
), using
Lemma

5.3
in place of
Lemma

5.5
.
∎

6.
The local model theorem

In this section we apply the calculations of
Section

5
towards comparing the stacks
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
and
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
.
In particular, we prove our main results
Theorem

6.4
and
Theorem

6.6
.

6.1.
Consequences of straightening

The upshot of straightening is that it enables us to relate the stacks
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
and
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
.
We now point out some of the most immediate consequences.
In the situation of
Lemma

5.11
or
Lemma

5.12
, we have an isomorphism of stacks

(37)

\mathcal{Y}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu},

where the subscript
\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
denotes base change to
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
.
We point out two special cases of (
37
):

•

If
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
and
x
is
d
-generic, where
d>h_{\mu}
is an integer, then

(38)

\mathcal{Y}_{0^{+},\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{0^{+},\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}.

•

As long as
n>h_{\mu}/(p-1)
, we have

(39)

\mathcal{Y}_{n,\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{n,\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}.

We have the following consequence of (
37
).

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{0^{+}\}
.
Then the stack
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
is a
p
-adic formal algebraic stack over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

Note that in order to prove that
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
is a
p
-adic formal algebraic stack, it suffices by
[
EG23
, Proposition A.13]

to show that
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}
is an algebraic stack over
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
for every
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
For
n\gg 0
we have by
Lemma

5.12
a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu},}

where both arrows are torsors for the smooth affine group scheme
L^{n}\mathcal{G}_{x}
from
Section

3.2.2
.
Since
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}
is a projective scheme over
\mathcal{O}
,
the
L^{n}\mathcal{G}
-torsor
\operatorname{Gr}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}
is also a scheme.
The quotient stack
