\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}=\left[\mathcal{Y}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}/L^{n}\mathcal{G}\right]

is then algebraic by
[
Sta
,
Theorem 06DC
]
.

The above completes the proof in the case
f=0
, so assume now that
f\neq 0
.
Algebraicity of
\mathcal{Y}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}
follows from algebraicity of
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}
.
Indeed, note that
\widetilde{Y}:=\mathcal{Y}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\times_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}}\mathcal{Y}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\to\mathcal{Y}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}

is an
L^{f}\mathcal{G}
-torsor if
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, a
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}
-torsor if
f=0^{+}
.
Either way it is a torsor for a smooth affine group scheme, so
\widetilde{Y}
is representable by a scheme since
\mathcal{Y}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}
is.
And the map
\widetilde{Y}\to\mathcal{Y}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}
is smooth surjective since
\mathcal{Y}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}
is.

We have to show that the diagonal
\Delta:\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}\to\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}\times\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
is representable by by algebraic spaces, in fact it is representable by affine schemes.
This follows from the statement that if
R
is an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra and
X,Y\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R)
, then the sheaf over
R

defined by
R^{\prime}\mapsto\{A\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}(R^{\prime}):A^{-1}X\varphi_{c}(A)=Y\}
is affine.
But this is clearly a closed subscheme of
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,f}
which is known to be an affine scheme.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

It follows from the proof that the morphism
\mathcal{Y}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
satisfies any property satisfied by
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
which
is smooth local on the source. In particular, it is locally of finite presentation
[
Sta
,
Remark 06FC
]
.
Moreover
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}
also satisifes any property satisfied by
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}
which is smooth local, some of which are listed in
[
Sta
,
Remark 04YH
]
.
In particular,
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}
is locally noetherian, and
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{f,\mathbb{F}}
is reduced under the assumption that
p\nmid\pi_{1}(G^{*}_{\text{der}})
by
[
PZ13
, Theorem 9.1]
.

Remark 6.3
.

Since
\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
has affine diagonal as shown in the proof, it satisfies descent for the fpqc topology by
[
Sta
,
Proposition 0GRH
]
.

6.2.
Smooth equivalence

We remind the reader that the current setup, including standing assumptions, are summarized in
Section

2.7
.
The following is one of our main results.

Theorem 6.4
.

There exists a
p
-adic formal scheme
Z^{\wedge\varpi}
and a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\wedge\varpi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}}

where both morphisms are smooth covers.

The starting point for the proof is straightening over the special fiber, so we make the following assumption.

Assumption Straight
.

We fix
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{0^{+}\}
and assume that one of the following holds:

(I)

I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, and
x
is
d
-generic, where
d>h_{\mu}
is an integer.

(II)

f>h_{\mu}/(p-1)
.

Then, as pointed out in
Section

6.1
, the quotient stacks

(40)

\mathcal{Y}_{f,\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{f,\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}

are isomorphic.
On the other hand, there is no obvious map between
\mathcal{Y}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}
and
\operatorname{Gr}_{f,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}

for some arbitrary
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
17
17

17

Although straightening dictates that
\mathcal{Y}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}
for
n\gg 0
, we are now in the situation that
f
is fixed.

But in some sense, the lack of an obvious map is the only issue.
The idea is that if we are able to define a map
\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
Zariski locally,
then this map will automatically be an isomorphism between open neighborhoods, as we will show in
Lemma

6.5
.

6.2.1.
Proof of
Theorem

6.4

Throughout the proof we keep
Assumption Straight
.
Let
U^{\wedge\varpi}
be a formal scheme over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathcal{O}

and
g:U^{\wedge\varpi}\to L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}
a morphism over
\operatorname{Spf}\mathcal{O}
.
We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\wedge\varpi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{Y}}

where by definition
g_{G}
and
g_{Y}
are the maps making the diagram commutative.

Lemma 6.5
.

We have the following:

(1)

g_{G}
is smooth if and only if
g_{Y}
is smooth.

(2)

g_{G}
is étale if and only if
g_{Y}
is étale.

(3)

g_{G}
is an open immersion if and only if
g_{Y}
is an open immersion.

Proof.

We prove the implications
g_{G}
is smooth/étale/an open immersion implies that so is
g_{Y}
.
The arguements will be sufficiently symmetric that they can also be used prove the converse, but we leave it to the reader to check the details.

By definition it suffices to prove the statements after
base change to
\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, so
from now on we assume that we are over
\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
but we
suppress the subcripts normally used to indicate this fact.
Part (1) will take up the majority of the proof.
Throughout we make the assumption that
g_{G}
is smooth.

Let
\widetilde{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{n,\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}}^{\leq\mu}

be as in the proof of
Proposition

6.1
.
Consider the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{Y}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{Y}}

Then
g_{Y}
is smooth if and only if
g_{Y}^{\prime}
is smooth.
It will be useful to observe that for
R
an
\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
-algebra we have

\widetilde{Y}(R)\cong L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)/^{c,\varphi}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{n}(R)\cong L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{n}(R)\backslash L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(R)

by
Proposition

4.13
. In particular, we can identify

U^{\prime}(R)=\left\{\left([X],u\right)\in\widetilde{Y}(R)\times U(R):\exists A\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(R)\text{ such that }X\star A=g(u)\right\}.

Note that
\widetilde{Y}
is a locally noetherian scheme by
Remark

6.2
, and
U^{\prime}\to\widetilde{Y}
is locally of finite type since
U
is locally of finite type over
\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
by the smoothness assumption on
g_{G}
.
So in order to show that
g_{Y}^{\prime}
is smooth, it suffices by
[
Sta
,
Lemma 02HY
]

to show that for any small
18
18

18

The extension being
small
implies that
pJ=0
.
extension

J\to R\twoheadrightarrow\overline{R}
of Artinian local

\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
-algebras of finite type over

\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{a}
,
the lifting problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}\overline{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Spec}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[X]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([\overline{X}],\overline{u})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{Y}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([X],u)}

has a solution.
A solution to the lifting problem will be provided by a
u\in U(R)
and a
C\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(R)
such that
X\star C=g(u)
.

Note that commutativity of the diagram means that there exists
\overline{A}\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(\overline{R})

such that
\overline{X}\star\overline{A}=g(\overline{u})
. Choose
A\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(R)
lifting
\overline{A}
(which exists by formal smoothness of
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}
),
and let
Y=X\star A
.
Then
\overline{Y}=g(\overline{u})
, so by smoothness of
g_{G}
there exists
u\in U(R)
and
B\in L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(R)
such that
BY=g(u)
.
Since
Y
and
g(u)
reduce to the same point
\overline{Y}=g(\overline{u})
in
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}(\overline{R})
,
in fact
B\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(J)
.
By
Assumption Straight
and infinitesimal straightening
19
19

19

More precisely, by
Lemma

5.15
if we are in situation (I) of
Assumption Straight
, by
Lemma

5.16
if we are in situation (II) of
Assumption Straight
.

there exists
B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Lie}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}(J)
such that
g(u)=BY=Y\star B^{\prime}=X\star(AB^{\prime})
,
so
u
and
C=AB^{\prime}
provides a solution to the lifting problem.

Now let us show (2), so assume that
g_{G}
is étale, or equivalently
smooth of relative dimension 0
[
Sta
,
Section 039P
]
.
By (1) it follows that
g_{Y}
is smooth so we only need to show that
g_{Y}
is of relative dimension
0. This can be checked after base change to
\mathbb{F}
, where it is a consequence of (
40
)
and the assumption that
g_{G}
is étale.

Finally, let us show (3). Being an open immersion is equivalent to being
universally injective and étale
[
Sta
,
Section 025F
]
,
so by part (2) it suffices to show that
g_{Y}
is universally
injective if
g_{G}
is an open immersion. Being universally injective can be checked after base change to
\mathbb{F}
,
so
g_{Y}
is universally injective because of (
40
) and the assumption that
g_{G}
is an open immersion.
∎

Zariski local triviality of the
L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{f}
-torsor
L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
follows from
Corollary

4.14
.
So let
\{Z_{i}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be a Zariski open covering for which there are lifts
g_{i}:Z_{i}\to L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}
.
By
Lemma

6.5
the associated maps
Z_{i}^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
are also open immersions, and the collection

\{Z_{i}^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is an open covering by (
40
).
The compositions
Z_{i}^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}

and
Z_{i}^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}

are both smooth, being a composition of smooth maps (where we use that
L^{f}\mathcal{G}
is a smooth affine group scheme).
So taking
Z=\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}Z_{i}
, we obtain a diagram as in
Theorem

6.4
.
This completes the proof of
Theorem

6.4
.

6.3.
Explicit smooth equivalence

Make
Assumption Straight
.
Let
g:U^{\wedge\varpi}\to L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}^{\wedge\varpi}
be a morphism for which
g_{G}:U^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}

and
g_{Y}:U^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
are open immersions (or equivalently by
Lemma

6.5
, one of them is an open immersion).
The composites
U^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}
and
U^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}

are smooth, being the composite of smooth maps, hence universally open by
[
Sta
,
Lemma 056G
]
.
So the images of these smooth maps are open substacks
U_{G}^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}
and
U_{Y}^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
.
The situation is summarized by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\wedge\varpi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{Y}^{\wedge\varpi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{G}^{\wedge\varpi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}}}

Both diagonal arrows are smooth coverings. Note that:

(1)

If the open subscheme
g_{G}:U^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{f}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}
is stable under action of
L^{f}\mathcal{G}
by left translations, then
U^{\wedge\varpi}\twoheadrightarrow U^{\wedge\varpi}_{G}
is an
L^{f}\mathcal{G}
-torsor for this action. Here, we use the convention
L^{0^{+}}\mathcal{G}:=\overline{\mathcal{T}}
.

(2)

If the open subscheme
g_{Y}:U^{\wedge\varpi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{f}^{\leq\mu}
is stable under the action of
L^{f}\mathcal{G}
by
c
-twisted
\varphi
-conjugation, then
U^{\wedge\varpi}\twoheadrightarrow U_{Y}^{\wedge\varpi}
is an
L^{f}\mathcal{G}
-torsor for this action.

We deal with one special case where (1) and (2) above is satisfied, corresponding to situation (I) of
Assumption Straight
.

Theorem 6.6
.

Assume that
I
acts trivially on
\widehat{G}
, invoke
Assumption Dil
, assume that
p\nmid\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})
, and assume that
x
is
d
-generic, where
d>h_{\mu}
is an integer.
Then there are Zariski open covers
U(z_{1})^{\wedge\varpi}\cup\cdots\cup U(z_{n})^{\wedge\varpi}\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu,\wedge\varpi}

and
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}(z_{1})\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}(z_{n})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}

such that for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
there is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{U}(z_{i})^{\wedge\varpi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}(z_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(z_{i})^{\wedge\varpi},}

where both maps are
\widehat{T}
-torsors.
Moreover, the open charts
U(z_{i})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
are the explicit charts from
Section

4.1.6
labeled by elements of the admissible set
\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)
.

Proof.

The assumptions ensure that
\mathcal{Y}_{0^{+},\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{0^{+},\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}
by (
38
), as a consequence of
Lemma

5.11
(straightening).
The group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the dilation of
\widehat{G}
in the Borel
\widehat{B}
at
v=0
by
Assumption Dil
,
and
\mathcal{G}_{x,0^{+}}
is then the dilation of
\widehat{G}
in the unipotent radical
\widehat{U}\subset\widehat{B}
at
v=0
by
Example

3.22
.
Since
p\nmid\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})
, we can take
U(z_{1}),\dots,U(z_{n})
as in
Section

4.1.6
, giving an open covering of
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}
.
Let
\widetilde{U}(z_{1}),\dots,\widetilde{U}(z_{n})
denote the preimages of
U(z_{1}),\dots,U(z_{n})
in
\operatorname{Gr}^{\leq\mu}_{0^{+}}
, which since

L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}/L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x,0^{+}}=\overline{\mathcal{T}}=\widehat{T}
by
Section

3.2.5
is given by the orbits under the action of
\widehat{T}\cdot L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
From
Lemma

3.35
we see that

\displaystyle\widehat{T}\cdot L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=\left\{A\in\mathcal{G}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{v+p}\right]\right):A\mod\frac{1}{v+p}\in\widehat{B}^{\text{op}}(R)\text{ and }A\mod\frac{v}{v+p}\in\widehat{B}\left(R\left[\frac{1}{p}\right]\right)\right\}.

In particular, note that
\widehat{T}\cdot L^{--}\mathcal{G}_{x}
is stable under both left and right translation by
\widehat{T}
, and consequently the open charts
\widetilde{U}(z_{1}),\dots,\widetilde{U}(z_{n})

are stable under both actions of
\widehat{T}
.
If we define
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}(z_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}(z_{n})\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
as the images of
\widetilde{U}(z_{1})^{\wedge\varpi},\dots,\widetilde{U}(z_{n})^{\wedge\varpi}
under the
\widehat{T}
-torsor
\mathcal{Y}_{0^{+}}^{\leq\mu}\to\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
,
the conclusion of the theorem is satisfied.
∎

6.4.
Odds and ends: The geometry of
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}

Make
Assumption Straight
.
Then we have a local model diagram over
\mathbb{F}

(41)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}_{f,\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}\cong\operatorname{Gr}_{f,\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu},}

where both arrows are
L^{f}\mathcal{G}
-torsors (and we use the convention
L^{0^{+}}\mathcal{G}=\overline{\mathcal{T}}
).
Let us now assume that we are in this situation.

Assume that
p\nmid\pi_{1}(G^{*})
.
Then the geometry of
\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}
is well understood due to
Theorem

4.3
.
Indeed, for each
\widetilde{w}\in\operatorname{Adm}^{x}(\mu)
(we abuse notation and write
\widetilde{w}
instead of
W_{x}\widetilde{w}W_{x}
) we have an affine Schubert cell
S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w})=L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}\widetilde{w}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}
whose closure is the affine Schubert variety
S(\widetilde{w})=\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Adm}^{x}(\mu),\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\leq\widetilde{w}}S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w}^{\prime})
(see
Section

4.1.3
for more details).
For each
\widetilde{w}\in\operatorname{Adm}^{x}(\mu)
we can then define an “affine Schubert cell”

S_{f}^{\circ}(\widetilde{w}):=L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}\widetilde{w}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{f,\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu},

the preimage of
S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w})
in
\operatorname{Gr}_{f,\mathbb{F}}^{\leq\mu}
.
Then the natural map
S_{f}^{\circ}(\widetilde{w})\to S^{\circ}(\widetilde{w})
is an
L^{f}\mathcal{G}
-torsor.
Since
S_{f}^{\circ}(\widetilde{w})
is stable under the
c
-twisted
\varphi
-conjugation action, it descends to a locally closed substack

\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F},\widetilde{w}}:=\left[S_{f}^{\circ}(\widetilde{w})/^{c,\varphi}L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}\right]\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F}}.

The analogue of these locally closed substacks in
[
CL18
, Section 5.1]
are referred to as
Kottwitz-Rapoport strata
.
We have a stratification

\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F}}=\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{w}\in\operatorname{Adm}^{x}(\mu)}\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F},\widetilde{w}},

and the closure of
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F},\widetilde{w}}
in
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F}}
is precisely

\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F},\leq\widetilde{w}}:=\bigsqcup_{\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Adm}^{x}(\mu),\widetilde{w}^{\prime}\leq\widetilde{w}}\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F},\widetilde{w}^{\prime}}.

Moreover, the irreducible components of
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F}}
are
\{\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\mathbb{F},\leq v^{w\mu}}\}_{w\in W}
, where
W
denotes the finite Weyl group of
G^{*}
.

In the literature on Breuil–Kisin modules with descent data, the analogue of an element in
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}(\mathbb{F})
is said to have
shape
(or
genre
)
\widetilde{w}
if it belongs to
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}_{\widetilde{w}}(\mathbb{F})
.
A defect with this notion of shape is that it is not an open condition for
\widetilde{w}\in\operatorname{Adm}^{x}(\mu)
which are not of maximal length (i. e.
\widetilde{w}\neq v^{w\mu}
for some
w\in W
), hence it is not stable under deformations.
For this reason the open substacks
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}(\widetilde{z})\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
from
Theorem

6.6
(when the conclusion of the theorem holds) are better suited for some purposes.
In
[
Le+23
, Section 5.2]
an element of
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}(\widetilde{z})(R)
is said to have a
\widetilde{z}
-gauge basis
.

7.
Examples

We have already given the following detailed examples:

(1)

In
Example

2.41
we worked out some Galois types for
\widehat{G}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}
, and in particular which ones are Frobenius invariant and which ones are not.

(2)

In
Example

3.10
we considered the question of when
\mathcal{G}_{x}
has connected fibers in the case
\widehat{G}=\operatorname{PGL}_{3}
.

In this section, we will give a few additional examples.

In
Section

7.1
we study the case of
\widehat{G}
being the dual group of
G=\operatorname{Res}^{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathrm{GL}_{3}
.
This may be helpful for readers familiar with
[
Le+23
]
, since it corresponds to the case of
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
over
K=\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}
in loc. cit.
In particular, we show how to compare inertial types as they appear in loc. cit. with our notion of Galois types and the corresponding points in the enlarged building of
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
.
We also give a very explicit description of the admissible set in one example.

In
Section

7.2
we consider the case of a tamely ramified unitary group.
This is the first example of a genuinely ramified group, where we have
G^{*}\neq\widehat{G}
.
(The reader will recall from
Remark

2.7
that we can undo any twisting in the unramified direction by taking a large coefficient field.)

In all examples, we assume that
\mathbb{F}
is large, so that we can identify
\mathcal{J}
with the set of embeddings
\mathbb{F}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}
as in
Example

2.1
.
We fix some embedding
\iota_{0}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}
and let
\iota_{j}=\iota_{0}\varphi^{-j}
for
j=0,1,\dots,r-1
.
Then
\iota_{0},\iota_{1},\dots,\iota_{r-1}
is a complete enumeration of the embeddings
\mathbb{F}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}
.

7.1.
Weil restriction of
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}

Let
G=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}}\mathrm{GL}_{3}
and
L=\mathbb{Q}_{p^{4}}((-p)^{1/(p^{4}-1)})
, and assume that
E
contains the Galois closure of
L
.
Then
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{3}\times\mathrm{GL}_{3}
.
The group
\Gamma^{\prime}=\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
acts on
\widehat{G}
by permuting the factors cyclically, and
\Gamma
acts via the quotient map
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
.
In particular,
\sigma
acts by the “swap” automorphism of
\widehat{G}
which we denote by
\psi
.
As a
\Gamma
-group we can identify
\widehat{G}=\operatorname{Ind}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}}}^{\Gamma}\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, where
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}}:=\operatorname{Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}})
is viewed as acting trivially.

7.1.1.
Galois type

We will make a concrete choice of a Galois type
\tau:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}\llbracket u\rrbracket)
.
First, we will describe
\tau
using the parametrization of
[
GHS18
, Section 9.2]
, as is done in
[
Le+23
]
.
This is primarily done to orient the reader and serves as a helpful example in comparing the notation of
[
Le+23
]
to ours.
We then describe how to view this same
\tau
as a point in the building
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
, following the classification from
Proposition

2.23
.

7.1.2.
Galois type in the manner of
[
Le+23
]

Let
s=\left((123),(12)\right)\in W
and
\mu=\left((16,11,7),(4,2,1)\right)\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})
.
Recall that
\eta=\left((2,1,0),(2,1,0)\right)
in this case, so
\mu+\eta=\left((18,12,7),(6,3,1)\right)
.
We assume that
p\geq 19
so that all entries of
\mu+\eta
are in the range
[0,p-1]
.
According to the parametrization of
[
GHS18
, Section 9.2]
we obtain a 1-cocycle
\tau(s,\mu+\eta):I\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F})
given by

\displaystyle\tau(s,\mu+\eta)=\bigg(

\displaystyle{\begin{pmatrix}\iota_{0}(\omega)^{18+3p+7p^{2}+p^{3}}&&\\
&\iota_{0}(\omega)^{12+6p+12p^{2}+6p^{3}}&\\
&&\iota_{0}(\omega)^{7+p+18p^{2}+3p^{3}}\end{pmatrix}},

\displaystyle{\begin{pmatrix}\iota_{0}(\omega)^{6+12p+6p^{2}+12p^{3}}&&\\
&\iota_{0}(\omega)^{3+7p+p^{2}+18p^{3}}&\\
&&\iota_{0}(\omega)^{1+18p+3p^{2}+7p^{3}}\end{pmatrix}}\bigg).

Note that we have

(42)

\tau(s,\mu+\eta)^{p}=s\psi(\tau(s,\mu+\eta))s^{-1},

so
\tau(s,\mu+\eta)
is an inertial type.

From
\tau(s,\mu+\eta)
we obtain a 1-cocycle
\tau:\Gamma\to\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{p^{4}})=\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{0})\times\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\times\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\times\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{3})
.
This is given by
\tau_{j}(\sigma)=s
for every
j\in\mathcal{J}=\{0,1,2,3\}
, and
\tau|_{I}=\omega^{\lambda}
,
20
20

20

The expression
\tau|_{I}=\omega^{\lambda}
means that the projection onto factor
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}_{j})
is given by
\iota_{j}(\omega)^{\lambda_{j}}
.

where

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}=\big(

\displaystyle(18+3p+7p^{2}+p^{3},12+6p+12p^{2}+6p^{3},7+p+18p^{2}+3p^{3}),

\displaystyle(6+12p+6p^{2}+12p^{3},3+7p+p^{2}+18p^{3},1+18p+3p^{2}+7p^{3}),\big)=s\psi(\lambda_{3}),

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=\big(

\displaystyle(1+18p+3p^{2}+7p^{3},6+12p+6p^{2}+12p^{3},3+7p+p^{2}+18p^{3}),

\displaystyle(12+6p+12p^{2}+6p^{3},18+3p+7p^{2}+p^{3},7+p+18p^{2}+3p^{3})\big)=s\psi(\lambda_{0}),

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}=\big(

\displaystyle(7+p+18p^{2}+3p^{3},12+6p+12p^{2}+6p^{3},18+3p+7p^{2}+p^{3}),

\displaystyle(6+12p+6p^{2}+12p^{3},1+18p+3p^{2}+7p^{3},3+7p+p^{2}+18p^{3})\big)=s\psi(\lambda_{1}),

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}=\big(

\displaystyle(3+7p+p^{2}+18p^{3},6+12p+6p^{2}+12p^{3},1+18p+3p^{2}+7p^{3}),

\displaystyle(12+6p+12p^{2}+6p^{3},7+p+18p^{2}+3p^{3},18+3p+7p^{2}+p^{3})\big)=s\psi(\lambda_{2}).

Note that for every
\iota_{j}\in\mathcal{J}
, we have
\tau_{0}|_{I}=\tau_{j}|_{I}:I\to\widehat{T}(\mathbb{F})
because
\tau_{j}|_{I}=\iota_{j}(\omega)^{\lambda_{j}}=\iota_{0}(\varphi^{-j}(\omega))^{p^{j}\lambda_{0}}=\iota_{0}(\omega)^{\lambda_{0}}
.
Note that
\varphi\tau=\tau
, so
\tau
is strictly Frobenius invariant.
Since
\sigma
acts on
\widehat{G}(\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{p^{4}})
as
\psi\circ\varphi=\varphi\circ\psi
, we see that (
42
) implies
the cocycle condition
\tau(\gamma)^{p}=\tau(\sigma)(^{\sigma}\tau(\gamma))\tau(\sigma)^{-1}
.
Note that we also have
\tau(\gamma)^{e}=1
since
\omega^{e}=1
and that

\left(\tau(\sigma)(^{\sigma}\tau(\sigma))(^{\sigma^{2}}\tau(\sigma))(^{\sigma^{3}}\tau(\sigma))\right)_{0}=s^{\prime}s^{\prime\prime}s^{\prime}s^{\prime\prime}=(13)^{2}=1,

where
s=(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})
, and similarly the entry corresponding to another
j\in\mathcal{J}
is equal to 1.

Define
w\in W^{\mathcal{J}}
by

\displaystyle w_{0}

\displaystyle=\left((123),(12)\right)=s,

\displaystyle w_{1}

\displaystyle=\left((13),(23)\right)=s\psi(w_{0}),

\displaystyle w_{2}

\displaystyle=\left((12),(321)\right)=s\psi(w_{1}),

\displaystyle w_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(1,1\right)=s\psi(w_{2}).

In fact,
w
is characterized by the property that
w^{-1}\lambda
is dominant, or even just making the leading
p^{3}
-coefficients dominant.
Let
n=w^{-1}u^{\lambda}.

Then it is straightforward to check that
\tau(\theta)=n^{-1}(^{\theta}n)
:

\displaystyle n^{-1}(^{\gamma}n)

\displaystyle=u^{-\lambda}(^{\gamma}u^{\lambda})=\omega^{\lambda}=\tau(\gamma),

\displaystyle(n^{-1}(^{\sigma}n))_{j}

\displaystyle=u^{-\lambda_{j}}w_{j}\psi(w_{j-1})^{-1}u^{\psi(\lambda_{j-1})}=u^{-\lambda_{j}+s\psi(\lambda_{j-1})}s=s.

We have the point
x=o-\frac{1}{p^{4}-1}w^{-1}\lambda\in\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right),
and for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
,

\varphi(x)_{j}=o_{j}-\frac{p}{p^{4}-1}w^{-1}_{j-1}\lambda_{j-1}\in\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{G},\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)
.
Now consider
c\in\widehat{N}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!))^{\Gamma}
with
c_{j}=\psi^{j}\left(s^{-1}v^{-\mu-\eta}\right)
.
Explicitly,

\displaystyle c_{0}=c_{2}

\displaystyle=((321),(12))v^{-((18,12,7),(6,3,1))},

\displaystyle c_{1}=c_{3}

\displaystyle=((12),(321))v^{-((6,3,1),(18,12,7))}.

Then we have
c\cdot\varphi(x)=x
. Indeed, for any
j\in\mathcal{J}
we have

c_{j}=w_{j}^{-1}w_{j-1}u^{w_{j-1}^{-1}(p\lambda_{j-1}-\lambda_{j})}=w_{j}^{-1}u^{p\lambda_{j-1}-\lambda_{j}}w_{j-1}.

Hence

\displaystyle c_{j}\cdot\varphi(x)_{j}

\displaystyle=o_{j}-w_{j}^{-1}\left(w_{j-1}\left(\frac{p}{p^{4}-1}w_{j-1}^{-1}\lambda_{j-1}\right)-\frac{p}{p^{4}-1}\lambda_{j-1}+\frac{1}{p^{4}-1}\lambda_{j}\right)=x_{j}.

Remark 7.1
.

Let us compare how our notation relates to that of
[
Le+23
]
, in particular
[
Le+23
, Section 5.1]
.
Note that loc. cit. works with the group
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
but only descent data with respect to
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}}
.
By Shapiro’s Lemma we have
H^{1}\left(\Gamma,\widehat{G}\left(\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q}\right)\right)\cong H^{1}\left(\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}},\mathrm{GL}_{3}\left(\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{q}\right)\right)
,
so we can specialize to their situation by projecting to a chosen factor of
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{3}\times\mathrm{GL}_{3}
and restricting the Galois action to
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{2}}}
.
What we call
\tau(s,\mu+\eta)
is precisely what is denoted by the same symbol in
[
Le+23
, Section 2.4]
.
What is called
\mathbf{a}^{\prime(j)}
and
s_{\text{or},j}^{\prime}
in
[
Le+23
, Section 5.1]
corresponds to the chosen
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
-coordinate of our
\lambda_{j}
and
w_{j}
, respectively.
The chosen
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
-coordinate of
c_{j}
makes an appearance in
[
Le+23
, Proposition 5.1.8]
.

7.1.3.
Galois type as a point in the building

We keep the notation from the previous subsection.
With
\lambda
and
w
as there, recall that we have
n=w^{-1}u^{\lambda}
and
x=n\cdot o=o-\frac{1}{e}w^{-1}\lambda\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)
, where
e=p^{4}-1
.
Recall from
Section

2.3
that
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)=\prod_{j=0}^{3}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)
.
So
x=(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, where
x_{j}=o_{j}-\frac{1}{e}w_{j}^{-1}\lambda_{j}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}_{j}(\!(u)\!)\right)
.
We have

\displaystyle w_{2}^{-1}\lambda_{2}=w_{0}^{-1}\lambda_{0}=\big(

\displaystyle(12+6p+12p^{2}+6p^{3},7+p+18p^{2}+3p^{3},18+3p+7p^{2}+p^{3}),

\displaystyle(3+7p+p^{2}+18p^{3},6+12p+6p^{2}+12p^{3},1+18p+3p^{2}+7p^{3})\big),

\displaystyle w_{3}^{-1}\lambda_{3}=w_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{1}=\big(

\displaystyle(3+7p+p^{2}+18p^{3},6+12p+6p^{2}+12p^{3},1+18p+3p^{2}+7p^{3}),

\displaystyle(12+6p+12p^{2}+6p^{3},7+p+18p^{2}+3p^{3},18+3p+7p^{2}+p^{3})\big).

Note that
x
is fixed by
\Gamma
since
\psi(x_{j-1})=x_{j}
for every
j\in\mathcal{J}
.
We will identify
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{\Gamma}
with
x_{0}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}_{0}(\!(u)\!)\right)
,
via the projection

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}_{0}(\!(u)\!)\right)
,
which yields an isomorphism

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}^{\mathcal{J}}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{\Gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}_{0}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{I}.

We may moreover identify
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\widehat{T},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(S,\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)\times\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(S,\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
, where
S\subset\mathrm{GL}_{3}
is the diagonal torus.
Then
x=(x^{(0)},x^{(1)})
, where each
x^{(i)}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(S,\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
, and in fact

\displaystyle x^{(0)}

\displaystyle=o-\frac{1}{e}\left(12+6p+12p^{2}+6p^{3},7+p+18p^{2}+3p^{3},18+3p+7p^{2}+p^{3}\right),

\displaystyle x^{(1)}

\displaystyle=o-\frac{1}{e}\left(3+7p+p^{2}+18p^{3},6+12p+6p^{2}+12p^{3},1+18p+3p^{2}+7p^{3}\right).

See
Fig.

4
on page
4
for an overview of the situation in the case
p=19
.
From
Fig.

4
we see that
x
is
2
-generic when
p=19
, something that can also easily be checked by hand.

7.1.4.
Bruhat–Tits group scheme

Let
\widehat{B}\subset\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{3}\times\mathrm{GL}_{3}
be the Borel which is
lower
triangular in each
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
-factor.
Then the Bruhat–Tits group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the dilation of
\widehat{G}
, viewed as a group scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}[v]
, in the Borel
\widehat{B}
along the origin
v=0
.
This is a consequence of
Proposition

3.16
(since
x
is lowest alcove according to
Definition

3.12
for the choice of positive roots corresponding to
\widehat{B}
).
It follows that for any
\mathcal{O}[v]
-algebra
R
in which
v
is a non-zerodivisor, we can identify

\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)=\{A\in\widehat{G}(R)|A\mod v\in\widehat{B}\left(R/vR\right)\}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle L_{0}^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)

\displaystyle=\{A\in\widehat{G}(R\llbracket v\rrbracket)|A\mod v\in\widehat{B}(R)\},

\displaystyle L_{0}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)

\displaystyle=\widehat{G}(R(\!(v)\!)).

Similarly, we have for a noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
R

\displaystyle L^{+}\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)

\displaystyle=\{A\in\widehat{G}(R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket)|A\mod v\in\widehat{B}(R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket/vR\llbracket v+p\rrbracket)\},

\displaystyle L\mathcal{G}_{x}(R)

\displaystyle=\{A\in\widehat{G}(R(\!(v+p)\!))|A\mod v\in\widehat{B}(R(\!(v+p)\!)/vR(\!(v+p)\!))\}.

The reason we impose the noetherian condition here is to ensure that
v
is a non-zerodivisor in
R\llbracket v+p\rrbracket
and
R(\!(v+p)\!)
.

Remark 7.2
.

In
[
Le+23
]
,
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the dilation in the
upper
triangular Borel for the same data as in
Section

7.1.2
.
The reason is the appearance of the dual in
[
Le+23
, Definition 5.1.3]
, which is adapted to the
contravariant
anti-equivalence between étale
\varphi
-modules and representations of
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{p,\infty})
.
Our notion of
type
from
Section

2.6
, which is better suited to the
covariant
functor, is therefore not compatible with
[
Le+23
, Definition 5.1.3]
.

7.1.5.
The admissible set and the moduli stack
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}

Let us take

\mu=\left((1,0,0),(1,0,0)\right)\in X_{*}(\widehat{T}).

Then
h_{\mu}=1
. Since
x
is
2
-generic,
Theorem

6.6
applies and we have a local model diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{U}(\widetilde{z})^{\wedge\varpi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}(\widetilde{z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(\widetilde{z})^{\wedge\varpi},}

where the elements
\widetilde{z}\in L^{\leq\mu}\mathcal{G}_{x}(\mathcal{O})
are representatives of elements in the
admissible set

\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)
that we described in
Section

4.1.3
.

As outlined in
Section

6.4
we can understand the underlying geometry of
\mathcal{Y}^{\leq\mu}
in terms of Kottwitz-Rapoport strata parametrized by elements of
\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)
.
To make this more concrete, we give an explicit description of
\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)
.

In the case of
\widehat{G}
, the Iwahori–Weyl group
\widetilde{W}^{*}=\widehat{N}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))/\widehat{T}(\mathbb{F}\llbracket v\rrbracket)\cong X_{*}(\widehat{T})\rtimes W
, where
W=\widehat{N}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))/\widehat{T}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
is the finite Weyl group, coincides with the extended affine Weyl group
W^{\text{ext}}
, because the same is true for
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
.
In fact,
\widetilde{W}^{*}=\widetilde{W}^{\prime}\times\widetilde{W}^{\prime}
, where
\widetilde{W}^{\prime}
is the Iwahori–Weyl group for
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
.
Moreover, the admissible set
\operatorname{Adm}(\mu)=\operatorname{Adm}(\mu^{\prime})\times\operatorname{Adm}(\mu^{\prime})\subset\widetilde{W}^{\prime}\times\widetilde{W}^{\prime}
, where
\mu^{\prime}=(1,0,0)
.
So let us just focus on the admissible set for
\mu^{\prime}
in
\widetilde{W}^{\prime}
.

Let
S\subset\mathrm{GL}_{3}
be the diagonal torus and let
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{A}\left(S,\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
be the base alcove containing
x^{(0)}
and
x^{(1)}
.
Simple reflections across the walls of
\mathcal{C}
are given by
\widetilde{s}_{1}=s_{(12)}
,
\widetilde{s}_{2}=s_{(23)}
,
\widetilde{s}_{3}=v^{(1,0,-1)}s_{(13)}=s_{(13)}v^{(-1,0,1)}
, where
s_{(ij)}
denotes the permutation that switches the
i
’th and
j
’th coordinates.
Note that
\widetilde{s}_{1},\widetilde{s}_{2},\widetilde{s}_{3}
are elements of the affine Weyl group
W^{\text{aff}}
(which corresponds to the Iwahori–Weyl group of
\operatorname{SL}_{3}
).
The admissible set of
\mu^{\prime}=(1,0,0)
is by definition the elements of
\widetilde{W}^{\prime}
that are
\leq
the elements
v^{\mu^{\prime}}=v^{(1,0,0)},v^{s_{(13)}\mu^{\prime}}=v^{(0,0,1)},v^{s_{(12)}\mu^{\prime}}=v^{(0,1,0)}
in the Bruhat order (whose definition is given in
Section

4.1.3
).
Note that
\widetilde{t}=v^{(1,0,0)}s_{(123)}
is an element of
\widetilde{W}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
, the stabilizer of
\mathcal{C}
in
\widetilde{W}^{\prime}
, acting as a rotation centered at the barycenter of
\mathcal{C}
.
We have

\displaystyle v^{\mu^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{s}_{3}\widetilde{s}_{2}\widetilde{t},

\displaystyle v^{s_{(13)}\mu^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{s}_{2}\widetilde{s}_{1}\widetilde{t},

\displaystyle v^{s_{(12)}\mu^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{s}_{1}\widetilde{s}_{3}\widetilde{t}.

From this, we see that the admissible set of
\mu^{\prime}
is precisely

\displaystyle\operatorname{Adm}(\mu^{\prime})=\{v^{\mu^{\prime}},\,\,v^{s_{(13)}\mu^{\prime}},\,\,v^{s_{(12)}\mu^{\prime}},\,\,\widetilde{s}_{1}\widetilde{t},\,\,\widetilde{s}_{2}\widetilde{t},\,\,\widetilde{s}_{3}\widetilde{t},\,\,\widetilde{t}\}.

Since the Bruhat ordering on
\widetilde{W}^{\prime}
is induced by the Bruhat ordering on the affine Weyl group for
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
, the elements of
\operatorname{Adm}(\mu^{\prime})
correspond to alcoves.
This is illustrated in
Fig.

5
on page
5
.
In fact, an efficient way to find the factorization
v^{\mu^{\prime}}=\widetilde{s}_{3}\widetilde{s}_{2}\widetilde{t}
by hand is to
first draw the picture of the alcoves as shown in
Fig.

5
.
By doing a “gallery walk” we can then easily see that
v^{\mu^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}=\widetilde{s}_{3}\widetilde{s}_{2}\mathcal{C}
, and then
\widetilde{t}=(\widetilde{s}_{3}\widetilde{s}_{2})^{-1}v^{\mu^{\prime}}
.

v^{s_{(13)}\mu^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}={\color[rgb]{0.9414,0,0.9414}\widetilde{s}_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9414}\widetilde{s}_{1}}\mathcal{C}

.

of the same colors,

\widetilde{w}
in the affine Weyl group by doing a “gallery walk”.

7.2.
Tamely ramified unitary group

Let
L=\mathbb{Q}_{p}\left((-p)^{1/e}\right)
, where
2|e
and
e|p-1
, in which case
\Gamma=I=\langle\gamma\rangle=\operatorname{Gal}(L/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.
Let
G=U_{3}
be the (quasi-split) tamely ramified unitary group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-points

G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})=\left\{g\in\mathrm{GL}_{3}\left(\mathbb{Q}_{p}\left((-p)^{1/2}\right)\right)|J{{}^{\gamma}g}^{-t}J^{-1}=g\right\},

where
J=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}
and
\gamma
acts via its image in
\operatorname{Gal}\left(\mathbb{Q}_{p}\left((-p)^{1/2}\right)/\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)
.
Let
\widehat{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{3}
be the dual group of
G
, on which
\gamma
acts as the pinned automorphism
g\mapsto Jg^{-t}J^{-1}
.

In this case, the group
G^{*}
over
\mathcal{O}[v^{\pm 1}]
is not
\widehat{G}
, and it is a genuinely non-split group.
For example,

G^{*}(E(\!(v)\!))=\left\{g\in\mathrm{GL}_{3}\left(E(\!(v^{1/2})\!)\right)|J{{}^{\gamma}g}^{-t}J^{-1}=g\right\}.

7.2.1.
Galois type

The action of
\gamma
on
X_{*}(\widehat{T})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, where
\widehat{T}\subset\widehat{G}
is the diagonal torus, is precisely

(43)

\gamma(a,b,c)=(-c,-b,-a).

Therefore we can identify
X_{*}(\widehat{T})^{I}=\{(a,0,-a)\in X_{*}(\widehat{T})|a\in\mathbb{Z}\}\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

By
Proposition

2.33
we have a surjective map
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow H^{1}\left(I,\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O}\llbracket u\rrbracket)\right)

which maps
a\in\mathbb{Z}
to the
1
-cocycle
\tau:I\to\widehat{G}(\mathcal{O})
with

\tau(\gamma)=u^{-(a,0,-a)}(^{\gamma}u^{(a,0,-a)})=u^{-(a,0,-a)+\gamma(a,0,-a)}\omega(\gamma)^{\gamma(a,0,-a)}=\omega(\gamma)^{(a,0,-a)}.

In fact,
Proposition

2.33
implies that this surjective map factors through
\widehat{H}^{0}(I,X_{*}(\widehat{T}))\cong\mathbb{Z}/(e/2)\mathbb{Z}
.

As a concrete example, suppose
e=6
and
a=-\frac{1}{6}
. Then
\tau(\gamma)=\omega(\gamma)^{(-1/6,0,1/6)}
. The corresponding point in the apartment
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(U_{3},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)

is
x=u^{(-1/6,0,1/6)}\cdot o=o+(1/6,0,-1/6)
. This is illustrated in
Fig.

6
.

o
o+(1,0,-1)
o+(\frac{1}{2},0,-\frac{1}{2})
o+(\frac{1}{4},0,-\frac{1}{4})
x

Figure 6.

A snapshot of the apartment
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(U_{3},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\mathrm{GL}_{3},\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!)\right)^{I}
(the solid black line) viewed inside
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\left(\mathrm{GL}_{3},\mathbb{F}(\!(u)\!)\right)
(the faint blue lines), for
e=6
.
A point classifying a Galois type corresponds to one of the black dots, for example
x=o+(1/6,0,-1/6)
.
The three large dots
o
,
o+\left(1/4,0-1/4\right)
and
o+\left(1/2,0,-1/2\right)
are the vertices of
\mathcal{A}\left(U_{3},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)\right)
that can be seen.
There are two full alcoves visible.

7.2.2.
Bruhat–Tits group scheme

Via
X_{*}(\widehat{T})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, we can identify
\Phi^{\vee}(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})\mathbb{Z}\cong\{(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}|a+b+c=0\}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(G^{*})=\pi_{1}(\widehat{G})=X_{*}(\widehat{T})/\Phi^{\vee}(\widehat{G},\widehat{T})\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle(a,b,c)

\displaystyle\mapsto a+b+c.

Since
\gamma(a,b,c)=(-c,-b,-a)
by (
43
), the corresponding action of
\gamma
on
\mathbb{Z}
is multiplication by
-1
.
It follows that

\pi_{1}(G^{*})_{I}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2.

As such,
\mathcal{G}_{x}
does not automatically have connected fibers, since
Proposition

3.7
doesn’t apply directly.
In fact,
\mathcal{G}_{x}
does
not
have connected fibers for any
0
-generic
x
.
This is a consequence of the short exact sequence (
15
) and the fact that the stabilizer in
G^{*}(\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))

of an alcove
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{A}(T^{*},\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!))
must fix every point of
\mathcal{C}
(which we can see by noting that
\mathcal{C}
has two bounding vertices of different specialities, see
[
KP23
, Example 7.11.6, Figure 7.11.1]
).

Remark 7.3
.

If we had considered a slightly different example in which
G^{*}_{\mathbb{F}(\!(v)\!)}
was a
special
unitary group instead, then the fibers of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
would be connected for any
x
.

Appendix A
Invariant pushforward and
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors

In this section,
\Gamma
denotes any finite group.
And
H
denotes a group object with a
\Gamma
-action (where we will consider different categories).
Roughly speaking, a
(\Gamma,H)
is an
H
-torsor with a
\Gamma
-action which is semilinear over the base and makes the action map equivariant.

The notion of
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors goes back to Grothendieck’s Bourbaki talk
[
Gro56a
]
in the context of analytic complex spaces,
where he considers the problem of descending
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors along a quotient map
X\to\Gamma\backslash X
.
If
\Gamma
acts freely on
X
, then
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors over
X
correspond to
H
-torsors on
X
, but this is not true in general.
As observed in
[
BS14
]
, if
X\to Y=\Gamma\backslash X
is a ramified covering of Riemann surfaces, then a
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor over
X

corresponds to a
\mathcal{H}
-torsor over
Y
where
\mathcal{H}
is a Bruhat–Tits group over
Y
which is equal to
H
over the part where
X\to Y
is unramified,
and admits parahoric structure over the branching points of
Y
. It is important to note that which parahoric structure on
\mathcal{H}
we take in this correspondence
is dictated by local invariants of
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors called the type.

These ideas were developed further in
[
DH23
]
and
[
PR23
]
for tamely ramified covers of curves over algebraically
closed fields (of characteristic zero in the former case).
In particular,
[
PR23
]
shows that if
X
is a curve over an algebraically closed field and
\mathcal{G}
is a Bruhat–Tits group scheme over
X
,
then (under suitable additional assumptions) there exists a tamely ramified covering
X^{\prime}\to X
such that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}\times_{X}X^{\prime}
is a connected reductive
group scheme.

In this section, we will explain a generalization of
[
BS14
, Theorem 4.1.6]
which holds in any topos (and in particular in the big étale or fppf topos over a scheme),
which we specialize to the algebro geometric setting in
Section

A.3
.
We will apply this in
Section

2.6
to describe
(\Gamma,\widehat{G})
-torsors
over
\mathfrak{S}_{L,R}
in terms of torsors for a certain group
\mathcal{G}
over
\mathfrak{S}_{R}
,
and in
Section

3.1
we will identify the group scheme
\mathcal{G}
with a Bruhat–Tits group scheme in the sense of Pappas–Zhu
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Remark A.1
.

There is an error in the statment of
[
BS14
, Theorem 4.1.6]
, since it does not account for the local type, as pointed out in Damiolini’s PhD thesis
[
Dam17
, Appendix A]
.
21
21

21

We thank Vikraman Balaji for pointing us towards Damiolini’s thesis.

However, note that this does not affect other results of
[
BS14
]
, which implicitly use the corrected version of the theorem.

A.1.
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors in general

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a topos and
\Gamma
be a finite group.
If
X\in\mathcal{C}
then a
\Gamma
-action on
X
is the same as a homomorphism
\Gamma\to\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal{C}}(X)
.
Assume that
\widetilde{A}\in\mathcal{C}
is an object with
\Gamma
-action and that the action of
\theta\in\Gamma
on
\widetilde{A}
is given by
a_{\theta}
.

A
\Gamma
-equivariant object over
\widetilde{A}
is an object
X\to\widetilde{A}
which is equipped with a
\Gamma
-action making this morphism
\Gamma
-equivariant.
In this situation, we will also say the the
\Gamma
-action on
X
is
semilinear
over
\widetilde{A}
.
The data of a
\Gamma
-equivariant structure on
X
is equivalent to any of the following:

(1)

A collection of isomorphisms
X\cong(a_{\theta})^{*}X
over
\widetilde{A}
satisfying a cocycle condition (by the adjunction
(a_{\theta})_{!}\dashv(a_{\theta})^{*}
).

(2)

A collection of isomorphisms
(a_{\theta})_{*}X\cong X
over
\widetilde{A}
satisfying a cocycle condition (by the adjunction
(a_{\theta})^{*}\dashv(a_{\theta})_{*}
applied to the inverses of the isomorphisms from (1)).

(3)

A collection of isomorphisms
(a_{\theta}^{-1})^{*}X\cong X
over
\widetilde{A}
satisfying a cocycle condition (since
(a_{\theta})_{*}\cong(a_{\theta}^{-1})^{*}
).

In algebraic geometry it is customary to use (3), taking into account the fact that if
\theta\in\Gamma
acts on a ring
A
by
f_{\theta}:A\to A
,
then the corresponding left action on
\operatorname{Spec}A
is given by
a_{\theta}=\operatorname{Spec}f_{\theta}^{-1}:\operatorname{Spec}A\to\operatorname{Spec}A
.

Note that if
Y\to\widetilde{A}
is another
\Gamma
-equivariant object over
\widetilde{A}
, then
X\times_{\widetilde{A}}Y\to Y
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant object over
Y
.

Let
H\to\widetilde{A}
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant group, that is, a
\Gamma
-equivariant object over
\widetilde{A}
which is also a group object over
\widetilde{A}
, and for which the multiplication, identity section, and inversion maps are
\Gamma
-equivariant.
A
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor over
\widetilde{A}
is an
H
-torsor
P\to\widetilde{A}
which is equipped with a
\Gamma
-action which is semilinear over
\widetilde{A}
and for which the action map
P\times_{\widetilde{A}}H\to P
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.
A morphism of
(\Gamma,H)
torsors
P\to Q
is a morphism of objects over
\widetilde{A}
which is both
\Gamma
-equivariant and a morphism of
H
-torsors (i. e. compatiable with the
H
-action).
It will also be important to consider
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors over
Y
, where
Y\to\widetilde{A}
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant object, and they are defined in the obvious way.

Remark A.2
.

By default, we consider torsors as being
right
torsors, with the group acting on the right.
We will also consider left torsors as well as bitorsors, but will specify this whenever we do.

A.1.1.
Group cohomology and
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors

(\Gamma,H)
-torsors whose underlying
H
-torsor is trivial are classified by group cohomology
H^{1}\left(\Gamma,H(\widetilde{A})\right)
, as we now explain.
If
H(\widetilde{A})
denotes the group of sections
\widetilde{A}\to H
over
\widetilde{A}
, then the group
\Gamma
acts on
H(\widetilde{A})
by “conjugation”.
That is, given such a section
h\in H(\widetilde{A})
and
\theta\in\Gamma
, we define
{{}^{\theta}h}\in H(\widetilde{A})
as the map fitting into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{\theta}h}

where both maps labeled
\theta
are given by the action map describing
\theta
(called
a_{\theta}
above).
Given a
1
-cocycle
\tau:\Gamma\to H(\widetilde{A})
, we define a
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor
{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}
as the
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor whose underlying
H
-torsor is the trivial
H
-torsor
\mathcal{E}^{0}
, and on which
\theta\in\Gamma
acts as the composition

\mathcal{E}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{E}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{\tau(\theta)}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{E}^{0},

where the map labeled
\theta
gives the action of
\theta
on
H=\mathcal{E}^{0}
, and
L_{\tau(\theta)}
denotes left translation by
\tau(\theta)
.

Lemma A.3
.

The map

\displaystyle Z^{1}\left(\Gamma,H(\widetilde{A})\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\{(\Gamma,H)\text{-torsors over }\widetilde{A}\text{ whose underlying }H\text{-torsor is }\mathcal{E}^{0}\}

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle\mapsto{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}

is an equivalence of groupoids. Consequently,
H^{1}\left(\Gamma,H(\widetilde{A})\right)
is in bijection with isomorphism classes of

(\Gamma,H)
-torsors on
\widetilde{A}
whose underlying
H
-torsor is trivializable.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward. The key point is that associativity of the
\Gamma
-action on
{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}
corresponds to the

1
-cocycle condition for
\tau
.
∎

A.1.2.
Invariant pushforward and
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor of trivial type

Let
p:\widetilde{A}\to A
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant morphism where
\Gamma
acts trivially on
A
.
Given a
\Gamma
-equivariant object
X\to\widetilde{A}
we define the
invariant pushforward

p_{*}^{\Gamma}(X)
as follows.
First,
p_{*}X\to A
is defined in the usual way.
For any object
V\to A
we have

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}\left(V,p_{*}(X)\right)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\widetilde{A}}\left(V\times_{A}\widetilde{A},X\right),

and
\Gamma
acts on the right hand side by conjugation (using the action on
V\times_{A}\widetilde{A}
where
\theta\in\Gamma
acts as
1\times a_{\theta}
).
By Yoneda’s lemma, this gives rise to a
\Gamma
-action on
p_{*}X
over
A
, and we define
p_{*}^{\Gamma}X=(p_{*}X)^{\Gamma}
as the obvious limit.

Note that
p_{*}^{\Gamma}H
is a group since
p_{*}^{\Gamma}
preserves limits and in particular products.
Similarly, if
P
is a
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor over
\widetilde{A}
, then the action map
P\times_{\widetilde{A}}H\to P
gives rise to an action map

p_{*}^{\Gamma}P\times_{A}p_{*}^{\Gamma}H\to p_{*}^{\Gamma}P
.
Therefore,
p_{*}^{\Gamma}P
is a
p_{*}^{\Gamma}H
-pseudotorsor.
But it is not always a
p_{*}^{\Gamma}H
-torsor, because the map
p_{*}^{\Gamma}P\to A
is not always surjective.
This failure is accounted for by the type of the torsor.

Definition A.4
.

Two
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors
P,Q
over
\widetilde{A}
are said to have the same
type
if there exists a covering
\{U_{i}\to A\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}

such that for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
the base change of
P
and
Q
to
U_{i}\times_{A}\widetilde{A}
are isomorphic
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors.
A
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor
P\to\widetilde{A}
is said to have
trivial type
if there
P
has the same type as the trivial
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor.
If
[\tau]\in H^{1}\left(\Gamma,H(\widetilde{A})\right)
, then a
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor
P\to\widetilde{A}
is said to have type
[\tau]
(or just
\tau
) if
it has the same type as the
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor corresponding to
[\tau]
via
Lemma

A.3
.

Proposition A.5
.

We have an equivalence of groupoids

\displaystyle p_{*}^{\Gamma}:\{(\Gamma,H)\text{-torsors over }\widetilde{A}\text{ of trivial type}\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\{p_{*}^{\Gamma}H\text{-torsors over }A\}

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\mapsto p_{*}^{\Gamma}P.

Proof.

It follows from unraveling the definitions that if
P\to\widetilde{A}
is a
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor of trivial type, then
p_{*}^{\Gamma}P\to A
is an epimorphism.
Hence the functor is well-defined.

Define an inverse
F
to
p_{*}^{\Gamma}
as follows. Given a
p_{*}^{\Gamma}H
-torsor
P\to A
,
we obtain a
p^{*}p_{*}^{\Gamma}H
-torsor
p^{*}P
over
\widetilde{A}
.
Via the natural map
p^{*}p_{*}^{\Gamma}H\to H
we can let
F(P):=p^{*}P\times^{p^{*}p_{*}^{\Gamma}H}H
be the associated
H
-torsor.
22
22

22

If
P^{\prime}
is a right
H^{\prime}
-torsor,
Q^{\prime}
is a
(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})
-bitorsor, then the associated bundle
P^{\prime}\times^{H^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}:=P^{\prime}\times_{\widetilde{A}}Q^{\prime}/(ph,q)\sim(p,hq)

is a right
H^{\prime\prime}
-torsor. More precisely,
P^{\prime}\times^{H^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}
is defined as the reflexive coequalizer
P^{\prime}\times H^{\prime}\times Q^{\prime}\rightrightarrows P^{\prime}\times Q^{\prime}\to P^{\prime}\times^{H^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}
.

Then one checks as in
[
Dam17
, A.0.3]
that there are natural maps
Fp_{*}^{\Gamma}(P)\to P
and
P\to p_{*}^{\Gamma}F(P)

(in fact
F
is left adjoint to
p_{*}^{\Gamma}
on the level of pseudotorsors).
Since all morphisms of torsors (and consequently,
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors) are isomorphisms, it follows that
F
is inverse to
p_{*}^{\Gamma}
.
∎

Remark A.6
.

One can alternatively see that above equivalence as follows.
Let
BH
denote the classifying stack of
H
-torsors over
\widetilde{A}
.
Then
p_{*}^{\Gamma}BH
is a stack over
A
which for an object
V\to A
classifies
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors over
V\times_{A}\widetilde{A}
.
The trivial
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor viewed as an object in this stack has automorphisms
p_{*}^{\Gamma}H
.
If
B(p_{*}^{\Gamma}H)
denotes the classifying stack for
p_{*}^{\Gamma}H
-torsors,
we can therefore view
B(p_{*}^{\Gamma}H)\subset p_{*}^{\Gamma}B(\Gamma,H)
as the gerbe (i. e. 1-truncated substack) containing the trivial
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor,
and one can check that being contained in this gerbe corresponds to having trivial type.
By taking
A
-points, one recovers
Proposition

A.5
.

A.2.
Twisting

We remain in the situation of
Section

A.1
, so
H\to\widetilde{A}
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant group.
Let
\mathcal{P}\to\widetilde{A}
be a
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor.
Then we can define a
\Gamma
-equivariant group

{{}_{\mathcal{P}}H}:=\operatorname{Aut}_{H}(\mathcal{P})

over
\widetilde{A}
, where the
\Gamma
-action is by conjugation.

Remark A.7
.

Let
\tau:\Gamma\to H(\widetilde{A})
be a 1-cocycle, and let
\mathcal{P}=_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}
be the
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor whose underlying
H
-torsor is trivial,
and with the
\Gamma
-action twisted by
\tau
(as in
Section

A.1.1
). Then we define
{{}_{\tau}}H:={{}_{\mathcal{P}}H}
as a
\Gamma
-group.
This is compatible with
[
Ser97
, Section I.5.3]
, in that we have the following formulas on
\widetilde{A}
-points:

\displaystyle\theta\cdot p

\displaystyle=\tau(\theta)(^{\theta}p),

\displaystyle p\in{{}_{\tau}\mathcal{E}^{0}}(\widetilde{A}),

\displaystyle\theta\cdot h

\displaystyle=\tau(\theta)(^{\theta}h)\tau(\theta)^{-1},

\displaystyle h\in{{}_{\tau}H}(\widetilde{A}),

where
\theta\cdot p
and
\theta\cdot h
denotes the “new”
\tau
-twisted action and
{{}^{\theta}p}
and
{{}^{\theta}h}
denotes the “old” action.

Note that
{{}_{\mathcal{P}}H}
acts on
\mathcal{P}
on the left, and this makes
\mathcal{P}
into a
(_{\mathcal{P}}H,H)
-bitorsor (since locally,
\mathcal{P}\simeq\mathcal{E}^{0}
and
{{}_{\mathcal{P}}H}\simeq H
).
We have an equivalence of groupoids

(44)

\displaystyle\left\{(\Gamma,{{}_{\mathcal{P}}H})\text{-torsors over }\widetilde{A}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left\{(\Gamma,H)\text{-torsors over }\widetilde{A}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{Q}\times^{{}_{\mathcal{P}}H}\mathcal{P},

where
\mathcal{Q}\times^{{}_{\mathcal{P}}H}\mathcal{P}
is the associated bundle construction.
The inverse is given by
\mathcal{R}\mapsto\mathcal{R}\times^{H}\mathcal{P}^{-1}
, where
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
is the
(H,_{\mathcal{P}}H)
-bitorsor
whose underlying object is
\mathcal{P}
on which
H
and
{{}_{\mathcal{P}}H}
act through the inverse on the opposite side.

Remark A.8
.

One can view (
44
) as a consequence of the fact that the classifying stack of torsors is a “delooping” of the group, as explained for example in
[
NSS15
]
.

Observe that a
(\Gamma,_{\mathcal{P}}H)
-torsor
\mathcal{Q}
has trivial type if and only if the associated
(\Gamma,H)
-torsor
{\mathcal{Q}\times^{{}_{\mathcal{P}}H}\mathcal{P}}
has the same type as
\mathcal{P}
.
Combining
Proposition

A.5
and (
44
), we obtain the following.

Corollary A.9
.

We have equivalences of groupoids

\displaystyle\{(\Gamma,H)\text{-torsors over }\widetilde{A}\text{ of type }\mathcal{P}\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\{(\Gamma,_{\mathcal{P}}H)\text{-torsors over }\widetilde{A}\text{ of trivial type}\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle\{p_{*}^{\Gamma}(_{\mathcal{P}}H)\text{-torsors over }A\}.

A.3.
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors in algebraic geometry

We now specialize the above results to the setting of algebraic geometry.
This will recover some results of
[
BS14
]
and
[
Dam17
]
, at least when over a base field.

By embedding schemes into the big étale or fppf topos via Yoneda’s lemma, we define
\Gamma
-equivariant schemes etc. as the corresponding notion for sheaves.
Because our group schemes will be smooth affine, torsors for such a group are representable by schemes, hence sheaves in the fpqc topology, yet étale locally trivializable.
Therefore it makes little difference which big topology we choose to work with.

Let
A
be a scheme,
\widetilde{A}
a scheme with
\Gamma
-action and
p:\widetilde{A}\to A
a morphism of schemes which is
\Gamma
-equivariant for the
trivial
\Gamma
-action on
A
.

Proposition A.10
.

Assume that
A
is locally noetherian and
p:\widetilde{A}\to A
is finite flat.
Let
X\to\widetilde{A}
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant and quasi-projective morphism of schemes.

(1)

p_{*}^{\Gamma}X
is representable by a scheme over
A
.

(2)

If
X\to\widetilde{A}
is affine, then so is
p_{*}^{\Gamma}X\to A
.

(3)

If
X\to\widetilde{A}
is smooth and
\#\Gamma
is invertible over
A
, then
p_{*}^{\Gamma}X\to A
is smooth.

Proof.

Since all statements are Zariski local on
A
and
\pi
is a finite (hence affine) morphism, we may assume that
A
and
\widetilde{A}
are the spectra of noetherian rings.
Representability of the Weil restriction
p_{*}X
is due to Grothendieck
[
Gro56
, Exposé 221, Section 4.c]
; see also
[
BLR90
, Section 7.6]
.
As shown in
[
Edi92
, Proposition 3.1]
or
[
CGP15
, Proposition A.8.10]
,
p_{*}^{\Gamma}X=(p_{*}X)^{\Gamma}
is then represented by a closed subscheme of
p_{*}X
.
If
X\to\widetilde{A}
is affine/smooth/étale/an open immersion, then so is
p_{*}X\to A
by
[
CGP15
, Proposition A.5.2]
, implying (2) since closed subschemes of affine schemes are affine.
The smoothness part of (3) is shown in
[
Edi92
, Proposition 3.4]
.
∎

Remark A.11
.

In
[
Edi92
, Proposition 3.4]
only the weaker assumption that
\#\Gamma
is invertible over
p_{*}X
is needed, so our assumption in (3) is stronger than necessary.
From the point of view of
[
CGP15
, Proposition A.8.10]
, the key is that the constant group determined by
\Gamma
is linearly reductive when
\#\Gamma
is invertible.

Remark A.12
.

Instead of requiring that
A
is locally noetherian and
p:\widetilde{A}\to A
is finite flat, we could have simply required that
p:\widetilde{A}\to A
is finite locally free.
For a comparison of these conditions, see
[
Sta
,
Lemma 02KB
]
.
The latter condition is the one which is used in
[
BLR90
, Section 7.6]
.

Let
H\to\widetilde{A}
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant smooth affine group scheme.
If
A
is locally noetherian and
p:\widetilde{A}\to A
is finite flat, then in light of
Proposition

A.10
the invariant pushforward

p_{*}^{\Gamma}
is well-behaved on scheme-theoretic
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors.
In particular,
Proposition

A.5
and
Corollary

A.9
apply to this situation.
The assumptions on
H
ensure that it doesn’t matter whether we use the étale, fppf, or fpqc topology in the definition of type, and it is even a pointwise local invariant, as the following result shows.

Proposition A.13
.

Assume that
A
is locally noetherian and
p:\widetilde{A}\to A
is finite flat, and let
H\to\widetilde{A}
be a
\Gamma
-equivariant smooth affine group scheme.
Let
P
and
Q
be
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors on
\widetilde{A}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists an fpqc covering
\{U_{i}\to\widetilde{A}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
and an isomorphism of
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors
P_{U_{i}\times_{A}\widetilde{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{U_{i}\times_{A}\widetilde{A}}
for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
,

(2)

There exists an étale covering
\{U_{i}\to\widetilde{A}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
and an isomorphism of
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors
P_{U_{i}\times_{A}\widetilde{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{U_{i}\times_{A}\widetilde{A}}
for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
,

(3)

For every geometric point
\overline{x}:\operatorname{Spec}\overline{\kappa}\to A
there exists an isomorphism of
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors
P_{\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}P_{\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}}
.

(4)

For every geometric point
\overline{x}:\operatorname{Spec}\overline{\kappa}\to A
and every point
y\in\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}
there exists an isomorphism of
(\Gamma_{y},H)
-torsors
P_{y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{y}
, where
\Gamma_{y}\leq\Gamma
is the stabilizer of
y
.

Proof.

Consider the sheaf
\underline{\operatorname{Iso}}_{H}\left(P,Q\right)
of isomorphisms of
H
-torsors
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q
.
By fpqc descent, this is representable by a smooth affine scheme over
\widetilde{A}
and it has a
\Gamma
-conjugation action.
Hence
p_{*}^{\Gamma}\underline{\operatorname{Iso}}_{H}\left(P,Q\right)
is a smooth affine scheme over
A
by
Proposition

A.10
.
Note that if
V\to A
is a scheme, then

p_{*}^{\Gamma}\underline{\operatorname{Iso}}_{H}\left(P,Q\right)(V)=\operatorname{Iso}_{H}\left(P_{V\times_{A}\widetilde{A}},Q_{V\times_{A}\widetilde{A}}\right)^{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Iso}_{(\Gamma,H)}\left(P_{V\times_{A}\widetilde{A}},Q_{V\times_{A}\widetilde{A}}\right)

is precisely the collection of isomorphisms of
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors from
P
to
Q
after base change to
V\times_{A}\widetilde{A}
.
The equivalence of (1), (2), and (3) now follows from the well-known fact that a smooth morphism of schemes admits sections fpqc locally if and only if it admits sections étale locally if and only if it is surjective.

It is obvious that (3) implies (4) so it remains to see that (4) implies (3). This may be thought of as a geometric version of Shapiro’s Lemma.
By partitioning
\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}
into
\Gamma
-orbits we may assume that
\Gamma
acts transitively on
\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}
.
Note that

\operatorname{Iso}_{H}\left(P_{\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}},Q_{\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}}\right)=\prod_{y\in\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}}\operatorname{Iso}_{H}\left(P_{y},Q_{y}\right).

If
\theta\in\Gamma
, then multiplication by
\theta
yields for any
y\in\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}
a bijection
\operatorname{Iso}_{H}\left(P_{y},Q_{y}\right)^{\Gamma_{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Iso}_{H}\left(P_{\theta y},Q_{\theta y}\right)^{\Gamma_{\theta y}}
.
From this observation it’s not hard to see that projection onto some factor
y
gives a bijection
\operatorname{Iso}_{H}\left(P_{\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}},Q_{\overline{\kappa}\times_{\overline{x},A}\widetilde{A}}\right)^{\Gamma}\cong\operatorname{Iso}_{H}\left(P_{y},Q_{y}\right)^{\Gamma_{y}}
.
∎

In the context of
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors on curves, as in
[
BS14
]
,
[
DH23
]
, or
[
PR23
]
,
it is condition (4) above that is used to define the local type.
Since every
H
-torsor over an algebraically closed field is trivializable, it follows from
Lemma

A.3
that the types in this case are even determined by cohomology
classes of
H^{1}\left(\Gamma_{y},H(\kappa(y))\right)
, and consequently all
(\Gamma,H)
-torsors are classified by such data.
This classification is used extensively in the cited works.

Appendix B
Generalities on positive loop groups

In this section we state some general results about positive loop groups that are difficult to track down in the literature.

Let
A
be a base ring, and let
X
be an fpqc sheaf over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
.
We define a presheaf on
A
-algebras

L^{+}X(R)=X\left(R\llbracket t\rrbracket\right),

the positive loop “group” for
X
. We also define the
n
-jet

L^{n}X(R)=X\left(R\llbracket t\rrbracket/t^{n}\right)

for any integer
n\geq 1
.
Note that
L^{n}
the pushforward from fpqc sheaves over
A[t]/t^{n}
to fpqc sheaves over
A
.

Proposition B.1
.

Assume that
A
is noetherian and let
X
be an affine scheme over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
.

(1)

For each
n
, the fpqc sheaf
L^{n}X
is represented by an affine scheme over
A
, say
B_{n}
. Then
L^{+}X
is represented by
B:=\varinjlim B_{n}
, corresponding to the isomorphism of fpqc sheaves
L^{+}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\varprojlim L^{n}X
.

(2)

If
X
is formally smooth, then for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
the inclusions
B_{n}\hookrightarrow B_{n+1}
and
B_{n}\hookrightarrow B
admit retractions.
Therefore we can identify each
B_{n}
as a subring of
B
, and
B=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}B_{n}
.

(3)

If
X
is smooth, then so is
L^{n}X
. In addition,
L^{+}X
is formally smooth, flat, and reduced.

(4)

If
Z\hookrightarrow X
is a closed immersion, then so is
L^{n}Z\hookrightarrow L^{n}X
and
L^{+}Z\hookrightarrow L^{+}X
.

(5)

If
U\hookrightarrow X
is an open immersion, then so is
L^{n}U\hookrightarrow L^{n}X
and
L^{+}U\hookrightarrow L^{+}X
.

Proof.

(1)

Note that
L^{n}X=\operatorname{Res}^{A[t]/t^{n}}_{A}X
. Since
A[t]/t^{n}
is finite flat over
A
, the first statement follows from
[
CGP15
, Proposition A.5.2]
.
Since
X=\operatorname{Spec}\Lambda
is affine, we have

L^{+}X(R)=\operatorname{Hom}_{A\llbracket t\rrbracket}\left(\Lambda,R\llbracket t\rrbracket\right)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{A\llbracket t\rrbracket}\left(\Lambda,\varprojlim R[t]/t^{n}\right)\cong\varprojlim\operatorname{Hom}_{A\llbracket t\rrbracket}\left(\Lambda,R[t]/t^{n}\right)\cong\varprojlim L^{n}X(R).

On the other hand, if
B=\varinjlim B_{n}
, then
\operatorname{Hom}_{A}\left(B,R\right)\cong\varprojlim\operatorname{Hom}_{A}\left(B_{n},R\right)=\varprojlim L^{n}X(R)
, so we see that
L^{+}X

is representable by
B
.

(2)

Assuming
X
is formally smooth, the map
L^{n+1}X(R)=X(R[t]/t^{n+1})\to X(R[t]/t^{n})=L^{n}X(R)
is surjective for any
A
-algebra
R

because
R[t]/t^{n+1}\to R[t]/t^{n}
is a square zero extension.
Taking
R=B_{n}
we see that the identity map
B_{n}=B_{n}
lifts to a retraction
B_{n+1}\to B_{n}
of the map
B_{n}\to B_{n+1}
.

(3)

Smoothness of
L^{n}X
follows from
[
CGP15
, Proposition A.5.2]
.
Formal smoothness of
L^{+}X
is immediate from
X
being smooth (and in
particular formally smooth). Being flat follows from

B=\varinjlim_{n}B_{n}
being a colimit of smooth (and in particular
flat)
A
-algebras, the fact that
M\otimes_{A}
preserves all
colimits for any
A
-module
M
, and the fact that taking filtered
colimits of
A
-modules is exact.

Note that each
L^{n}X
is reduced by
[
Sta
,
Lemma 034E
]
.
If
f\in B
is a (nilpotent) element, then
f\in B_{n}
for some
n
, so reducedness of
B
follows from reducedness of each
B_{n}
.

(4)

Let
f:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion of affine
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
-schemes.
For each
n
the map
L^{n}Z\hookrightarrow L^{n}Z
is a closed immersion by
[
CGP15
, Proposition A.5.5]
.
So if
C_{n}
and
C
are to
Z
what
B_{n}
and
B
are to
X
, then the corresponding map
B_{n}\to C_{n}
identifies
C_{n}=B_{n}/I_{n}
,
where
I_{n}\subset B_{n}
is an ideal. If we let
I=\varinjlim I_{n}
, then by exactness of filtered colimits it follows that the map
B\to C
identifies
C=B/I
,
meaning that
L^{+}Z\hookrightarrow L^{+}X
is a closed immersion.

(5)

The statement about
L^{n}
follows from
[
CGP15
, Proposition A.5.2]
, so it remains to prove the statement about
L^{+}
.

As an intermediate step, let us first prove that
L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{m}\hookrightarrow L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{a}
is open.
If we write
\mathbb{G}_{a}=\operatorname{Spec}A\llbracket t\rrbracket[x]

then we can identify
L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{a}=\operatorname{Spec}A[a_{0},a_{1},\dots]
where
a_{i}
corresponds to the
i
’th coefficient of a power series.
Similarly, if we write
\mathbb{G}_{m}=\operatorname{Spec}A\llbracket t\rrbracket[x]
, then we can identify
L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{m}=\operatorname{Spec}A[a_{0}^{\pm 1},a_{1},a_{2},\dots]
,
using the fact that a power series
a_{0}+a_{1}t+\cdots\in R\llbracket t\rrbracket
is invertible if and only if
a_{0}
is invertible.
So
L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{m}=D(a_{0})\subset L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{a}
is the affine open on which
a_{0}
doesn’t vanish.

We now consider the general case, so let
U\subset X
be an open immersion of
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
-schemes.
Let
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be a collection of functions on
X
such that

U=\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}D(f_{i})
.
Viewing each
f_{i}
as a function
X\to\mathbb{G}_{a}
, there are induced maps
L^{+}f_{i}:L^{+}X\to L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{a}
, and we can identify

\displaystyle L^{+}U(R)

\displaystyle=U(R\llbracket t\rrbracket)=\{x\in X(R\llbracket t\rrbracket):f_{i}(x)\in R\llbracket t\rrbracket^{\times}\text{ for some }i\in\mathcal{I}\}

\displaystyle=\{x\in L^{+}X(R):L^{+}f_{i}(x)\in R\llbracket t\rrbracket^{\times}\text{ for some }i\in\mathcal{I}\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(L^{+}f_{i})^{-1}\left(L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{m}\right)(R).

Since
L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{m}\subset L^{+}\mathbb{G}_{a}
is open, it follows that
L^{+}U\subset L^{+}X
is open.

∎

Remark B.2
.

In the case that
A
is a field, and
X
is of finite type over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
,
some parts of the above result are stated without proof in
[
Zhu16
, Proposition 1.3.2]

and proved in
[
PR08
, Section 1.a]
.
Some general results on various loop groups can also be found in
[
Ces24
]
.

B.1.
Congruence loop groups

Let
H
be a smooth affine group scheme over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
.
Define

L^{+n}H
as the kernel in the exact sequence of fpqc sheaves

1\to L^{+n}H\to L^{+}H\to L^{n}H\to 1.

Note that right exactness of this sequence is a consequence of formal smoothness of
H
.

Lemma B.3
.

Let

H_{n}(R):=\ker\left(H(R)\to H\left(R/t^{n}R\right)\right)

for any
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
-algebra
R
. Then
H_{n}
is a smooth
affine group scheme over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
, and
L^{+n}H=L^{+}H_{n}
.

Proof.

It is obvious that
L^{+n}H=L^{+}H_{n}
.
By
[
MRR23
, Lemma 3.7]
we can identify
H_{n}
with the dilation of
H
in
the identity section over
j:\operatorname{Spec}A[t]/t^{n}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A\llbracket t\rrbracket

along this closed subscheme. The result is then immediate from
[
MRR23
, Theorem 3.2]
.
∎

B.2.
The
t
-adic metric

Let
X
be a smooth affine scheme over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
.
By
Proposition

B.1
we can write

L^{+}X(R)=\varprojlim L^{n}X(R)

for any
A
-algebra
R
.
A point
x\in L^{+}X(R)
can therefore be identified with a compatible sequence of points
(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)
, where
x_{n}\in L^{n}X(R)
.
We can equip
L^{+}X(R)
with a complete ultrametric
d
by setting

d(x,y)=2^{-\inf\{n|x_{n}\neq y_{n}\}}.

We call
d
the
t
-adic metric
on
L^{+}X(R)
.

It is clear that for any map
R\to S
of
A
-algebras, the induced map
L^{+}X(R)\to L^{+}X(S)
is continuous.
If
f:X\to Y
is a morphism of smooth affine group schemes over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
, then so is
L^{+}f:L^{+}X(R)\to L^{+}Y(R)
for any
R
.

Suppose now that
X=H
is a smooth affine group scheme over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
.
Then we can identify

L^{+n}H(R)=\{g\in L^{+}H(R)|d(1,g)<2^{-n}\}.

In other words, the principal congruence subgroups
\{L^{+n}H(R)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}
form a neighborhood basis of the identity.

B.3.
Questions about reducedness

We take care of some reducedness questions that are needed in the proof of
Lemma

5.6
.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
X
be a smooth affine scheme over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
,
and let
Y
be a reduced scheme over
A
.
The scheme
Y\times_{A}L^{+}X
is reduced.

Proof.

According to
Proposition

B.1
, we can write
L^{n}X=\operatorname{Spec}B_{n}
and
L^{+}X=\operatorname{Spec}B
, where
B_{n}\subset B_{n+1}\subset\cdots\subset\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}B_{n}=B
.
Since the claim is Zariski local we may assume that
Y=\operatorname{Spec}C
.
We have

C\otimes_{A}B\cong\varinjlim\left(C\otimes_{A}B_{i}\right)

and each

C\otimes_{A}B_{i}\hookrightarrow C\otimes_{A}B

is injective since
B_{i}\hookrightarrow B
has a retraction.
Since any (nilpotent) element

f\in C\otimes_{A}B
is contained in some

C\otimes_{A}B_{i}
, the result follows from the fact that
C\otimes_{A}B_{i}
is reduced by

Proposition

B.1
(3) and
[
Sta
,
Lemma 034E
]
.
∎

Lemma B.5
.

Let
H
be a smooth affine group scheme over
A\llbracket t\rrbracket
,
amd let
Y
be a scheme over
A
.
Let
Z\subset Y
be a subscheme, and let
\overline{Z}\subset Y
be the reduced closure of
Z
in
Y
.
Then
\overline{Z}\times_{A}L^{+}H
is the reduced closure of
Z\times_{A}L^{+}H
in
Y\times_{A}L^{+}H
.

Proof.

Let
V\subset Y\times_{A}L^{+}H
denote the reduced closure of
Z\times_{A}L^{+}H
.
The inclusion
V\hookrightarrow Y\times_{A}L^{+}H
factors through
\overline{Z}\times_{A}L^{+}H
by
Lemma

B.4
,
and we have to show that
V\hookrightarrow\overline{Z}\times_{A}L^{+}H
is an isomorphism.
By continuity of the right
L^{+}H
-action via translations,
V
is stable under this action, and therefore descends to
a scheme
V^{\prime}=\left[V/L^{+}H\right]
by fpqc descent
[
Sta
,
Section 0244
]
.
We have
Z\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\overline{Z}\hookrightarrow Y
, and since
V^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y
is a closed immersion
[
Sta
,
Lemma 0420
]
,
it follows that
V^{\prime}=\overline{Z}
. So
V=V^{\prime}\times_{A}L^{+}H=\overline{Z}\times_{A}L^{+}H
.
∎
